ii 
 
PREFACE 
I, the Chairman of the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on 
Health and Family Welfare, having been authorized by the Committee to present the Report 
on its behalf, hereby present this 163rd Report of the Committee on the Demands for Grants 
(Demand No. 46) for the year 2025-26 of the Department of Health and Family Welfare, 
Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. 
 
2.  The Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committees are entrusted with the 
constitutional role of ensuring accountability of executive to the Parliament.  In this regard, 
Rule 270 (a) of Rules & Procedure and Conduct & Business in the Council of States (Rajya 
Sabha) empowered the Department-related Standing Committees to consider the Demands for 
Grants of the related Departments/Ministries with the objective of ensuring financial 
accountability of the executive and report thereon. In exercise of its constitutional mandate, 
the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Health & Family Welfare 
scrutinised the Demands for Grants for the year 2025-26 (Demand No. 46) of the Department 
of Health and Family Welfare, Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. The Committee at its 
sitting held for examination of Demands for Grants (2025-26) of the Department of Health 
and Family Welfare on 14th February, 2025 heard the Secretary and other Officers of the 
Department. 
 
3.  The Committee, in its meeting held on 11th March, 2025, considered the Draft Report 
and adopted the same.  
 
4.  The Committee while making its observations/recommendations has mainly relied 
upon the following documents:– 
i. Detailed Demands for Grants of the Department of Health and Family Welfare for 
the year 2025-26; 
ii. Annual Report of the Department for the year 2024-25; 
iii. Detailed Explanatory Note on Demands for Grants of the Department of Health 
and Family Welfare for the year 2025-26; 
iv. Written replies furnished by the Department to the Questionnaires sent to them by 
the Secretariat;  
v. Presentation made by the Secretary (Ministry of Health and Family Welfare) and 
other concerned officers; 
vi. Economic Survey 2024-25; 
vii. National Health Accounts 2021-22;  
viii. Global Multidimensional Poverty Index 2024; 
ix. Budget Speech of the Finance Minister; and 
x. WHO's World Health Statistics 2022 Report etc. 
5.  For facility of reference and convenience, observations and recommendations of the 
Committee have been printed in bold letters in the body of the Report.

1 
 
CHAPTER I 
INTRODUCTION 
 
1.1.1  Health is central to well-being, influenced by cultural, socio-economic, and 
biomedical factors. India's rich public health tradition dates back to the Indus Valley 
civilization's concept of “Arogya” (holistic well-being). The Constitution of the World Health 
Organization (WHO) defined health “as a state of complete physical, mental and social well-
being, not merely the absence of disease and infirmity”. Though the notion of complete 
absence of disease is unimaginable, nevertheless in 2015, a total of 195 nations committed to 
the UN’s Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) including universal health coverage, 
financial risk protection, access to quality essential health-care services and access to safe, 
effective, quality and affordable essential medicines and vaccines for all (target 3.8 of Goal 
3).  
1.1.2  With the progression of new millennium, the concept of health has evolved 
from being an entity which was to be achieved with the advancement of healthcare 
technology, along with the Universal Coverage of Health, to be an achievement which is 
possible only when the human beings are in attune with their physical and mental 
environment. This new concept of “One Health” is an approach calling for the collaborative 
efforts of multiple disciplines integrating human, animal, and environmental health. 
1.1.3  India is undergoing a rapid health transition, encompassing demographic, 
epidemiological, nutritional, and socio-cultural changes, marking a new era in global health. 
The access to affordable and quality healthcare services is crucial for optimizing the 
productivity of human resources in National economic growth and development. The National 
Health Policy-2017 recognized the pivotal importance of UN’s SDGs and envisaged the 
attainment of the highest possible level of health and well-being for all, at all ages, as its goal. 
The Government of India has made concerted and sustained efforts to march in the direction 
of achieving universal health coverage and delivering quality health care services to all at 
affordable cost that resonate with the targets set in SDGs. With economic growth and shifting 
health needs, India is advancing policies for comprehensive healthcare. 
 
1.2 MINISTRY OF HEALTH AND FAMILY WELFARE 
1.2.1  The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare is the nodal Ministry that is 
responsible for formulation and implementation of health policy in the country. The Ministry 
of Health and Family Welfare comprises of the following two Departments: 
(a) Department of Health and Family Welfare; and 
(b) Department of Health Research 
1.3 VISION MISSION AND OBJECTIVES 
1.3.1  The Vision, Mission and Objectives of the Ministry of Health and Family 
Welfare are indicated as under: 

2 
 
Vision 
Attainment of highest possible level of health and well-being for all, 
through preventive and promotive health care and universal access to good 
quality health services without anyone having to face financial hardship as a 
consequence. 
Mission 
(i) To ensure availability of quality healthcare on equitable, accessible and 
affordable basis across regions and communities with special focus on under-
served population and marginalized groups. 
(ii) To establish comprehensive primary healthcare delivery system and well-
functioning linkages with secondary and tertiary care health delivery system. 
(iii)To develop the training capacity for providing human resources for health 
(medical, paramedical and managerial) with adequate skill mix at all levels. 
(iv) To regulate health service delivery and promote rational use of 
pharmaceuticals in the country. 
Objectives 
(i) To improve health status of the people through concerted action. 
(ii) To expand preventive, promotive, curative, palliative and rehabilitative 
services provided through public health sector with focus on quality. 
(iii)To progressively achieve Universal Health Coverage. 
1.3.2  The Committee takes note of the vision, mission and objectives of the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare which determines the roadmap for activities 
of the Department. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should 
undertake the projects/schemes/plans in consonance with the vision and mission of the 
Department and execute the same with the intention to achieve the cherished objectives. 
The Committee believes that the Department would move forward with Management by 
Objective (MBO) rather than Management by Activities (MBA) to achieve the targets 
set in National Health Policy (NHP) 2017 and under specific projects.   
 
1.4 INSTITUTIONAL MECHANISM 
1.4.1  The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare has the following two attached 
Offices: 
(a) Directorate General of Health Services (DGHS): It is an attached office of 
the Department of Health and Family Welfare. DGHS provides technical 
advice on medical and public health matters and is involved in the 
implementation of various health services. 
(b) National Health Authority: National Health Authority (NHA) has been set up 
as an attached Office to Ministry of Health & Family welfare in 2019. It is the 

3 
 
apex body responsible for the implementation of India's flagship public health 
insurance/assurance scheme "Ayushman Bharat - Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya 
Yojana (AB-PMJAY)" and has been entrusted with the role of designing 
strategy, building technological infrastructure and implementation of "National 
Digital Health Mission" to create a National Digital Health Eco-system. 
1.4.2  Several statutory and regulatory bodies, Autonomous bodies/ Institutions, 
Central Government Health Scheme etc. work under the Department.

1.4.3  The Committee is of the view that Institutions/Organisations are the 
organs of the Department which play pivotal role in the successful functioning of the 
Department, therefore, efficient and effective working of the organisations/institutions is 
indispensable for result-oriented implementation of the undertaken projects/schemes. 
The Committee, therefore, desires that the officers at the top of the Department should 
ensure to set up dynamic working equilibrium amongst various institutions in order to 
achieve the objectives of the Department. The Committee feels that a streamlined 
monitoring mechanism with Always Better Control (ABC) would further ensure the 
desirable Rate of Returns (RoR). The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should keep constant Strength, Weaknesses, Opportunities and Threats 
(SWOT) Analysis of its organs/institutions for better results.  
 
1.5 FLAGSHIP SCHEMES 
1.5.1  Besides, some of the Flagship Schemes of the Ministry of Health and Family 
Welfare are enumerated as under: 
A. Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY): 
 The Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY) aims at correcting 
regional imbalances in the availability of affordable tertiary healthcare Services and to 
augment facilities for quality medical education in the country. The Scheme has two 
components, namely - (i) Setting up of All India Institute of Medical Sciences 
(AIIMS); and (ii) Upgradation of existing Government Medical Colleges/institutions 
(GMCIs). So far setting up of 22 new AIIMS and 75 up-gradation projects of existing 
GMCIs have been approved under the Scheme in various phases. The Up-gradation 
programme broadly envisages improving tertiary health infrastructure through 
construction of Super Speciality Blocks / Trauma Care Centres etc. and/or 
procurement of medical equipment at/for existing Government Medical Colleges / 
Institution.  
B. Central Government Health Scheme: 
 This Scheme provides comprehensive medical facilities to Central Government 
Employees, pensioners and members of their families in addition to other specified 

5 
 
categories like Members of Parliament (MPs), ex-MPs, ex-Governors, ex-Vice-
Presidents, Retired Judges of Supreme Court and High Court, Freedom Fighters and 
members of their family,etc. The facilities under this scheme include outpatient care 
through a network of allopathic, ayurvedic, homoepathic, unani/siddha 
dispensaries/units. 
C. Ayushman Bharat - Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJAY): 
Being the world's largest health assurance scheme, AB- PMJAY aims to 
provide health cover of Rs. 5 Lakhs per family per year for secondary and tertiary care 
hospitalization to poor and vulnerable population of the country. Initially, with 12 
crore families under the scheme, the beneficiary base has been further expanded by 
including 37 lakh ASHAs/Anganwadi Workers (AWWs)/Anganwadi Helpers 
(AWHs) and approximate 4.5 crore senior citizens across the country. 
D. Pradhan Mantri Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM- 
ABHIM):  
 
 This is a Centrally Sponsored Scheme with  some Central Sector component  
spread over 5 years from 2021-22 to 2025-26 for implementation of the Atmanirbhar 
Bharat Package for Health Sector announced by the Hon’ble Prime Minister as part of 
the series of Government measures for providing stimulus to the economy. The 
measures under the PM ABHIM focus on developing capacities of health systems and 
institutions across the continuum of care at all levels; primary, secondary and tertiary, 
to prepare health systems in responding effectively to the current and future 
pandemics/disasters 
 
E. Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM): 
 Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM) provides for creating a National 
Digital health ecosystem that supports universal health coverage in an efficient, 
accessible, inclusive, affordable, timely and safe manner through provision of a wide 
range of data, information and infrastructure services, duly leveraging open, 
interoperable, standards based digital systems and ensuring the security, 
confidentiality and privacy of health-related personal information. 
1.5.2  The Committee takes notes of the flagship schemes of the Department, 
successful implementation thereof ultimately rates the success of the Department as a 
whole. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should enhance the 
executing capacity of the implementing agency so that the absorbing capacity of the 
scheme is increased to the optimal level. This would ensure the optimum utilisation of 
the allocated funds under the schemes which would enable the Department to seek the 
budgetary allocation as per the projected demand in the ensuing financial years.

Welfare, the in-position strength vis-a-vis the sanctioned strength of Group A, B and C posts

1.6.5  In respect of the Department of Health and Family Welfare, the BE 2025-26

(i) 10,000 additional medical seats will be added in medical colleges and 
hospitals, towards the goal of adding 75,000 medical seats in the next 5 years  
(ii) Government will facilitate the establishment of Day Care Cancer Centers in all

2025-26

schemes pertaining to Health Sector:

healthcare to them under PMJAY which is likely to assist nearly one crore Gig-
workers. 
(ii) Medical Tourism in India will be promoted along with private sector, including 
easier Visa norms.  
1.6.7  The Committee in this Report will examine the budgetary allocation and the 
utilisation trend of various Schemes and initiatives under the Department of Health and 
Family Welfare and accordingly give recommendations on the Demands for Grants of the 
Department. The Department of Health & Family Welfare is implementing various schemes, 
programmes and national initiatives to provide universal access to quality healthcare as per 
the commitments of the National Health Policy 2017.

CHAPTER II 
 
ANALYSIS OF BUDGETARY ALLOCATIONS  
 
2.1 BUDGETARY ALLOCATION 
 
2.1.1  While examining the Demand for Grants (2025-26) of the Department of 
Health and Family Welfare, the Committee in its meeting held on 14th February, 2025 heard 
the views of the Secretary, Department of Health and Family Welfare. The Secretary 
submitted the following details on the budget allocation to the Department of Health & 
Family Welfare:-

2.1.3  The Department apprised the Committee that the budgetary allocation 
depends on the overall Government budgetary resources availability, the current 

9 
 
demand by various Programme Divisions of the Department, availability of funds as per 
annual envelope of the schemes and pace of expenditure of the previous financial year. 
Ministry of Finance, accordingly, allocate ceiling of Budget Estimates. The Committee 
notes that a sum of Rs. 95,957.87 crore has been allocated to the Department of Health 
and Family Welfare for 2025-26, representing 1.89% of the total Union Budget estimate 
and 0.27% of GDP (at current prices). A review of past trends indicates that actual 
expenditure has often fallen short of the budgeted allocation, remaining at just 0.27% of 
GDP in both 2022-23 and 2023-24. 
 
2.1.4  The Committee notes the 10.83% increase in the Budget Estimate (BE) for 
2025-26 as compared to the Revised Estimate (RE) for 2024-25, but the Committee 
expresses concern over trend of the health sector's budget allocation, as a percentage of 
GDP and the total Union Budget that has been consistently declining since 2022-23. 
There is a need to accelerate the rate of improving healthcare to global standard and 
proportional to the rate of population growth. India being the most populous country, 
and given the inflationary pressures and the National Health Policy's target of 
increasing government health expenditure to 2.5% of GDP by 2025, the Committee 
believes that the allocation to the health sector, particularly to the Department of Health 
and Family Welfare, should have been much higher. The Committee, therefore, urges 
the Department to take concrete steps to enhance the utilization of allocated funds 
effectively so that the targets set under a specific scheme are achieved.

2.2.4  The Committee notes that since FY 2022-23, the Revised Estimates have 
consistently been lower than the Budget Estimates. This trend undermines the 
credibility of the Department’s demand for higher fund allocations. Moreover, the 
concern is further reinforced by the fact that, except for FY 2023-24, the actual 
expenditure has remained below the Revised Estimate, which itself is already lower than 
the Budget Estimate. This raises concerns about the effective utilization of allocated 
funds in real terms. 
2.2.5  The Committee notes that, as of January 31, 2025, the Department had 
utilized 81.32% of the total Revised Estimate, leaving 18.68% of the funds, 
approximately ₹16,000 crores, available unspent for the remaining two months. The 
Ministry of Finance has repeatedly issued guidelines to regulate expenditure within a 
financial year. In its 143rd Report, the Committee recommended that the Department of 
Health and Family Welfare cap its expenditure in the final month of the financial year 
in accordance with existing guidelines and closely monitor fund utilization during the 
last quarter. This is crucial to ensuring the prudent and efficient use of allocated funds. 
2.2.6  Public health plays a crucial role in driving the country’s economic 
development. Given the inadequacy and overstretched nature of public health 
infrastructure, investments in the health sector must be increased. With inflation 
expected to remain high in the near term, the Department should consider for 
requesting higher budgetary allocations for the health sector at the Revised Estimate 
stage and ensure the optimal utilization of allocated funds. 
2.2.7  On a query asking for the criteria of estimation and ratio of revenue and capital 
outlay to the total health budget, the Department submitted that the budgetary allocation of 
the Revenue and Capital part of overall Budget is formulated considering the overall envelop 
of the Schemes/Non-Schemes on the basis of recurring demands raised by the Programme 
Divisions under the Department of Health & Family Welfare on year to year basis and status 
of on-going projects progress at different stages. The nature of this Department is ‘Social 
Service Sector’ hence there is a limited scope of Capital expenditure. However, as per recent 
developments a thrust on Infrastructure up-gradation/capital expenditure has been made and 
efforts are being made to increase the capital expenditure through Capital and GIA- Capital 
budget heads (part of revenue expenditure head). A trend for five year’s allocation for 
Revenue and Capital are as under:

*  GIA Capital creation is part of the Revenue expenditure however it is spent for the purpose of 
capital creation of Autonomous Bodies and other Institutions and constitutes a major factor for 
Capital creation.  
 
2.2.8  The Committee notes that the primary determinant for revenue and 
capital outlay in healthcare budgeting is the annual, recurring demands from program 
divisions within the Department of Health & Family Welfare, coupled with the progress 
of ongoing projects. While the social service nature of the Department inherently limits 
capital expenditure, a recent push for infrastructure upgrades is evident, with "GIA-
Capital Creation" (grants-in-aid for capital creation) playing a significant role in 
boosting effective capital spending, despite being categorized under revenue. Analyzing 
the five-year trend reveals to the Committee that revenue expenditure consistently 
dominates, averaging over 90% of the total budget. However, the effective capital 
expenditure, including GIA-Capital, shows a fluctuating pattern in the period from FY 
2021-22 to FY 2025-26, decreasing consistently since the FY 2023-24, from 27.22 % in 
2022-23 to 15.60% in 2025-26 of the total allocation. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends establishing a formalized, multi-year strategic capital expenditure plan, 
distinct from annual recurring demands, that clearly outlines infrastructure 
development priorities and targets, including specific allocations for GIA-Capital, to 
ensure sustained and predictable growth in healthcare capital assets, thereby creating a 
more balanced approach between revenue and capital spending.  
 
2.3 GOVERNMENT HEALTH EXPENDITURE IN INDIA 
2.3.1  The Department of Health and Family Welfare, on a specific query, submitted 
that as per the latest National Health Account (NHA) Estimates 2021-22, the Government 
Health Expenditure (GHE) is 1.84% of GDP and Union Government Health Expenditure is 
41.8% of GHE. As per the NHA estimates 2021-22, the share of GHE in the total GDP of the 
country has increased from 1.35% in 2017-18 to 1.84% in 2021-22. As per available NHA

health institutions to universal comprehensive primary healthcare services

(iii) 15th Finance Commission Grants: The 15th Finance Commission has 
recommended health grants of Rs.70,051 crore to Local Government for 
strengthening the primary health infrastructure in rural and urban areas. 
 
2.3.3  National Health Policy (NHP), 2017 prescribed increasing Government 
Health Expenditure (GHE) as a share of GDP to 2.5% by 2025. Following the objective 
of NHP, 2017, GHE, as a share of GDP, has increased from 1.35% in 2017-18 to 1.84% 
in 2021-22, indicating progress in health sector investment. The Committee notes that 
the upward trend in GHE is positive; however, further acceleration is required to meet 
the 2.5% GDP target by 2025. The current pace of increase may not be sufficient to 
achieve this goal within the remaining timeframe. A strategic focus on public health 
infrastructure, preventive care, and efficient resource utilization will be crucial in 
ensuring long-term improvements in healthcare accessibility and quality. 
 
2.3.4  A close scrutiny of the Health Budget reflects the lack of priority assigned 
to health. The Committee observes that the declining trend in health budget allocation in 
terms of BE as % of GDP (at Current Price) between 2020-21 and 2024-25 (refer to 
Table 2.3 above) do not align with the target set in National Health Policy. The 
Committee is of the opinion that adequate health financing forms the pillar of a well-
functioning health system which is crucial for reduction of Out-of-Pocket Expenditure 
(OOPE) in health. The pandemic further reaffirmed the need of a sustainable health 
financing ecosystem especially in tackling a sudden public health crisis. The Committee 
recommends the Government to prioritize Health Sector in Budget Allocation by 
increasing the annual budget allocation for healthcare to maintain the momentum and 
ensure sufficient resources for infrastructure, medical research, and service delivery. 
 
2.3.5  The Committee, in its 126 th Report on DFG (2022-23), had also 
recommended the Government for increasing its health expenditure to 5% of GDP by 
2025. However, with such stagnant allocation to health and the slow pace of increase in 
allocation, the goal of achieving the NHP target of health expenditure to 2.5% seems a 
distant dream.  
 
2.3.6  The Committee is of the view that strengthening of public health 
infrastructure,  enhancing preventive and primary care services, leveraging Public-
Private Partnerships (PPP), exploring additional revenue sources, innovation in health, 
robust monitoring and evaluation mechanism to track the utilization of health funds and 
assess the impact are crucial for establishing an affordable and accessible healthcare 

delivery system, especially for a developing economy like India. The need of the hour is to recognize health as a key sector for economic as well as social development. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends the Government to make health a priority and increase its investment in the health sector.   2.3.7  The Department also submitted that as per the Estimates 2021-22, the Government Health Expenditure (GHE) is 48.0% and OutExpenditure (OOPE) is 39.4% as share of Total Health Expenditure (THE) in 2021increase in government spending on health has an impfinancial hardship endured by households. appreciates that the 25 States and the Union Territories of Delhi and Puducherry as per 
the NHA Estimates for FY 2021-22 have achieved the National Health Policy target of 
8% of their budget allocated to health. The Committee recommends that other State/UT 
Governments should follow the same.  
 
2.3.14  The Committee, in its 134th Report, had highlighted the existing wide 
inter-State and intra-State disparity in healthcare infrastructure across the country. The 
Committee observes that States with poor financial resources and inefficient fiscal 
capacity face umpteen challenges in establishing a robust health infrastructure. The 
Committee, accordingly, reiterates its recommendation that the Union Government 
must persuade the remaining State/UT Governments to achieve the prescribed 
budgetary health expenditure to 8% of State General Government Expenditure.  
 
2.3.15  The Committee observes that the data on General Health Expenditure 
(GHE), Total Health Expenditure (THE), Out-of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE), and 
other relevant indicators is only available up to the year 2021-22, with the National 
Health Accounts (NHA) Estimates for 2021-22 being released in September 2024. The 
Committee expresses concern over delay in updating data as old and obsolete data 
significantly hampers the assessment of the progress made by various government 
schemes and policies. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Government to 
update data pertaining to GHE, THE, OOPE, etc. without delay, sooner the better.  
 
2.3.16  In today's digital age, where Information Technology and Artificial 
Intelligence play a key role, it is crucial to leverage these tools for the efficient collection 
and real-time processing of data. Therefore, the Committee recommends that the 
Government take immediate steps to integrate digital tools into the data collection 
process. Additionally, the Government should ensure that data for a given financial year 
is made available by the last quarter of the following financial year. This approach will 
rationalize data analysis, enabling more effective evaluation and improvement of 
government schemes and policies, ensuring they are aligned with the intended objectives 
and targeted effectively. 
 
 
2.4 15 th FINANCE COMMISSION HEALTH GRANTS 
 
2.4.1  As informed by the Department, the 15th Finance Commission (FC-XV) 
Health Grants were announced as part of the Union Budget 2021-22 to address gaps in 
primary healthcare facilities across India. The total allocation for health grants through Local 
Governments is Rs. 70,051 crore, out of which Rs. 61,661.95 crore has been approved for 
states over five financial years (FY 2021-22 to FY 2025-26). The year-wise approval is as 
under:

2.4.2  As on 30th January 2025, total of Rs. 24,304.24 crore (39.42%) have been 
released to the States. The year-wise releases are:

Rs.14,415.95 crore. The year-wise expenditure against the fund released is as under: 
 
Rs. 9051.25 Crore (69 %) in FY 2021-22,  
Rs. 4193.76 Crore (62%) in FY 2022-23,  
Rs. 1157.94 Crore (34%) in FY 2023-24 and  
Rs 13 Crore (1.3%) in FY 2024-25    
 
2.4.4  The Committee observes that, as of January 30, 2025, only 39.42% of the 
funds approved by the 15th Finance Commission for the States have been released, with 
only the funds for the fiscal year 2021-22 being fully disbursed. The States have reported 
spending only 59.31% of the released funds. These funds were specifically allocated to 
address gaps in primary healthcare facilities across the country. However, the States' 
low utilization of these funds undermines the purpose of this financial support. In light 
of these concerns, the Committee strongly recommends that the Government conduct a 
comprehensive study to determine the underlying causes of this under-utilization and 
implement measures to resolve these issues. Furthermore, the Union Government should 
establish a dedicated communication channel with the States to facilitate continuous 
dialogue and ensure timely and effective resolution of challenges, thereby optimizing the 
utilization of these critical funds for primary healthcare. The Committee expresses 
serious concern that the Department must persuade the states for optimal utilization of 
funds released to the States.

(iii) For the Financial Year 2025-26, CGHS has proposed to construct a ‘State of 
the Art Multi-Facility Centre’ at Koliwada, Mumbai. The estimated worth of the 
project is Rs.122 crores for which Rs.49.5 crore has been projected in BE 2025-26 
under Buildings and Structures Object Head.  
3.1.3  In addition to the above, CGHS has signed MoU with Morarji Desai National 
Institute of Yoga to provide preventive health care facilities through Yoga in 20 CGHS 
dispensaries in Delhi NCR. In 2019, 53 Ayush Units (27 Homeopathic & 26 Ayurveda) have 
been sanctioned to be opened in cities where CGHS Allopathic Dispensaries are already 
functioning across the country, out of which, 30 Ayush Units (19 Homeopathic & 11 
Ayurveda) have been made functional.  
3.1.4  The Committee acknowledges the Government's efforts to digitize 
healthcare resources through the Healthcare Management Information System (HMIS), 
establish new Wellness Centres in tier-II cities, and promote Ayush Centres. However, 
the examination of budget allocation and utilization under Revenue Head reflects a 
significant gap between the RE allocation of Rs. 2245.82 crore for CGHS and the actual 
expenditure of Rs. 1842.51 crore, representing only 82% utilization as of February 7, 
2025. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department conducts a thorough 
and immediate review of the CGHS expenditure process, specifically focusing on 
identifying and addressing the bottlenecks causing the underutilization of allocated 
funds. By streamlining administrative procedures, improving procurement processes, 
and ensuring timely disbursement of funds, the Department can ensure that the 
allocated resources are effectively used to enhance healthcare services for beneficiaries, 
thereby maintaining the intended continuity between budget allocation and service 
delivery. 
3.1.5  In its 155th Report, the Committee observed that, as of August 2023, 26% 
of the 340 allopathic wellness centres (spread across 80 cities) were concentrated in the 
Delhi NCR region. Additionally, only six states (excluding Delhi NCR) have more than 
ten CGHS centres, while Arunachal Pradesh and the Union Territories of Ladakh, 
Andaman & Nicobar Islands, and Lakshadweep have none. The Committee noted that 
CGHS services are unevenly distributed, limiting accessibility in many regions. The 
Committee, therefore, wishes to highlight that numerous cities and towns with a 
significant population of Central Government employees, pensioners, and their 
dependents lack CGHS wellness centres. As a result, beneficiaries—particularly 
retirees—have to travel long distances for medical treatment, causing financial strain 
and other hardships. To address this, the Committee urges the Ministry to proactively 
advocate for new CGHS centres in underserved areas, particularly in suburban regions 
with a substantial number of serving and retired beneficiaries. The Committee 
recommends that the Ministry engage with the Ministry of Finance for speedy approval 
of new CGHS Wellness Centres. Moreover, the Committee suggests that the 
Government should consider revising existing norms or introducing new ones to 

22 
 
facilitate the establishment of wellness centres in rural, hilly, remote, and Northeastern 
regions. 
3.1.6  The Committee notes the Government’s initiative to integrate the Indian 
system of medicine with the CGHS wellness centre network. However, in its 155th 
Report, the Committee notes that the number of Ayush centres increased from 85 in 
March 2014 to 107 in August 2023, a modest 25% growth over nine years. As of 
February 2025, this number has just risen slightly to 111. The Committee observes with 
concern that such limited slow pace of expansion does not align with the Government’s 
vision to promote indigenous healthcare to under-served regions especially in remote 
areas. The Committee, therefore, recommends the speedy operationalisation of the 
remaining 23 Ayush units out of the targeted 53 and urges the Government to establish 
additional Ayush centres nationwide while actively promoting traditional Indian 
medicine among CGHS beneficiaries. 
3.1.7  The Committee takes into account the proposal to establish a State-of-the-
Art Multi-facility Centre in Mumbai. The Committee reiterates its recommendation, as 
made in its 155th Report, for setting up at least ten CGHS hospitals with advanced 
facilities and research capabilities, particularly for critical illnesses such as cancer, 
cardiovascular diseases, and renal failure. The Committee further desires that at least 
one such advanced multi-facility centre be established in major cities across the country. 
3.1.8  In response to a specific query on strengthening of monitoring mechanism for 
quality assurance of drugs supplied through CGHS, the Ministry informed that in the bulk 
procurement of medicines in CGHS {procured by the Medical Store Organisation (MSO) of 
DGHS, MoHFW}, each batch of medicine is tested twice (by two independent CDSCO-
approved labs) before the consignment is accepted at the time of procurement. In the case of 
supply of medicines through Jan Aushadhi Pariyojana (JAP), each medicine is tested by an 
independent NABL-accredited lab before acceptance of supply by Pharmaceuticals and 
Medical Devices Bureau of India (PMBI).  
3.1.9  The Committee wishes to highlight with concerns over the quality of 
medicines dispensed at dispensary/pharmacy counters. At several time, apprehensions 
and complaints have been raised regarding the effectiveness of medicines procured 
through MSO and JAP, underscoring the critical importance of quality in healthcare 
services. Ensuring compliance with the World Health Organization’s (WHO) Good 
Manufacturing Practice (GMP) standards is essential, and any quality-related 
complaints must be addressed seriously and quickly to generate faith towards quality of 
medicines delivered at dispensary counters.  
3.1.10  The Committee takes into account the acknowledgement of the 
Department about the quality assurance of drugs, and the need for stronger quality 
assurance measures viz. implementing a systematic drug testing program at the Wellness 
Centre level. The Directorate is also considering empanelling the Quality Council of 
India for enhanced monitoring. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Government establish a rigorous quality control mechanism without delay, ensuring 
systematic drug testing at the Wellness Centre level. Additionally, the Department 

23 
 
should conduct surprise audits by randomly testing medicine batches through an 
independent NABL-accredited or internationally reputed laboratory. 
3.1.11  The Ministry inter-alia informed that the following measures have been taken 
to simplify referrals and drug dispensing under CGHS: 
i) The referral process has been simplified wherein the validity of referral has been 
extended to 03 months with six consultations, and no further endorsement is needed 
for routine listed investigations/minor surgeries.  
ii) Direct Specialist Consultation has now been allowed for pensioner beneficiaries 
aged 70 and above without the need of a CGHS referral. 
iii) Restricted medicines have been clearly defined and new instructions have been 
issued for restricted medicines that pave the way for a systematic inclusion of new 
medicines for use by CGHS beneficiaries.  
iv) In case of medical emergency, no referral is required for undergoing treatment from 
CGHS empanelled Hospitals and even from non-empanelled hospitals. CGHS 
empanelled hospitals have been instructed to provide treatment on a cashless basis.  
v) In case a CGHS beneficiary is not in a position to physically visit the CGHS 
Wellness Centre, a request for referral to an empanelled hospital/endorsement may 
be submitted through an authorized representative. 
vi) In case of emergency, permission from the competent Authority for any non-listed 
investigations shall not be required. 
3.1.12  The Committee continues to recommend the Department to simplify 
referral procedures further as there is need to significantly reduce the burden on 
patients and their families by eliminating the requirement for repeated visits to 
dispensaries during illness. The Committee notes the provision made allowing CGHS 
pensioner beneficiaries aged 70 and above to directly consult specialists without 
requiring a CGHS referral, however, the Committee reiterates its recommendation 
made in the 155th Report to further review the guidelines and extend the benefit to all 
CGHS pensioners/beneficiaries aged 60 and above for continuing medical care to CGHS 
beneficiaries. Allowing direct specialist consultations at empanelled private hospitals 
without a CGHS referral would ensure consistency and continuity of care with the 
existing system, where pensioners/beneficiaries aged 60 and above already receive 
cashless treatment from these hospitals. 
3.1.13  With regards to the status of recruitment of non-gazetted and gazetted officers 
category under CGHS, the Department submitted that the CHS Division of Ministry of Health 
and Family Welfare recruits Medical Officers through Combined Medical Services 
Examination (CMSE) conducted by UPSC. Similarly, an 'Integrated Recruitment System' has 
been implemented under CGHS, wherein the 24 CGHS cities all over India have been divided 
into nine (09) zones in conformation with the regional offices of Staff Selection Commission. 
The respective designated Zonal Heads/Additional Directors (ADs) are undertaking the 
recruitment process for Group-B (Non-Gazetted) and Group-C posts under CGHS through the 
concerned regional offices of Staff Selection Commission. In order to streamline the 
recruitment in CGHS, it has been decided in the meeting held on 28.1.2025 with Staff 

Selection Commission that currently CGHS has nine integrated zones and the same willreduced to one at CGHS headquarters, Delhi. 3.1.14 The Committee observedispensaries, with 2,023 positions (28.81% of the sanctioned strength) unfilled in Allopathic Dispensaries and 111 positions (26.12%) in Ayush Dispensaries. say that the vacancies negatively impact the doctorwaiting times, and reduce the quality of care.recommends that the Government should promptly filladequate staffing. Status of Projects: 
 
1. Construction of Lecture theatre in VMMC:- The tree transplantation work has been 
kept on hold in view of the direction issued by the Forest deptt. in compliance of the 
directions dated 31.08.2023 of Hon’ble High Court of Delhi in the ongoing Court Case 
No1149/2023: Bhavreen Kandhari Vs Shri C D Singh & Others.  
 
2. Boys Hostel, West Kidwai Nagar: Delhi Development Authority has allotted land 
measuring 1.58Ha. at Sector G-2, G-3, G-4, G-6, G-7, G-8 Narela sub-city for 
compensatory afforestation. However, the tree transplantation work is kept on hold in 
view of the direction dated 31.08.2023 of Hon’ble high Court of Delhi in the ongoing 
Court Case. 
 
3. Dwarka Housing Project: The project is in Progress. 24% physical progress has been 
achieved as on date. Tentative date of completion of the project is 31.03.2026.  
 
3.2.3  On a specific query related to key concern areas of SJH and VMMC for 
optimum functioning, the Ministry submitted that the institution is facing staffing shortages, 
space constraints, and infrastructure delays. Additionally, there is inadequate internet 
connectivity, and a lack of accommodations for resident doctors.  
 
3.2.4  The Committee takes note of delay in the construction of the lecture 
theatre and boys' hostel of the SJH and VMMC Institute, where tree transplantation 
work has been kept on hold in compliance of directions of the Hon’ble High Court of 
Delhi. To address this issue, the Committee desires that the Department promptly 
pursue the matter with court and take necessary steps to resolve the litigation on 
priority. This will help prevent any adverse impact on project timelines. Additionally, 
the Committee suggests that a review of such legal challenges be incorporated into 
policy-making processes to mitigate similar obstacles in future projects. 
3.2.5  The Committee acknowledges the increase in capital outlay from ₹119.15 
crore in RE 2024-25 to ₹238.50 crore in BE 2025-26. However, it notes that the shortfall 
in capital allocation during FY 2024-25 adversely impacted the progress of key 
infrastructure projects, including the construction of the MCH block, Auditorium, Boys' 
Hostel at VMMC, Dwarka Housing Project, Raj Nagar Staff Quarters, and 
Dharamshala Project. The Committee urges the Department, subject to the progress of 
pending court cases, to enhance the capital outlay further at RE stage for timely 
completion of all infrastructure projects in the FY 2025-26. 
3.2.6  Given that the Dwarka Housing Project has achieved 24% physical 
progress as of February 2025, with a tentative completion date of 31.03.2026, the 
Committee urges the Government to ensure the timely completion of all these projects. 
If additional funds are required, they should be provided at the RE stage to prevent 
further delays. Timely completion is essential to enhance facilities and improve the 
overall functioning of the Institute. The Committee in its 134th and 143rd Reports had 
flagged the issues related to slow pace of construction activities and recommended the 
Department to adhere to the timeline of 27 months for completion of the Dwarka 
Project.

3.3.6  The Ministry has identified the major challenges faced by the institution 
as Staffing Shortages, Space Constraints and Infrastructure Delays Project 
Implementation Status. While examining the implementation status of projects 
undertaken by the institute, the Committee wishes to highlight its 
observation/recommendation contained in 143rd Report that the Super Specialty Block 
(SSB) was originally scheduled for completion on March 10, 2022, later extended to 
November 28, 2023. The revised deadline of April 27, 2024 was not met and the new 
expected handover date is May 31, 2025. Further, in respect of the Medical College, the 
Committee notes that the tender, floated by the consulting agency HITES, is yet to be 
opened, leading to further delays. The Committee reviews the Statement of Expenditure 
(as of February 7, 2025) and finds poor and inefficient fund utilization for the ongoing 
projects. The Committee is deeply concerned about the repeated extensions and 
inefficiencies in project execution.  
 
3.3.7  To address these issues related to delay in projects and inefficient 
utilization of funds, the Committee strongly recommends the Department to adopt a 
robust project management strategy by implementing advanced tools and best practices, 
strengthen accountability mechanisms and strictly adhere to the new completion 

timeline to avoid further extensions. 3.4.  LADY HARDINGE MEDICAL COLLEGE (LHMC) & SMT. S.K. HOSPITAL

3.4.1  The total budgetary allocation to the tune of Rs. 800 crore as BE 2025-26 has 
been made for the Lady Hardinge Medical College (LHMC) and Smt. Sucheta Kriplani 
Hospital (SKH). Out of the total allocation, Rs. 101 crore has been earmarked for 
procurement of equipment(s) under approved project "Augmentation of Infrastructure of 
LHMC 2022".  
3.4.2  While examining the Demands for Grants for 2025-26, the Committee 
observes that the total budgetary allocation of Rs. 750 crore at the Budget Estimate (BE) 
stage for 2024-25, which included a capital outlay of Rs. 101 crore, was reduced to Rs. 
630 crore at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage, with the capital outlay declining to Rs. 
51.50 crore. When asked about the reasons for this variation, the Department attributed 
it to delays in obtaining approvals from the competent authority in the early part of the 
financial year. The Committee is particularly concerned that the capital outlay was 
reduced by 49% at the RE stage in 2024-25. To address this issue, the Committee 
recommends that the Department should streamline the approval process and establish 
a Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) to ensure efficiency and reduce delays. 
Additionally, monitoring of fund utilization and evaluation of infrastructure projects 
should be strengthened to enhance effective implementation.

vis-à-vis in-position strength (contractual and regular) as follows: -

3.4.4 The Committee observes that there is significant manpower crunch across 
various groups in the LHMC and SKH institutions impacting the institute’s optimal 
functioning. The in-position strength is only 64% of the sanctioned strength which also 
includes the contractual staff. Some of the critical cadre like Neurology and Cardiology 
have zero in-position strength. The Committee take a serious view of this manpower 
crunch and recommends the Department to take immediate steps to streamline the 
recruitment process and fill up the vacant positions at the earliest.

3.5.1  The Committee, on examination of the Demands for Grants, notes that in the 
FY 2024-25, the BE amounting to Rs. 180 crore was reduced to Rs. 155.85 crore at RE stage. 
In specific, the capital outlay was decreased by 50.75% from Rs. 7.94 crore at BE stage to Rs. 
3.91 crore at RE stage. In the FY 2023-24 also, the actual expenditure was Rs. 149.25 crore 
(consisting of expenditure of Rs. 4.83 crore in capital head) against the RE amounting to Rs. 
167.14 crore (consisting of capital outlay amounting to Rs. 9.30 crore). This trend of under-
utilisation budget outlay reflects inefficient deployment of funds by the institute.  
 
3.5.2  The Committee, specifically, expresses concern on high under-utilisation 
of funds under capital head which is pivotal for up-gradation of infrastructure and 
maintenance of machinery and equipments in the hospital. The Committee, therefore, 
urges the Department to ensure optimal utilisation of budget allocated to the institution 
by assisting the institute in review of the reasons for under-utilisation and in timely 
approval of the proposals/projects under consideration.

3.5.4  The Department provided the details of sanctioned strength vis-à-vis in 
position (contractual and regular) strength across various cadres of the Kalawati Saran 
Children’s Hospital (KSCH) that of the sanctioned strength of 762, there exist 139 
vacant posts which is 18% of the sanctioned strength. The Committee, therefore, finds 
that there is manpower crunch in the institution which should be addressed on priority 
basis to fill up the vacant posts.  
 
3.5.5  In response to a specific query regarding ongoing and proposed 
projects/initiatives, the Committee was informed that a proposal has been made to upgrade 
KSCH into the “Kalawati Saran National Institute of Medical Science for Children,” with an 
additional 500 beds, including ICU beds. This proposal has received in-principle approval 
from the Department of Expenditure, with an estimated cost of ₹497 crores, and is currently 
under the process of DIB appraisal. Additionally, it was submitted that KSCH, a 70-year-old 
children's hospital, serves a large number of patients. A significant challenge faced by the 
hospital is the declaration of its old building as unsafe by CPWD. The hospital is in the 
process of catering to indoor healthcare services from the old building of SKH.  
 
3.5.6  The Committee recommends that the Government prioritize addressing 
the infrastructure-related challenges of KSCH and expedite the approval and 
implementation of the proposed up-gradation project in a time-bound manner.

A total of 22 new AIIMS have been announced so far under this component. Out of 22 
AIIMS approved under PMSSY, 18 AIIMS are functional. 
(ii)  Upgradation of Government Medical College (GMC)/ Institutions. Each up-
gradation project would be adding 8-10 Super Speciality Departments; around 
15 new PG seats; and 150-250 beds.  
A total of 75 Projects have been considered under this component for 75 Government Medical 
Colleges (GMCs) under different phases across the country. Out of 75 GMCs approved for 
up-gradation, 70 have been completed and 3 are at various stages of up-gradation. In the 
remaining two cases, works are yet to start. 
3.6.2  Under the Scheme, funds to the tune of Rs.2200 crore (Revenue: Rs. 60 crore 
& Capital: Rs. 2140 crore) had been allotted in BE 2024-25.  However, the same was reduced 
to Rs. 1736 crore (Rs. 70 crore and Rs. 1666 crore under Revenue and Capital head 
respectively) at RE stage.  In BE 2025-26, Rs. 60 crore and Rs. 2140 crore has been allocated 
under Revenue and Capital head respectively.  
3.6.3  The Committee expresses concern over the significant reduction in the 
PMSSY Scheme's budget allocation from Rs. 2200 crore in Budget Estimates (BE) 2024-
25 to Rs. 1736 crore in Revised Estimates (RE) 2024-25. While noting the actual 
expenditure of Rs. 1552.36 crore as of 31.01.2025, indicating 89.42% of the RE 
allocation and 70.56% of the BE, the Committee emphasizes the importance of 
consistent and adequate funding for the effective implementation of the PMSSY Scheme. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends the Ministry to ensure the judicious and 
optimal utilization of the remaining allocated funds by the end of the financial year. The 
Ministry should investigate and implement processes to increase the efficiency of 
spending, to ensure the maximum benefit from the allocated funds towards achievement 
of set targets/objectives/goals under PMSSY. 
3.6.4  Recognising the allocation of Rs. 2200 crore (Rs. 60 crore Revenue and Rs. 
2140 crore Capital) in BE 2025-26, the Committee urges upon the Ministry to even 
utilization of funds throughout the financial year, thereby avoiding significant 
reductions at the RE stage, unless absolutely necessary and supported by compelling 
reasons/justifications. The efficient utilization of funds is indispensable to ensure the 
effective implementation of the PMSSY Scheme and achieve its intended healthcare 
objectives.

3.6.6  On being asked about the reasons for the decrease in the capital outlay to Rs. 
1666.00 crore in RE 2024-25 in comparison to the capital outlay of Rs. 2140.00 crore in BE 
2024-25, the Department replied that it was anticipated that the work of AIIMS Darbhanga, 
Madurai and Rewari would begin in 2024-25.  However due to non provision of encumbrance 
free site, by the State Government, the work of AIIMS Darbhanhga could not commence. The 
State Govt of Bihar has provided the land in Sep 2024 for establishment of AIIMS in 
Darbhnaga and the pre investment works are in progress. Due to site related conditions IIT 
Delhi has been engaged to conduct soil testing, hydrological, geotechnical studies etc.  
3.6.7  The work of AIIMS Rewari was started in 2024-25 in the initial stage issues 
were faced in mobilisation of and material and in obtaining Statutory clearances. The work of 
AIIMS Madurai awarded in March 2024. The work on ground however started in May, 2024 
after obtaining requisite statutory clearances including environmental clearance from the State 
Govt. Due to start of construction work of three new AIIMS (Madurai, Darbhanga and 
Rewari) BE 2025-26 has been kept same as was BE 2024-25. This may further increase at the 
time of RE depending on the pace of work.

3.6.9  The Committee recommends that the Ministry of Health and Family 
Welfare, in collaboration with state governments, establish a standardized and 
expedited process for land acquisition and obtaining statutory clearances for AIIMS 
projects. This should include formulating clear guidelines and timelines for state 
governments regarding the provision of providing encumbrance-free land; creating a 
fast-track mechanism for obtaining environmental and other necessary clearances; and 
establishing a dedicated coordination cell to address site-related hindrances. Delays in 
land acquisition and statutory clearances are significant bottlenecks in the timely 
completion of AIIMS projects. As evident by the situations in Darbhanga, Madurai, and 
Rewari, these delays lead to cost escalations and hinder the delivery of essential 
healthcare services. 
 
3.6.10  In a reply, the following challenges have been informed by the Department 
which were faced in project implementation that resulted in delay: -  
i. Provision of encumbrance free land by the State Government;  
ii. Time taken in obtaining Statutory Clearances including Environmental Clearance;  
iii. Site related hindrance both man-made and natural including removal of HT Lines, 
IOC Pipe line passing through the land in Madurai, Natural formations like nallah 
in rock outcrops;  
iv. Timely provision of regular supply of potable water, electricity, road 
connectivity, road over-bridge due to a railway line, construction of subway to 
join two land patches across the road; 
v. Land acquisition related issues, encroachment /land disputes; 
vi. Limited availability of labour and harsh weather conditions in remote locations 
like Awantipora;  
vii. Creation of Posts for New AIIMS;  
viii. Recruitment, Shortage of Faculty, Technicians, issues in retaining faculty and 
staff in remote locations like Deogarh, Bilaspur, Raipur;  
ix. Procurement of Medical Equipment by HITES, issues relating to finalisations of 
specifications, single tenders in propriety items, delay in import of high end 
equipment; 
x. Delay in signing of MoU as in case of AIIMS Deogarh by the Jharkhand 
Government; and 
xi. Mentoring of New AIIMS on Clinical and Academic Front. 
 
3.6.11  The following measures/strategies have been taken to combat these challenges 
and for effective utilisation of funds earmarked to various institutes under PMSSY: - 
i. A Project Review Committees under the chairpersonship of Joint Secretary 
(PMSSY) has been set for each ongoing project of setting up of AIIMS. Further, 
progress of projects is reviewed regularly in the Ministry at various levels with 
the Executing Agencies and Procurement Support Agency to ensure timely 
completion of the project and supply of medical equipment. 

41 
 
ii. The Project Monitoring Group (PMG), headed by Secretary (Coordination) in the 
Cabinet Secretariat, supports all mid and large sized Public, Private and ‘Public – 
Private Partnership’ (PPP) Projects with fast-tracking of approvals, sectoral 
policy issues and removal of bottlenecks for expediting project commissioning. 
PMG helps in resolving issues in projects through regular coordination with the 
concerned State Governments and also with other Ministries of the Union 
Government. 
iii. Through Pro-Active Governance and Timely Implementation (PRAGATI) portal, 
the Prime Minister reviews the issues with all the stakeholders including Central 
Ministries/Departments and State Governments for expeditious resolution of 
various issues like land acquisition, utility shifting and other issues for timely 
completion of projects. The PRAGATI portal ensures quick and effective 
decision making for swift resolution. 
iv. In addition to the aforesaid structural institutional arrangements, the issues 
affecting progress of the projects are also taken up with the State Government 
through letters from Secretary (HFW) to the Chief Secretary of the concerned 
State Government. Need-based directions are also given to Institutes/Executive 
Agencies/PSA for expeditious resolution of the issues.  
v. Further, site visits by senior officers of the Ministry and Minister of Health and 
Family Welfare are also being undertaken to monitor the progress.  
vi. Low value medical equipment i.e costing below Rs 5 lakh as per DPR in r/o 11 
AIIMS have been de-scoped from the ambit of HITES and AIIMS to procure the 
same.   
3.6.12  The Committee has been informed that the following provisions have been 
facilitated by the Ministry for augmenting faculty and non-faculty strength in the newly set up 
AIIMS: - 
i. Standing Selection Committee has been as per the provision of the AIIMS Act to 
facilitate expeditious filling up of vacancies. 
ii. Centralized Nursing Officer Recruitment Common Eligibility Test (NORCET) 
and Common Recruitment Examination (CRE) for recruitment of Non-faculty 
posts is conducted by AIIMS Delhi. 
iii. Rolling advertisement for recruitment with one-year validity are issued to fast 
track the process of filling up vacancies. 
iv. Institute of National Importance Combined Entrance Test (INI-CET) and Institute 
of National Importance  Super Specialty (INI-SS) Exam  for SRs/JRs- INI-CET  
and INI- SS conducted twice a year by AIIMS Delhi  for  3,000 and 1,600 seats 
approx. , respectively  for selection of Junior Residents ( Academic ) and  Senior 
Residents ( Academic ) for various AIIMS/INI’s etc.  

42 
 
3.6.13  So far as proposals for setting up AIIMS-like Institutions from other States/ 
UTs  are concerned, the Ministry has received requests from  some States like Karnataka, 
Kerala, Goa, Tripura, Sikkim, Mizoram and Manipur Uttar Pradesh , Bihar ,Maharashtra etc., 
however, at present there is no proposal under consideration  for setting up of more AIIMS-
like institutes in the current phase of PMSSY.  
3.6.14  On a specific query on the extent to which PMSSY has been successful in 
correcting regional imbalances in the availability of affordable/reliable tertiary health care 
services and to augment facilities for quality medical education in the country, the 
Department submitted the following points: - 
i. These AIIMS and GMICIs cover States of Andhra Pradesh, Assam, Bihar, 
Chhattisgarh, Goa, Gujarat, Haryana, Himachal Pradesh, Jharkhand, Karnataka, 
Kerala, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, Odisha, Punjab, Rajasthan, Tamil Nadu, 
Telangana, Tripura, Uttar Pradesh, Uttarakhand, West Bengal and UT of Jammu 
and Kashmir. 
ii. During 2023-24, 1,00,51,620 patients got consultations done in OPD and 
5,00,464  patients got treatment in IPD. 
iii. In the current year 2024-25, approximately 1,04,21,277 patients  visited OPD 
and  6,05,090  patients got treatment in IPD. 
iv. As per the Survey conducted by the office of DG Audit Kolkatta across 10 
AIIMS as part of Performance Audit of the scheme Patients satisfaction have 
been seen across various areas such as diagnostics, affordability, accessibility and 
attendant, help desk and staff behaviour, grievance redressal, use of website and 
online facility, helpline, attendance and admission, pharmacy and diet. 
 
3.6.15  The Department also furnished the status of up-gradation of Government 
Medical Colleges/institutions in the different phases under the PMSSY, and stated that out 
of 75 Government Medical Colleges (GMCs) approved for upgradation, 70 have been 
completed and 3 are at various stages of upgradation. In two cases, works are yet to start. 
Details of these 5 GMCIs are given below: 
 
i. PMCH, Patna Bihar: 91% completed 
ii. GMC, Surat in Gujarat- 73.50% completed 
iii. STCIMST in Kerala: 99.90% completed. Proposed to be inaugurated in Feb 
March 2025  
iv. UCMS-GTB Hospital at Delhi: Upgradation not started yet as State share of 
funds (Rs. 80 Cr.) not received from UT of Delhi. 
v. SCBMCH, Cuttack, Odisha: upgradation not started yet due to frequent changes 
in approved location of GMC by State Government. 
 
3.6.16 The Committee takes into account various challenges confronting the 
Department that result into delay in implementation of projects and strategy measures 
chalked out to combat those challenges for effective utilization of funds earmarked for 
various institutions under PMSSY and timely completion of projects though the 

43 
 
Committee understands that certain challenges are really a Himalayan barrier, the 
resolution of which is of course herculean task. Despite that the Committee believes that 
the Pro-Active Governance and Timely Implementation (PRAGATI) Portal, reviewed 
by the Prime Minister for expeditious resolution of various issues like land acquisition, 
utility shifting, contains the strength of mount-melting capacity with quick and effective 
decision-making for speedy resolution of all vexing issues/challenges. The Committee 
feels that the two components of PMSSY i.e. setting up of new AIIMS and up-gradation 
of Government Medical College/Institutions equipped with basic infrastructural 
facilities and equipments contain capacity measures for correcting regional imbalance in 
the availability of affordable/reliable tertiary healthcare services and to augment 
facilities for quality medical education in the country. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends the Government for identifying more GMCs (beyond already identified 75 
GMCs) and setting up of more AIIMS-like institutions as requests received by the 
Department from Karnataka, Kerala, Goa, Tripura, Sikkim, Mizoram, Manipur, Uttar 
Pradesh, Bihar (in Kosi region) and Maharashtra in the next phase of PMSSY keeping 
in view the density of population, distance from existing AIIMS and regions from where 
patients inflow to AIIMS, New Delhi for treatment, increasing its workload and pushing 
up the waiting time even for critical patients.  
 
3.6.17  The Committee notes the provisons made by the Department for 
augmenting faculty and non-faculty strength in the newly set-up AIIMS viz. Standing 
Selection Committees, Centralised Nursing Officer Recruitment Common Eligiblity Test 
(NORCET), CRE, rolling advertisement for recruitment, INI-CET, INI-SS and the 
Committee hopes that measures so undertaken would yield outcome in a time-frame 
manner. The Committee urges upon the Ministry to implement comprehensive 
strategies for the recruitment and retention of faculty and staff in new AIIMS, 
particularly in remote locations. The Department should offer attractive compensation 
packages and incentives to attract and retain qualified personnel; developing robust 
mentoring programs; addressing the challenges of creating adequate living conditions 
and infrastructure in remote areas. The Committee is of the considered view that 
shortages of faculty and staff, especially in remote AIIMS, certainly impede the delivery 
of quality healthcare and medical education, therefore, addressing these human 
resource challenges is crucial for imbibing professional pursuits in new AIIMS for the 
successful functioning of these institutions. 
 
3.6.18 The Committee recommends that the Ministry further strengthen its 
monitoring and coordination mechanisms for PMSSY projects, including both AIIMS 
and GMC upgrades. This should involve enhancing the effectiveness of Project Review 
Committees and utilizing the PRAGATI portal for timely issue resolution; conducting 
regular site visits by senior ministry officials to monitor progress and address 
challenges; improving coordination with state governments and executing agencies to 
ensure seamless implementation. The Committee considers that effective monitoring and 
coordination are essential for ensuring the timely completion of PMSSY projects and the 
efficient utilization of funds. Strengthening these mechanisms will help mitigate delays 
and improve overall project outcomes. 

3.6.19 The Department presented the status of the PMSSY which aims to upgrade Government Medical Colleges/Institutions (GMCIs). Out of 75 approved GMCIs, 70 have been completed. However, 5 remain either partially completed or have not started at all. Specifically, PMCH Patna (91% complete), GMC Surat (73.5% complete), and STCIMST Kerala (99.9% complete, scheduled for inauguration) are in advanced stages. However, UCMSOdisha have not commenced uprespectively. The Committee strongly recommends that the Department of Health and Family Welfare, in collaboration with the respective state/UT governments, establish a strict, time-bound action plan with cleathe completion of the remaining five GMCIs under the PMSSY. 3.7 NATIONAL AIDS AND STD CONTROL PROGRAMME

management; Care, support and treatment for HIV-infected persons; Viral load monitoring for

3.7.3  While the National AIDS and STD Control Programme (NACP) program 
has allocated funds to the tune of Rs. 2858 crore in RE 2024-25, the actual expenditure 
in 2024-25 upto 4th February, 2025 is Rs. 2256.47 crore, just 79% of the revised estimate 
(RE). The reduction is especially remarkable for "Supplies & Materials", indicating 
potential inefficiencies or delays in procurement and distribution. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends conducting a detailed analysis of the "Supplies & Materials" 
expenditure to identify and address bottlenecks in procurement and distribution. The 
Committee, in nutshell, recommends the Department for optimal utilization of funds 
under NACP. 
 
3.7.4  Government is currently implementing Phase-V of the NACP through three-
pronged strategy of prevention of new infections, early detection of HIV infections, and 

46 
 
initiation and retention on treatment to prevent spread of AIDS and STDs in the country. The 
key strategies/actions adopted by the Government to prevent outspread of AIDS and STDs in 
the country have been informed as under: - 
 
i. Covering of more than 1.25 crore high-risk and bridge population through 1590 
targeted interventions and 164 link worker schemes,  
ii. Management of more than one crore of persons suffering from STI/RTI through 
1127 designated STI/RTI centres,  
iii. Doing more than 6 crore HIV tests annually through 4657 confirmatory HIV 
counselling and testing centres,  
iv. Providing free, high quality, life long anti-retroviral treatment to around 1.25 
lakh HIV-infected persons every month through 794 anti-retroviral treatment 
centers, and  
v. Undertaken around 13.78 lakh viral load tests among HIV-infected persons in 
2023-24 with 97% having suppressed viral load.  
vi. Integrated STI, HIV, TB & Hepatitis (ISHTH) Campaign among inmates of 
prison and other closed settings covering 4.14 lakh inmates identifying around 
2100 HIV-positive inmates, 
vii. Integrated Health Campaigns in 73 districts (till November 2024) covering 
around 1.02 lakh persons and identifying 1650 HIV positive persons,  
viii. Index testing campaign eliciting around 17.81 lakh partners and biological 
children of HIV-positive persons and identifying 22074 HIV-positive among 
them, 
ix. Intensified IEC campaign reaching out to around 2.20 crore individuals in 1.24 
lakh villgaes, 8794 slums, 14594 colleges and 51,446 schools.  
 
3.7.5  These efforts have led to a 44% reduction in new HIV infections (2010-2023), 
79% decline in AIDS-related deaths, and improved case detection and treatment access (88% 
People living with HIV are on treatment).  
 
3.7.6  The Committee has been apprised of the following activities that can be 
undertaken to achieve the objectives of the programme: 
 
i. State-specific intensive awareness generation campaigns to improve the 
knowledge about HIV/AIDS focusing on demand generation for update of 
services, 
ii. Undertaking of integrated health campaigns among high-risk groups and bridge 
population in select districts for early identification of HIV-positive population, 
iii. Institutionalize index testing campaigns for partners and biological children of 
HIV positive persons, 
iv. Lost-to-follow up tracking campaigns for HIV-infected persons to ensure their 
continuity on treatment, and  
v. Increasing the uptake of IT-enabled SOCH MIS system of NACP to improve 
tracking of beneficiaries and services offered to them. 

47 
 
vi. Completion of round 2 of programmatic mapping and population size 
estimation to guide evidence-based programme designing for high-risk group 
populations. 
 
3.7.7  The NACP program highlights the importance of integrated health 
campaigns among high-risk and bridge populations for early HIV identification. The 
Committee recommends the Department to increase the frequency and scope of 
integrated health campaigns in select high-risk districts, focusing on early HIV detection 
and STI/RTI management; the strengthening of link worker schemes by providing 
enhanced training and resources, ensuring they can effectively reach and engage with 
high-risk populations; increase in the usage of the SOCH MIS system to better track the 
beneficiaries and services offered to them; and to complete and utilize the data from the 
round 2 of programmatic mapping and population size estimation to refine targeted 
interventions. 
 
3.7.8  The Committee highlights that NACP emphasizes index testing campaigns 
for partners and biological children of HIV-positive persons and Lost-to-follow-up 
tracking campaigns are crucial to ensure continuity of treatment. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends the Department for institutionalizing and expanding index 
testing campaigns, providing incentives and support to HIV-positive individuals to 
encourage partner notification. A robust, technology-driven lost-to-follow-up tracking 
system, utilizing mobile technology and community health workers to reconnect 
individuals with treatment services should be implemented to achieve the objective of 
the Programme.   
 
3.7.9   The Committee takes into consideration the Intensify and Tailor IEC 
Campaigns and Community Engagement to Reduce Stigma and Discrimination and 
Increase Service Uptake. NACP recognizes the importance of 360-degree multimedia 
campaigns and community outreach with reducing stigma and discrimination as a key 
objective. The Committee, therefore, recommends developing culturally sensitive IEC 
campaigns that address local stigma and misconceptions about HIV/AIDS; expanding 
community engagement through partnerships with local NGOs, community leaders, and 
faith-based organizations; and providing targeted training and sensitization programs 
for key stakeholders, including Self-Help Groups, Anganwadi workers, ASHAs, etc. to 
promote understanding and reduce stigma.

 The scheme provides for Swastha Nagrik Abhiyan (SNA), Population Centres, Health Surveys & Research Studies, procurement of Contraceptives for Social Marketing and Free Distribution, Training of Doctors in No Scalpal Vasectomy There are six components listed in the proposal under the 
BE 2025-26620.480.00620.48

50 
 
Family Welfare Schemes which are crucial for attaining the goals and objectives laid out in 
National Health Policy 2017, and international commitments in the form of Sustainable 
Development Goals.  
 
i. Swastha Nagrik Abhiyan (SNA): Aims to improve health-seeking behavior 
through awareness and service uptake. Health surveys and research, along with 
Population Research Centers, monitor progress and enable course correction. 
Social marketing and free contraceptives support population stabilization and 
maternal/child health.  
ii. PNDT Act Awareness: Focuses on enforcing the Pre-Conception and Pre-
Natal Diagnostic Techniques Act, 1994, to prevent sex selection and female 
foeticide.  
iii. Population Research Centers (PRCs): The Ministry of Health and Family 
Welfare, Government of India has established Population Research Centers 
(PRCs) in various Universities and research Institutions in the Country. 
Objective of the PRCs are critical research-based input to the Health and 
Family Welfare programmes and policies of the Government of India and 
states. 18 centers (funded by MoHFW) conduct research on family planning, 
demographics, and population control, supporting government health policies 
and surveys like NFHS and LASI.  
iv. Health Surveys (NFHS): The National Family Health Survey (NFHS), 
conducted with IIPS, provides data on population, health, and family welfare, 
enabling policy evaluation and benchmarking.  
v. Contraceptive Distribution: Provides free contraceptives (condoms, pills, 
IUCDs, etc.) to achieve population stabilization and reduce maternal/child 
mortality.  
vi. WHO-NPS Support: WHO-supported National Polio Surveillance Project 
provides technical and operational support for vaccine-preventable disease 
surveillance, immunization, and other health initiatives.
