3.9.3 The Committee has been informed that the main reason for projecting less 
amount under BE 2025-26 over BE 2024-25  is less allocation  of funds under WHO 
supported National Polio Surveillance Project (NPS) due to non  finalization of 
continuation of this component by Expenditure Finance Committee (EFC) at time of 
finalization of BE 2025-26. Now, this programme has now been approved in SFC, the 
additional funds shall be sought at the time of finalization of RE 2025-26. The 
fluctuation of the WHO-NPS project budget due to the EFC process, shows the need for 
better long term financial planning. The Committee is of the view that the Government 
should prefer to implement a long term financial planning system, that reduces the 
impact of delayed approval processes on program funding. 
3.9.4 The Committee observes that, for the FY 2024-25, BE was Rs. 694.72 
crore which was revised to Rs. 619.37 crore at RE stage and the total amount released 
till 31.01.2025 is Rs.   360.56 crore which is 58.21 % of RE. The budget utilization trends 
reveal discrepancies between approved budgets and actual spending, indicating 
potential inefficiencies or shortfall in fund allocation. The Committee recommends the 
Department to strengthen data-driven program management facilitating real-time 
monitoring of key performance indicators, enabling timely identification of program 
gaps and areas requiring intervention. The Department should utilize this data to 

52 
 
conduct regular, evidence-based reviews of program effectiveness and resource 
allocation, ensuring that funding is aligned with program priorities and achieving 
desired outcomes. Further, the department should create a system that tracks the 
percentage of program goals achieved, versus the percentage of budget spent ensuring 
effective and optimal/rational utilization of funds. 
 
3.9.5  The Committee notes that the success of the Family Welfare Scheme 
depends on the synergistic interaction between its various components (SNA, 
Contraceptive Distribution, PNDT Act Awareness, etc.). Further, the general public 
awareness of the programs is vital to the programs’ success. The Committee 
recommends the Department to develop a comprehensive communication strategy that 
integrates messaging across all components of the Family Welfare Scheme. The 
Department should leverage a variety of communication channels, including digital 
media and community outreach programs to reach diverse populations focusing on 
raising public awareness about the availability of services, promoting healthy behaviors, 
and addressing social and cultural barriers to accessing care. The Committee believes 
that the co-ordination and collaboration among various programs should be further 
enhanced to increase their effectiveness due to co-location of activities, and caution the 
Department that the programs do not progress in silos. 
 
 
3.10 ESTABLISHMENT AND STRENGTHENING OF NCDC BRANCHES AND 
HEALTH INITIATIVES, INTER SECTORAL CO-ORDINATION FOR 
PREPARATION AND CONTROL OF ZOONOTIC DISEASES AND OTHER 
NEGLECTED TROPICAL DISEASES, SURVEILLANCE OF VIRAL HEPATITIS, 
ANTI MICROBIAL RESISTANCE 
3.10.1  The scheme is envisaged as a centre-par-excellence to give impetus to the 
advancement of knowledge in the field of prevention and control of communicable/infectious 
diseases of public health importance and strengthening of other Health Initiatives. 
3.10.2  Under National Programme on Anti-Microbial Resistance (AMR) 
Containment, the physical achievements during the year 2024-24 are as under: 
i. Strengthened 50 state medical college microbiology labs across 33 states/UTs for 
AMR detection; 10 additional labs included for 2024-25. 
ii. Trained all network sites in bacteriology lab procedures to ensure quality-assured 
testing and the development of facility-based antibiograms for evidence-based 
antibiotic treatment. 
iii. Established a National Reference Lab for AMR surveillance in bacterial 
pathogens at NCDC HQ and strengthened the National Reference Lab for AMR 
surveillance in fungal pathogens at Vallabhbhai Patel Chest Institute (VPCI), 
Delhi University. These labs conduct alert confirmations of bacterial pathogens 
and EQAS isolate testing from network sites. 

53 
 
iv. Collected and uploaded National AMR surveillance data to the Global AMR 
Surveillance System (GLASS). 
v. Provided support to three states to strengthen State AMR Surveillance Networks 
and to six additional states for establishing new networks to generate annual AMR 
surveillance reports for evidence-based containment strategies. 
vi. Trained 40 network sites to conduct surveillance of Healthcare-Associated 
Infections (HAI). 
vii. Strengthened 33 state medical colleges under the National Antimicrobial 
Consumption Surveillance Network (NACNET) in a phased manner. 
 
The Activities proposed for 2025-26 under the programme: 
 
i. Strengthen 60 existing and include 10 additional state medical colleges for AMR 
surveillance. 
ii. Support two National Referral Labs for antifungal and antibacterial resistance. 
iii. Assist 15 states in establishing and strengthening State AMR Surveillance Lab 
Networks. 
iv. Train 10 additional sites for HAI surveillance, adding to the 40 already trained. 
v. Strengthen 33 existing and 10 additional sites in a phased manner for 
antimicrobial consumption surveillance. 
 
3.10.3  The Center for One Health, NCDC, is implementing three National Health 
Programs through the National Health Mission (NHM) across states and districts: 
i. National Rabies Control Programme (NRCP) 
ii. Programme for Prevention and Control of Leptospirosis (PPCL) 
iii. National Programme for Prevention & Control of Snakebite Envenoming (NPSE) 
and National One Health Programme for Prevention and Control of Zoonoses 
(NOHP-PCZ) under Umbrella Scheme D of NCDC. 
3.10.4   The following physical achievements and activities proposed to be undertaken 
under the above three programs have been provided by the Department: - 
A. National Rabies Control Programme (NRCP) 
i. Rabies has been made a notifiable disease in 29 states. 
ii. Regional workshops for developing SAPRE were conducted for North region 
states (Himachal Pradesh, Punjab, Ladakh, Chandigarh, Jammu & Kashmir, 
Haryana) and North Eastern region (Meghalaya, Mizoram, Assam, Manipur, 
Nagaland, Arunachal Pradesh, Tripura, Sikkim). 
iii. SAPRE was launched for Meghalaya, Mizoram, and Tamil Nadu, along with 
the "Operational Guideline for Rabies Free City Initiative." 
iv. A network of 11 Rabies Diagnostic Labs has been strengthened. 
v. A dedicated NRCP website and a pilot Rabies helpline were launched. 

54 
 
vi. Regular multidisciplinary training for health, veterinary, and wildlife 
professionals is conducted. 
 
Proposed Activities for Next Year: 
i. Strengthening five laboratories for rabies diagnosis in both animals and 
humans. 
ii. Conducting regional workshops for Western and Central states for the 
preparation of the State Action Plan for Dog-Mediated Rabies Elimination. 
iii. Expanding the Rabies Helpline to all states and UTs. 
iv. Conducting training on laboratory diagnosis of rabies, animal bite 
management, and rabies surveillance. 
v. Preparing and circulating awareness materials on rabies prevention and control. 
 
B. Programme for Prevention and Control of Leptospirosis (PPCL) 
 
i. A network of five laboratories for leptospirosis diagnosis has been 
strengthened. 
ii. Funds provided to 12 states and two UTs under PPCL through the NHM PIP 
mechanism. 
iii. MoH&FW issued advisories on leptospirosis to 14 states during every 
monsoon season. 
iv. Two hands-on training sessions for laboratory professionals were conducted at 
NIVEDI Bangalore and RMRC Port Blair. 
Proposed Activities for Next Year: 
i. Strengthening four laboratories for leptospirosis diagnosis in both animals and 
humans. 
ii. Conducting training and workshops on leptospirosis diagnosis, case 
management, and prevention & control. 
 
C. National Programme for Prevention & Control of Snakebite Envenoming 
(NPSE) 
 
i. Funds provided for procurement of Anti-Snake Venom (ASV) under the 
National Free Drug Initiative through NHM. 
ii. Launched the "National Action Plan for Prevention and Control of Snakebite 
Envenoming in India (NAPSE)." 
iii. Developed IEC material for snakebite prevention and control. 
iv. 25 states have identified and appointed nodal officers for snakebite prevention 
and control activities. 
v. Developed and disseminated protocols on the initial management of snakebite 
cases in CHC/PHC. 
vi. Implemented notification of snakebite cases and deaths. 
vii. Launched a snakebite helpline pilot project in five states. 

55 
 
viii. Conducted training for medical officers on snakebite management in 19 states. 
 
Proposed Activities for Next Year: 
i. Establishing Poison Information Centers (PICs) for snakebite management in 
medical colleges, tertiary care hospitals, and private hospitals. 
ii. Strengthening institutions involved in snakebite management and anti-snake 
venom production as Regional Venom Centers. 
iii. Expanding the snakebite helpline across the country. 
iv. Developing a standardized training module for medical officers and frontline 
workers. 
v. Establishing a Programme Management Unit (PMU) at NCDC. 
 
D. National One Health Programme for Prevention & Control of Zoonoses (NOHP-
PCZ) 
i. Established and strengthened a network of 11 Regional Coordinators of One 
Health and four AIIMS institutes as Centers of Excellence. 
ii. Strengthened the Wildlife Institute of India (WII), Dehradun, as a Regional 
Coordinator under the program. 
iii. Integrated NOHP-PCZ into the Integrated Health Information Platform (IHIP). 
iv. Developed "Technical Guidance on Zoonotic Disease Prevention, Preparedness 
& Response Using One Health Approach." 
v. Conducted multi-stakeholder consultations for developing the State One Health 
Action Plan in Rajasthan and Odisha. 
vi. Conducted a National Consultation for Legal Environment Assessment for One 
Health Activities in India. 
vii. Identified 16 institutes as Regional Coordinators for laboratory testing and 
community awareness (IEC) on zoonotic diseases. 
viii. Conducted over 50 capacity-building and hands-on training sessions, training 
more than 1,000 participants in the last four years. 
ix. Established 75 Sentinel Surveillance Sites for laboratory testing and training on 
zoonotic diseases. 
x. Formed State-Level Zoonotic Committees in 34 states/UTs and District-Level 
Zoonotic Committees in 373 districts. 
Proposed Activities for Next Year: 
i. Strengthening four additional institutes/AIIMS as Regional 
Coordinators/Centers of Excellence for laboratory testing, capacity building, 
and community awareness (IEC) on zoonotic diseases. 
ii. Conducting five sensitization workshops and ten hands-on laboratory training 
sessions. 
iii. Launching and implementing guidelines for the Integrated Community 
Outreach component. 

56 
 
iv. Developing and implementing the Augmented Zoonotic Disease Surveillance 
(AZDS) proposal for strengthening zoonotic pathogen surveillance in hotspot 
areas. 
3.10.5  The Committee recommends a sustained and expanded focus on capacity 
building across all programs, with a particular emphasis on AMR containment and 
zoonotic disease management. Leveraging the existing network of laboratories, training 
sites, and regional coordinators, the program should prioritize consistent, high-quality 
training delivery to strengthen expertise and ensure standardized practices. For AMR 
containment, the Committee recommends the expansion of laboratory networks must be 
accompanied by rigorous, standardized training in bacteriology and HAI surveillance, 
ensuring quality-assured testing and evidence-based treatment. Similarly, for zoonotic 
disease management, the successful approach of multi-stakeholder consultations and 
hands-on training should be scaled up, with a strong focus on developing and 
implementing standardized training modules for medical officers and frontline workers. 
Additionally, the expansion of Regional Venom Centers and the establishment of Poison 
Information Centers for snakebite management should be supported through 
comprehensive training programs. The Committee recommends for wider dissemination 
of IEC material for snakebite prevention and control, and protocols on initial 
management of snakebite especially in remote/rural areas. The Committee also 
recommends for launching snakebite Helpline Project and training for medical officers 
on snakebite in all States/UTs. The Committee is of the opinion that a continuous 
investment in human resource development will be the key to ensure long-term success 
and sustainability of these critical public health initiatives. 
3.10.6  The Committee recommends strengthening surveillance systems and data 
integration to enhance proactive disease management. Building on the AMR 
surveillance network and NOHP-PCZ integration into IHIP, programs should 
implement robust surveillance protocols and data management systems. For AMR, data 
collection and reporting to Global Antimicrobial Resistance and Use Surveillance 
System (GLASS) should be complemented by facility-based antibiograms to support 
evidence-based antibiotic treatment. For zoonotic diseases, implementing the 
Augmented Zoonotic Disease Surveillance (AZDS) proposal is critical for strengthening 
pathogen surveillance in hotspot areas. Additionally, the Rabies Helpline expansion and 
snakebite case notifications should be integrated into a unified national surveillance 
platform for timely data analysis and informed decision-making. The Committee is of 
the view that establishing a Programme Management Unit (PMU) at NCDC will ensure 
coordinated surveillance, data quality, and rapid information dissemination.

3.10.8  The Committee notes that for construction of NCDC branches across the 
country, the land has not yet been identified in the State of Chhattisgarh. Further, the 
construction has been started in eight States only. The Committee recommends that the 
NCDC prioritize and expedite the establishment of fully functional branch 
infrastructure across all states, particularly focusing on Chhattisgarh where land 
identification is still pending and accelerating construction in the remaining states where 
it has not yet commenced. This is crucial for maintaining the continuity and 
effectiveness of the NCDC's national disease surveillance and response capabilities, 
ensuring timely implementation of the Integrated Disease Surveillance Programme 
(IDSP) and the Integrated Health Information Platform (IHIP), and strengthening the 
national public health framework. 
 
3.10.9  INTEGRATED DISEASE SURVEILLANCE PROGRAMME (IDSP) - The 
Integrated Disease Surveillance Programme (IDSP), under the National Health Mission 
(NHM), aims to establish and maintain a decentralized, laboratory-based, IT-enabled disease 
surveillance system for epidemic-prone communicable diseases. Currently, IDSP monitors 
33+ syndromes/diseases, with special surveillance for ILI/SARI, COVID-19, and Mpox. 
IDSP operates through a multi-tiered surveillance network, comprising Central Surveillance 
Unit (CSU) at NCDC Delhi; State Surveillance Units (SSU) at all State/UT headquarters; and 
District Surveillance Units (DSU) at all districts. Rapid Response Teams (RRTs) at district, 
state, and central levels investigate outbreak-prone diseases and recommend necessary 
precautions to prevent disease spread. Designated laboratories, including District Public 
Health Laboratories (DPHLs) and State Referral Laboratories (SRLs), support disease 
surveillance and investigation. 
 
3.10.10 IDSP has fully transitioned to a near real-time, GIS-enabled Integrated Health 
Information Platform (IHIP). As of December 2024, all 36 States/UTs report data through 
IHIP. Key Functions under IDSP are: - 
 

59 
 
i. Outbreak Investigations: Rapid response to disease outbreaks across districts, 
states, and the country. 
ii. Media Scanning & Verification: Monitors print and electronic media for event-
based surveillance, utilizing AI tools for early warning detection and timely 
outbreak response. 
iii. Health Preparedness: Enables early detection and quick response by state health 
authorities. 
 
3.10.11 Outcomes under IDSP have been informed as follows: - 
 
i. IHIP Implementation- On an average 76%, 79% and 80% Reporting Units have 
reported in S (Syndromic) form, P (Presumptive) form and L (Laboratory 
Confirmed) form respectively under IDSP-IHIP Portal (1st Jan 2024 to 31st 
December 2024). 
 
ii. Outbreak Investigations- A total of 2947 outbreaks have been reported during the 
period 1st Jan 2024 to 15th December 2024 (Week 50).  
 
iii. Public Health Emergency Operation Centre (PHEOC)- at NCDC was activated on 
23rd April, 2024 in response to Multi-State H5N1 outbreak (Level 2), on 9th May’ 
2024 in response to Avian Influenza in Kerala & Jharkhand (Level 1), on 21st 
august’ 2024 in response to Mpox, on 28th August’ 2024 in response to Avian 
Influenza outbreak in Ajmer Rajasthan, and on 4th September’2024 in response to 
Avian Influenza outbreak in Odisha. 
 
iv. Media Scanning and Verification Cell- IDSP performs media scanning & 
verification of print and electronic media to strengthen the event-based 
surveillance. IDSP also uses Artificial Intelligence tools in Media Scanning and 
Verification to detect early warning signals and generating alerts for timely 
management of the potential outbreaks. A total of 2,338 media alerts were 
generated (1st January 2024 to 31st December 2024). 
 
v. Laboratory strengthening- Strengthening of public health labs is an important 
component of IDSP that focuses on developing lab capacity in India. As on 
December’ 2024, 439 district public Health labs in 36 States/ UTs have been 
approved for strengthening out of which 382 (87%) labs are performing as per 
tests recommended by IDSP. State Referral labs (SRLs) network is currently 
present in 27 States/UTs involving 163 sanctioned SRLs. 
 
3.10.12 To ensure the sustained effectiveness and responsiveness of the Integrated 
Disease Surveillance Programme (IDSP), the Committee recommends a targeted 
initiative to enhance data collection and analysis to arrive at a decision in specific time-
frame in the Integrated Health Information Platform (IHIP). The Committee believes 
that it can be achieved through: (i) intensified training and capacity building for 
healthcare workers at all levels on IHIP data entry & analytics and utilization; (ii) 

60 
 
streamlined data entry processes and user interface improvements to minimize 
reporting burdens; (iii) regular monitoring and feedback mechanisms to identify and 
rectify reporting challenges; and (iv) leveraging the recently launched community 
reporting tool to supplement and validate data from health facilities, thereby fostering a 
more comprehensive and robust surveillance system. 
 
3.10.13 NATIONAL PROGRAM FOR SURVEILLANCE OF VIRAL HEPATITIS 
(NPSVH): The National Program for Surveillance of Viral Hepatitis (NPSVH) helps in 
determining the seroprevalence of Hepatitis B and Hepatitis C and this helps in estimating the 
burden of infection. This data is used for planning, policy, prevention and control of Viral 
Hepatitis through National Viral Hepatitis Control Program (NVHCP) to achieve SDG target 
3.3. 
 
3.10.14 The Committee recommends building on existing collaborations with 
NACP and NFHS, it should implement a standardized framework for data collection 
and scientific analysis of data, integrating seroprevalence data across multiple disease 
areas to ensure continuity and clarity between surveillance of data and programmatic 
action. This expansion will optimize laboratory infrastructure, generate a 
comprehensive dataset essential for informed policymaking, targeted interventions, and 
effective disease control. 
 
3.11 NATIONAL PHARMACOVIGILANCE PROGRAMME 
 
3.11.1  National Pharmacovigilance Program of India (PvPI) is a Central Sector (CS) 
Scheme being implemented in the country with the objective to collect, collate and analyse 
the Adverse Events reported with the use of medical products marketed in India to safeguard 
the health of Indian population by ensuring that the benefits of use of medicine outweigh the 
risks associated with its use. The key objectives of PvPI are to create a nationwide system for 
patient safety reporting in respect to adverse drug reactions (ADRs) of medical products, to 
identify and analyze the new ADR (Signal) from the reported cases and to analyze the 
benefit-risk ratio of medicinal products.  
 
3.11.2  The PvPI collaborates with national and international stakeholders, ensuring 
patients’ safety by monitoring ADRs. A nationwide network has been set up with Healthcare 
Professionals (HCPs) by enrolling Adverse Drug Reaction Monitoring Centres (AMCs) in 
PvPI and the current outreach is more than 842 AMCs across the country. The National 
Coordination Centre for PvPI has enrolled 1,025 Adverse Drug Reaction Monitoring Centres 
(AMCs) across the country so far. There are 9,22,005 lacs of Individual Case Safety Reports 
(ICSRs) as on 7.2.2025 in VigiBase on behalf of India. PvPI is the 8th largest reporter of 
ICSRs to the WHO Programme of International Drug Monitoring. The Materiovigilance 
Programme of India (MvPI) and Hemovigilance Programme of India (HvPI) are also under 
the ambit of PvPI. IPC is also functioning as a NCC for MvPI and National Institute of 
Biologicals (NIB) is a NCC for HvPI. 
 
3.11.3  Out of Rs. 8.53 crore allocated for FY 2024-25, Rs. 6.40 cr has been released 
out of which Rs. 5.80 cr has been utilized during the year 2024-25. For the FY 2025-26, the 
programme has been allocated Rs. 8.53 crore at BE stage. Physical Progress PvPI for 
FY2023- to FY 2024-25 is given below:

3.11.4  The Committee has been apprised of the indigenously developed Adverse 
Drug Reaction Monitoring Software (ADRMS) of Pharmacovigilance Program of India 
(PvPI) launched on 19th August 2024. The Committee recommends a strategic focus on 
improving the functionality and accessibility of this indigenously developed ADRM 
software to strengthen the efficacy and safety database for new drugs. The key measures 
in this direction should include implementing rigorous data validation and quality 
control protocols; expanding targeted training programs and developing user-friendly 
interfaces and advanced data analytics tools within ADRMS to enable timely signal 
detection and benefit-risk analysis, reinforcing the program’s leadership in international 
drug monitoring and safeguarding public health. The whole purpose is to create 
effective nation-wide system for patient safety reporting in respect to adverse drug 
reactions (ADRs) of medical products.  
 
3.12 DEVELOPMENT OF NURSING SERVICES 
 
3.12.1  In order to improve the quality of nursing education and Services, the 
Government of India funds a Central Sector Scheme of Development of Nursing Services 
with 100% funding pattern, for implementation in the States. The Scheme envisages the 
training of Nurses in the field of specialization, in-service training, infrastructure development 
by way of strengthening of Schools and upgrading of schools of nursing to Colleges of 
Nursing. The Government approves the proposals received from the State governments for 
upgradation as and when received from the State government. Further, as per the Scheme 
guidelines, funds are released to State Government, State Nursing Council, Central 
Government institutions, State Government institutions, etc for training of Nurses.  
 
3.12.2  Under a separate Scheme for Establishment of new Nursing Colleges, the 
Cabinet has approved establishment of 157 new nursing colleges in co-location with the 
existing medical colleges. The initiative aims to address the geographical and rural-urban 

62 
 
imbalances in the healthcare sector. The establishment of these nursing colleges will provide a 
significant boost to the availability of qualified human resources in healthcare.  
 
3.12.3  The Scheme was allocated Rs. 22 crore in 2024-25 at both BE and RE stage. 
An expenditure of Rs. 14.77 crore has been incurred by the end of January 2025. In FY 2025-
26, Rs. 28.74 crore has been allocated at BE stage.  
 
3.12.4  The training of Nursing Personnel in identified domains/topics is being done as 
a part of Continuing Nursing Education (CNE) Regulations, 2019. Renewal of 
registration/license every five years is linked with 150 hours of CNE for all categories of 
nurses to update the knowledge and skills of nursing personnel under the scheme of DNS. 
 Under the component ‘Training of Nurses’ of the Central Sector Scheme “Development of 
Nursing Services”, Central Government provides funds for training of nurses in a batch of 30 
nurses per course of 7 days which adds to their CNE credits. As per information received 
from Indian Nursing Council, details of the nursing colleges/institutes are functional in India 
is at Table below. Further,

3.12.5  As informed by Indian Nursing Council (INC), there are 38.49 lakh nursing 
personnel registered in the country. Assuming 80% nursing personnel are active in the 
country, this gives a nurse population ratio of about 2:06/00. Initiatives/Steps taken by the 
Government to increase nursing manpower in the country are as follows: 
 
i. The requirement of land for School/College of Nursing and Hostel has been 
relaxed. 
ii. Relaxation in the norms of having a 100 bedded parent hospital for hilly and tribal 
areas has been done. 
iii. The student teacher ratio for M.Sc.(N) programme has been relaxed from 1:5 to 
1:10. 
iv. Student patient ratio for Nursing Institutions has been relaxed from 1:5 to 1:3. 
v. Maximum number of 100 seats for Nursing College to be given to parent hospitals 
with 300 beds without insisting for a Medical College. 
vi. Distance from school/college to hospital has been relaxed. 
vii. Eligibility criteria to admission for Diploma and Degree has been relaxed. 
viii. Relaxation in norms has been done for opening M.Sc. (N) programme by allowing 
Super Speciality Hospital to start M.Sc. (N) without having under graduate 
programme.  

63 
 
3.12.6  The Committee believes that, to achieve sustained improvements in 
nursing services and rectify geographical disparities, the Government need to 
implement a comprehensive, data-driven strategy for the targeted expansion and 
enhancement of nursing education and training with a particular focus on underserved 
regions. The Committee urges for integrated implementation of the existing Central 
Sector Scheme for the Development of Nursing Services, the establishment of 157 new 
nursing colleges in co-location with existing medical colleges, and the Continuing 
Nursing Education (CNE) framework. The Committee further recommends that the 
scheme must emphasize the training of nurses in the field of specialization, 
frequent/periodic in-service training, infrastructure development by way of 
strengthening of Schools and upgrading of Schools of Nursing to Colleges of Nursing. 
 
3.12.7  The Committee is of the opinion that Government should actively enforce 
CNE regulations with rigorous monitoring of training outcomes to ensure that all nurses 
maintain their competency through mandatory training. The approval and funding 
processes for state government proposals should be streamlined, expediting 
infrastructure development and training initiatives. These measures will directly 
address the shortage of qualified nursing personnel and enhance healthcare delivery 
across India. Additionally, the Committee gives emphasis to the efficient utilization of 
allocated funds and the strategic distribution of resources based on state-wise data on 
nursing institutions and admission capacities. 
 
3.13 HEALTH SECTOR DISASTER PREPAREDNESS AND RESPONSE AND 
HUMAN RESOURCE DEVELOPMENT FOR EMERGENCY MEDICAL SERVICES 
 
3.13.1  The scheme (Health Sector Disaster Preparedness and Response and Human 
Resource Development for Emergency Medical Services) is being implemented by the 
Disaster Management Cell, under Ministry of Health & Family Welfare. Its objectives are in 
line with National Health Policy 2017, National Disaster Management Plan 2019 and other 
national policies and guidelines. The major objective of the scheme includes: 
 
a) Infrastructure development which includes: 
i. Setting up of National Emergency Life Support (NELS) Skill Centers in 
medical colleges/institutions under Central/State Governments 
ii. Setting up of secondary and tertiary level Chemical, Biological, Radiological 
and Nuclear (CBRN) medical management Centers 
 
b) Capacity building initiatives which includes a wide array of pan-India skill-based 
hands on trainings, including the following: 
i. Trainings of doctors, nurses and paramedics on National Emergency Life 
Support (NELS) 
ii. Hospital disaster preparedness and response for hospital administrators 
iii. Management of public health emergencies for district level health officers 

64 
 
iv. Psycho-social care in disaster settings for psychiatrists, psychologists and 
psycho-social workers 
v. Medical management of radio-nuclear emergencies for medical officers 
 
3.13.2  In line with the above-mentioned objectives, the achievements of the scheme, 
especially since the COVID-19 pandemic have been informed as detailed below: 
 
i. Finalization of training modules on hospital disaster preparedness and 
management of public health emergencies, inculcating learnings from COVID-
19 management. 
ii. Review of existing course modules on psycho-social care and on medical 
management of radio-nuclear emergencies, inculcating learnings from 
COVID-19 management. 
iii. To minimize the reliance of foreign, payment based professional courses on 
emergency life support, specialized, indigenously developed skill-based 
courses on NELS course modules for trainings of doctors, nurses and 
paramedics, have been reviewed and updated, while inculcating learnings from 
COVID-19 management. 
iv. Identification and signing of MoUs with partner training institutes and roll-out 
of pan-India trainings 
v. Identification, inspections and signing of MoUs with medical colleges, for 
setting up of NELS skill centers. 
vi. Pre-project activities have also been initiated for establishment of tertiary level 
CBRN medical management centers at Chengalpattu Medical College, 
Chengalpattu, Tamil Nadu and ICMR’s Bhopal Memorial Hospital and 
Research Centre, Madhya Pradesh. 
 
3.13.3  Under the scheme an amount to the tune of Rs. 94 crore (Rs. 45.17 crore under 
Revenue head and Rs. 48.83 crore under Capital head) has been allocated in BE 2025-26. On 
a specific query, the component-wise expenditure profile against Budget Estimate (BE) 2023-
24 and 2024-25 for the scheme has been provided as under:

3.13.4  On being asked about the reasons for significant reduction in allocation under 
the scheme at the RE stage compared to the BE stage in FY 2024-25, the Department 
explained that a major portion of the scheme’s budget is allocated for infrastructure projects, 
such as establishing NELS Skill Centers and tertiary and secondary-level CBRN medical 
management centers. The reduction in funds at the RE stage for 2024-25 is attributed to the 
following factors: 
 
a) Delays in site identification and changes in location: The identification of 
suitable land/space for tertiary and secondary-level CBRN medical management 
centers faced delays, compounded by frequent site changes. This hindered site 
finalization and the selection of an execution agency, reducing capital outlay. 
 
b) Delayed fund utilization by grantee medical colleges: Funds meant for NELS 
Skill Centers remained pooled at the Central Nodal Agency (CNA) due to slow 
utilization by grantee medical colleges. This restricted further disbursement to newly 
identified institutes for setting up NELS Skill Centers. 
 
c) Unutilized funds earmarked for Health Sector Disaster Preparedness & 
Response: A significant portion of the budget was reserved for immediate logistics in 
response to disasters and public health emergencies. However, as of 2024-25, no such 
requests were received from any State/UT, leading to savings in these funds. 
 
3.13.5  The Department noted that site-related issues are being actively addressed in 
coordination with States/UTs, alongside the finalization of the Project Consultant. As a result, 
the allocated funds are expected to be utilized as planned in BE 2025-26. 
 
3.13.6  The Committee noted the under-utilisation of funds under Health Sector 
Disaster Preparedness and Response and Human Resource Development for Emergency 
Medical Services during the past two years. In the FY 2023-24, Rs. 128.14 crore was 
allocated at BE stage which was reduced to Rs. 70 crore at RE stage. Moreover, the 
actual expenditure was just Rs. 30.50 crore (43.57% of the already reduced revised 

66 
 
estimate). Similarly, in the next FY 2024-25, the amount of Rs. 94 crore allocated at BE 
stage was reduced to Rs. 15 crore at RE stage, and again, the actual expenditure has 
been reported to the tune of Rs. 7.57 crore as on 07.02.2025. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends the Government to implement a rigorous, time-bound monitoring and 
evaluation framework for the "Health Sector Disaster Preparedness and Response and 
Human Resource Development for Emergency Medical Services" scheme, specifically 
focusing on the timely execution of infrastructure projects and Capacity Building 
Initiatives that inter-alia include setting up of National Emergency Life Support (NELS) 
skill centers in medical colleges/institutions under Central/State Government and setting 
up of Chemical, Biological, Radiological and Nuclear (CBRN) medical management 
centers besides imparting skills training of doctors, nurses and paramedics on NELS 
and CBRN, and fund utilization by grantee institutions. 
 
3.13.7  For efficient and optimal utilization of funds under the scheme, the 
Committee believes that the Government should consider the following: (i) pre-
identified and vetted site alternatives to mitigate delays due to site changes; (ii) 
mandatory quarterly fund utilization reports from grantee medical colleges for NELS 
Skill Centers, with clear timelines for disbursement and penalties for non-compliance; 
and (iii) a proactive, needs-based assessment system for disaster and public health 
emergency response logistics, rather than solely relying on state/UT requests, to ensure 
allocated funds are strategically utilized. This systematic approach will address the 
recurrent issues of delayed project execution and underutilization of funds. 
 
3.14 NATIONAL ORGAN TRANSPLANT PROGRAM (NOTP) 
 
3.14.1  The basic objective of National Organ Transplant Programme (NOTP) is to 
promote Deceased Organ Donation across the country with a view to increase availability of 
organs for transplantation, to organize an efficient mechanism for organ and tissue 
procurement/retrieval especially from deceased donors and their distribution for 
transplantation, to establish new and strengthen the existing organ and tissue retrieval and 
transplant infrastructure facilities especially in public sector hospitals/institutions and to 
monitor organ and tissue transplant services and bring about policy and Programme 
corrections/changes whenever needed.  
 
3.14.2  On a specific query, the Department furnished the budgetary allocation along 
with actual expenditure under the Scheme in the past five years as under: - 
 
Table-3.20 
 
Year Revenue Capital Total 
BE RE Actual BE RE Actual BE RE Actual 
2019-20 38.9 27.7 11.42 2.1 1.25 1.08 41 28.98 12.50 
2020-21 31.65 6 4.81 1.35 0 0 33 6 4.81 
2021-22 29.15 6.39 5.88 1.35 0 0 30.5 6.39 5.88 
2022-23 13.43 9.98 9.02 1.57 0.26 0.09 15 10.24 9.11 
2023-24 14.33 10.66 7.12 1.35 0.34 0.33 15.68 11.00 7.46 
 

67 
 
3.14.3  The features and achievements under the scheme have been detailed as below: 
- 
 
i. Awareness activities to promote Organ and Tissue Donation by Deceased 
Donors: The Indian organ donation day is celebrated every year to promote organ 
and tissue donation. Besides that, many other activities are organized across the 
country by national/regional and state organ and tissue transplant organizations.  
 
ii. Website: A dedicated website (www.notto.mohfw.gov.in ) provides 
information on organ and tissue donation, organ and tissue pledging, networking 
of hospitals and also has National registry of organ and tissue transplantation. 
 
iii. Helpline/Call centre: A 24x7 days call centre has been made operational with 
provision of a toll-free helpline (No. 1800114770) since 1-7-2015. 
 
iv. National Registry: A National Registry, National Organ and Tissue Transplant 
Registry (NOTTR) being maintained by NOTTO centrally, has been established 
with more than 940 Hospitals linked with networking organizations. 
 
 
v. Establishments of Organization/Institutions for undertaking networking for Organ 
procurement and distribution and other measures: Under NOTP, one National 
Organ and Tissue Transplant Organization (NOTTO) at New Delhi, 5 Regional 
Organ and Tissue Transplant Organizations (ROTTOs) in 5 Tamil Nadu, 
Maharashtra, Assam, West Bengal and Chandigarh and 22 State Organ and 
Tissue Transplant Organizations (SOTTOs) have been established in the 
States/UTs of Andhra Pradesh, Arunachal Pradesh, Bihar, Chhattisgarh, Dadra 
and Nagar Haveli & Daman and Diu, Goa, Gujarat, Haryana, Himachal Pradesh, 
Jammu & Kashmir, Jharkhand, Karnataka, Kerala, Madhya Pradesh, Mizoram, 
Orissa, Puducherry, Punjab, Rajasthan, Telangana, Uttar Pradesh.  
 
vi. Financial assistance is also provided for post-transplant immune-suppressants to 
BPL transplant recipients at the rate of Rs.10,000/- per month for 
immunosuppressant therapy. Provision has been made for financial support for 
the maintenance of deceased donor. 
 
vii. Total number of transplants in the country has been 12259 in 2021 which 
increased to 18378 in 2023 (India is 3rd in the World). Organ donation Rate (No. 
of deceased donors per million population) in the country increased from 0.52 in 
2019 to 0.79 in 2023 (1099 donors in 1400 million population). 
 
viii. Besides a significant enhancement in capacities for undertaking relatively 
common transplants of Kidney, Liver and Heart, capacity for undertaking rare 
transplants like pancreas, intestine, hand, limbs, lung, uterus have also been 
developed within the country. 
 
 
3.14.4  Under the scheme, Rs. 15 crore has been allocated in BE 2025-26. On being 
asked about the reasons for reduction in funds from Rs. 15 crore at BE stage to Rs. 10 crore at 
RE stage in FY 2024-25,  the main reasons for reduction of funds have been stated as follows: 
- 

68 
 
 
i. No fresh GIA has been released in current FY, as the unspent balance which was 
originally sanctioned and released to States prior to CNA was submitted back to 
CNA A/c of NOTTO. The same is being re-assigned among other ROTTOs / 
SOTTOs for utilization. 
ii. States like Tamil Nadu, Telangana, Andhra Pradesh, Punjab, Rajasthan, Haryana, 
Karnataka did not utilize the funds sanctioned under the program sighting various 
reasons. 
iii. In many of the States manpower have not been hired under the program instead 
regular officers are designated for the same. 
iv. Regular meetings have been organized with the states for developing infrastructure 
for transplantation/retrieval facilities particularly in Public sector facilities/ 
Institutions.  Despite regular guidance only few states have sent their proposals. 
 
3.14.5  The Committee notes that the funds utilization trend under the National 
Organ Transplant Program (NOTP) show significant discrepancies between budgeted 
and actual expenditures, indicating poor fund utilization. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends the Government to establish a robust mechanism incorporating 
accountability and ensuring enhanced and effective fund utilization by ROTTOs and 
SOTTOs. The Department needs to address the root causes of underutilization, 
including administrative bottlenecks and lack of state-level capacity and overcome the 
reasons for under-utilization and also persuading the non-performing States for optimal 
utilization of allocated fund.   
3.14.6  The Committee, on examination of the submissions made by the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare, observes that many ROTTOs and SOTTOs 
are in their infancy stage, lacking infrastructure and operational efficiency. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends the strengthening of ROTTOs and SOTTOs by 
provide comprehensive capacity-building programs for ROTTOs and SOTTOs, 
focusing on website development, data management, budget utilization, and training 
program organization.  
3.15 NATIONAL TELE MENTAL HEALTH PROGRAMME 
 
3.15.1  The National Tele Mental Health Programme of India, Tele Mental Health 
Assistance and Networking Across States (Tele MANAS) was announced by the Union 
Finance Minister in the Union Budget 2022 on 1st February, 2022 and launched as the digital 
arm of District Mental Health Programme (DMHP) by the Governor of Karnataka at the event 
of the World Mental Health Day ceremony held at NIMHANS on 10th October, 2022. The 
objectives of the program are: - 
 
i. To exponentially scale up the reach of mental health services to anybody who 
reaches out, across India, any time, by setting up a 24x7 tele-mental health 
facility in each of the States and UTs of the country.  
 
ii. To implement a fully-fledged mental health service network that, in addition to 
counselling, provides integrated medical and psychosocial interventions 

69 
 
including video consultations with mental health specialists, e-prescriptions, 
follow-up services and linkages to in-person services.  
 
iii. To extend services to vulnerable groups of the population and difficult to reach 
populations. 
 
The features and achievements under the program are: - 
 
i. Presently, Tele MANAS is Operational in all 36 States/ UT’s through 51 
functional Tele MANAS cells including 2 additional cells at AFMC Pune and at 
Nagpur in collaboration with Aditya Birla Empower Initiative Group (CSR).  
 
ii. More than 18 lakhs calls have been handled on the helpline number (14416) 
since launch till 7th February, 2025.  
 
iii. Tele MANAS helpline is available for all age group while majority of the 
callers (more than 60%) engaging with the T MANAS helpline falls in the age 
group of 19- 45 years. 
 
iv. In the initial phase the numbers of Male callers (68%) were more in comparison 
to the female callers (32%). This gap has reduced to the ratio of 53 (Male 
Callers): 47 (Female callers) now throwing effectiveness of Tele MANAS to 
provide services in the vulnerable group. 
 
v. On October 10th, 2024, the Tele MANAS mobile application was launched on 
the second anniversary of the National Tele Mental Health Program (NTMHP). 
The app provides an invaluable resource for users seeking guidance, 
counselling, and emotional support. 
 
vi. Video consultations in Tele MANAS are another upgrade to the already 
existing audio calling facility. This facility has been initially launched in the 
states of Karnataka, Jammu & Kashmir and Tamil Nadu and will be later scaled 
up to the whole country. 
 
vii. The program also incorporates call-backs for follow-up, reinforcing the care 
plan for individuals. 
 
viii. Tele MANAS services are being actively promoted through various platforms, 
including print media, radio, and social media. These efforts aim to raise 
awareness about mental health services and ensure that people in need can 
easily access free and confidential psychological support. 
 
 
3.15.2  The scheme has been allocated an amount to the tune of Rs. 79.60 crore in 
the BE 2025-26. The expenditure profile under the scheme during the last three years has 
been informed as follows-: 
 
 
 
 
 

70 
 
Table-3.21 
Rs. (in Crores) 
FY BE RE Funds Released Expenditure (%) 
2022-23 - 120.62 65.49 54.3% 
2023-24 133.73 65.00 32.91 50.6% 
2024-25 90.00 45.00 36.57 (till date) 81.3% 
 
 
The reasons behind the drastic reduction in the allocation due to low expenditure in the initial 
years and savings under the scheme have been informed as follows: - 
 
i. Delay in recruitment process of Human resources under NTMHP by States. 
 
ii. The expenditure under AMC, was factored on the basis of peak capacity of cells 
to handle calls. However initially the number of calls was less and has grown 
exponentially since then. 
iii. All the States / UTs / Institutes were mapped as Central Nodal Agency (CNA) 
which took considerable time and effort causing delay in release of funds to 
States. 
 
 
3.15.3  The Committee notes significant under-utilisation of fund under the 
National Tele Mental Health Programme. In both FYs 2023-24 and 2024-25, the 
allocation of funds at RE stage was significantly reduced as compared to the allocation 
at BE stage. The Committee believes in immediate and targeted interventions to resolve 
bottlenecks in fund utilization to ensure the sustained growth and effectiveness of Tele 
MANAS. Further, the recruitment processes for human resources at the State and 
Union Territory levels must be expedited through streamlined procedures and proactive 
central government support.  
 
3.15.4  The Committee urges upon the Department for an intensified focus on 
targeted outreach and awareness campaigns to build on the increasing engagement of 
female callers and extend services to vulnerable populations. These efforts should 
leverage the Tele MANAS mobile application and diverse media platforms to address 
specific barriers preventing access to services. Furthermore, the nationwide expansion 
of video consultation services, currently available in select states, should be prioritized to 
enhance accessibility, particularly for individuals in remote and underserved areas. This 
expansion must be supported by comprehensive training and technical assistance to 
ensure effective implementation and utilization of the video consultation platform. 
 
 
3.16 NATIONAL DIGITAL HEALTH MISSION (NDHM)/ AYUSHMAN BHARAT 
DIGITAL MISSION (ABDM): 
3.16.1  Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM), earlier known as the National 
Digital Health Mission (NDHM) has been launched in Sep 2021, with the aim of enabling 

71 
 
interoperability of health data within the health ecosystem and helping create longitudinal 
electronic health records for every citizen that ensures continuum of care for citizens. ABDM 
comprises key registries intended through building trusted, verified registries such as 
Ayushman Bharat Health Account (ABHA), Healthcare Professional Registry (HPR) and 
Health Facility registry (HFR). As on 6th February 2025, a total of 73,98,09,607 (Rs. 73.98 
Cr) ABHA have been created, 3,63,520 (Rs. 3.63 Lac) health facilities have registered on 
HFR, 5,64,851 (Rs. 5.64 Lac) healthcare professionals have registered on HPR and 
49,06,02,540 (Rs. 49.06 Cr) health records have been linked with ABHA. 
 
3.16.2  Leveraging the aforesaid registries, ABDM envisages making healthcare 
accessible and affordable. OPD registration has been made simpler by ABHA enabled QR 
code based registration services. As of February 2025, 18,879 facilities across 35 states/UTs 
have generated 8.34 crore tokens, significantly reducing waiting times in the registration 
process for the patients. A public dashboard [dashboard.abdm.gov.in] is available for NHA, 
the implementing body of ABDM, and the states/UTs to assess the different indicators 
reflecting the progress of the scheme. NHA is providing necessary support including technical 
assistance and training for all stakeholders to integrate their solutions under the ABDM 
ecosystem. 
 
3.16.3  To ensure the effective implementation of ABDM, the Union Health Ministry 
provides financial assistance to States/UTs for human resources, information education & 
communication (IEC), and capacity building including necessary handholding support in on 
boarding the facilities/ health professionals and integrate state health digital health 
programs/solutions under ABDM ecosystem. Union Health Ministry also provides financial 
assistance to all States/UTs under National Health Mission for strengthening of health 
infrastructure for the implementation of IT interventions such as Hospital Management 
Information System (HMIS) and telemedicine services across the country. 
 
3.16.4  The Committee has been informed that the financial outlay for ABDM is 
₹1600 Cr for five years (FY 2021-22 to FY 2025-26). For the FY 2023-24, the BE was Rs. 
341.02 crores. This BE was revised to Rs. 209.90 crores owing to the clear realization that 
budgets under ‘Grants-in-Aid’ to the States and ‘Digital Equipment’s’ will not be fully spent. 
Out of the RE of Rs. 209.90 crores, Rs. 175.90 crores was utilized (83% utilization). For the 
year FY 2024-25, the expenditure till date is Rs. 114 crores [as on 31.01.2025], against a RE 
of Rs. 225 crores. An amount to the tune of Rs. 340.11 crore in BE 2025-26 have been 
allocated to the Scheme as compared to Rs. 225.00 crore in RE 2024-25.

3.16.7  Given the recurring issues of underutilization of funds within the 
Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM), specifically due to delays in state-level 
Project Monitoring Unit (PMU) establishment, inefficient management of advertising 
contracts, performance issues with Managed Service Providers (MSPs), and 
complications in professional service contracts, the Committee recommends a robust 
fund disbursement and monitoring mechanism should be instituted. This should include 
strict timelines and performance-linked incentives for States/UTs to expedite the 
establishment of Project Monitoring Units (PMUs), along with real-time tracking of 
fund allocation and utilization. Furthermore, ensuring timely on-boarding of competent 
service providers and enforcing clear contractual obligations with penalties for non-
performance will help streamline expenditure under Digital Equipment and 
Professional Services. Strengthening coordination between NHA, States/UTs, and 
implementing agencies will be critical for optimizing fund flow and ensuring effective 
implementation of ABDM objectives. 
3.16.8  On a specific query regarding inclusion of artificial intelligence (AI) and 
machine learning (ML) in the operations at healthcare facilities, the Department replied that 
Registries and Gateways built under ABDM ensure that data silos can be broken and a 
longitudinal electronic health record (EHR) of citizens is created. This enables integration of 
emerging technologies such as Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Machine Learning (ML) with 
existing healthcare IT applications to enhance efficiency and performance of the health 
services. In this regard, NHA has also signed an MoU with IIT Kanpur on 14th Oct 2024. 
Under this MoU, a federated learning platform across a variety of machine learning model 
pipelines, a quality-preserving database, an open benchmarking platform for comparing 
&validating AI models, and a consent management system for research under Ayushman 
Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM) would be developed by IIT Kanpur. The platform will 
subsequently be operated and governed by NHA, thereby unlocking the immense potential of 
AI for improving health outcomes. Further, e-Sanjeevani, telemedicine system developed by 
CDAC, already incorporates AI based system. In e-Sanjeevani, CDAC has implemented an 
in-house developed AI-based Clinical Decision Support System (CDSS). This module 
analyses patient ailment information and empowers doctors by suggesting differential 
diagnoses, thereby enhancing productivity and aiding in decision-making. Further, an AI 
driven diagnostic is also at experimental stage.  
3.16.9  The following key initiatives under ABDM for improving health data 
management and interoperability among hospitals has been informed: - 
 
 

74 
 
i. Microsite Project: Microsites drive ABDM adoption in specific areas, on boarding 
private healthcare providers onto the platform. Currently, 121 microsites have 
registered over 54,000 facilities and linked 44 lakh ABHA health records. 
 
ii. Scan and Share: This ABHA enabled QR-code-based OPD registration service enables 
patients to share demographic details digitally, reducing queue times. As of February 
2025, 18,879 facilities across 35 states/UTs have generated 8.34 crore tokens, saving 
3.3 crore person - hours. 
 
iii. Scan and Pay: Patients can scan facility QR codes using PHR apps to view and pay for 
open orders linked to their ABHA address. As of February 2025, nine facilities, have 
operationalized this service, recording 5452 transactions worth over ₹ 10,00,000. 
 
iv. Model ABDM Facility Initiative: This initiative digitizes healthcare facilities to 
enhance patient care and streamline services. By February 2025, 133 facilities have 
been selected, with workshops conducted for action plan creation. 
 
3.16.10  The Committee recommends to fully leveraging the potential of AI 
and ML in healthcare by accelerating the integration and scaling of AI-driven tools 
within the Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM). Specifically, the successful 
collaborations, such as the MoU with IIT Kanpur and the AI-based Clinical Decision 
Support System (CDSS) in e-Sanjeevani, should be rapidly replicated. This includes 
prioritizing the development and deployment of unified learning platforms, quality-
preserving databases, and open benchmarking systems, ensuring that these technologies 
are accessible and beneficial across all healthcare facilities, especially in rural and 
underserved areas. This will enhance diagnostic accuracy, streamline patient care, and 
improve overall health outcomes by making data-driven decisions more efficient. 
3.16.11  The Committee believes that the ABDM should effectively reach 
rural and tribal populations for gains in real terms. The Committee, in this connection, 
emphasizes the need for enhanced digital literacy and connectivity initiatives alongside 
the mission's technical advancements. The NHA should intensify efforts to implement 
and promote the assisted mode for ABHA creation in areas with poor internet 
connectivity and limited digital literacy. Additionally, targeted awareness campaigns 
and training programs should be conducted in local languages to educate healthcare 
providers and citizens about the benefits and usage of ABDM services like Scan and 
Share and Scan and Pay. Collaborations with local community leaders and NGOs can 
further facilitate the adoption of ABDM, ensuring that even the most remote 
populations have equitable access to digital health services while maintaining robust 
privacy and security measures. 
 
3.17 STATUTORY AND REGULATORY BODIES 
 
3.17.1  The following statutory and regulatory bodies come under the purview of the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare: -  
 

75 
 
i. FSSAI has been established under the Food Safety and Standards Act, 2006 for 
laying down the science based standards for articles of food and to regulate their 
manufacture, storage, distribution, sale and import, to ensure availability of safe 
and wholesome food for human consumption.  
 
ii. IPC provide for performing activities to Publish Indian Pharmacopeia and its 
Addendum, to develop and validate the Indian Pharmacopeia Reference 
Substances (IPRS) and Impurity Standards, Skill Development of Drugs Analyst, 
Drugs Inspectors and Stakeholders etc.  
 
iii. NMC is a statutory body with the responsibility of establishing and maintaining 
high standards of medical education and recognition of medical qualifications in 
India. It registers doctors to practice in India, in order to protect and promote the 
health and safety of the public by ensuring proper standards in the practice of 
medicine.  
 
iv. DCI is a Statutory Body incorporated under an Act of Parliament viz. The Dentists 
Act, 1948 (XVI of 1948) to regulate the Dental Education and the profession of 
Dentistry throughout India.  
 
v. PCI is a statutory body governed to regulate Pharmacy education and profession 
upto graduate level and prescribe minimum standard of education required for 
qualifying as pharmacist.  
 
vi. INC is a statutory body constituted by the Central Government under section 3(1) 
of the Indian Nursing Council Act, 1947 to establish a uniform standard of training 
for nurses, Midwives and health visitors.  
 
vii. NAMS is a unique institution which fosters and utilizes academic excellence as its 
resource to meet medical and social goals, established under Societies Registration 
Act XXI of 1860.  
 
viii. NBE is an independent autonomous body of its kind in the country in the field of 
medical sciences with the prime objective of improving the quality of the medical 
education by elevating the level and establishing standards of post graduate 
examinations in modern medicine on all India basis. 
 
 

 3.17.2  The Committee, on examination of Demands for Grants, notes that in the FY 2023-24, there was minor variation in revision of budgetary allocation from Rs. 639.10 crore at BE stage to Rs. 633.98 at RE stage. To achieve this, regular surveillance drives, monitoring, regulatory inspections, and random mpling of various food products are conducted throughout the year by officials from the food safety departments of the respective States/UTs and Regional Offices of FSSAI. 3.17.5  The activities planned for FY 2025-26 have been stated as follows: 
 
i. Financial and technical support to States/ UTs under MoU for strenthening 
food safety ecosystem.

3.17.6  The Committee notes that the reduction in budgetary allocation for the 
Food Safety and Standards Authority of India (FSSAI) in the RE 2024-25 was primarily 
due to delayed submission of Utilization Certificates (UCs) from States/UTs. This delay 
hinders the timely disbursement of funds for strengthening the food safety ecosystem. 
The committee, therefore, recommends the Department for implementing a streamlined 
UC submission and tracking system. To ensure consistent and timely fund utilization, 
the FSSAI should establish a mandatory, digitally enabled system for States/UTs to 
submit and track their UCs. This system should include automated reminders and alerts 
for pending submissions, and provide real-time visibility of UC status to both FSSAI and 
the respective States/UTs. Furthermore, the FSSAI should conduct periodic workshops 
and training sessions for State/UT officials to improve their understanding of UC 
requirements and submission processes, thereby preventing future delays and ensuring 
efficient fund utilization. This proactive approach will facilitate smoother fund flow and 
enable FSSAI to effectively carry out its mandate of ensuring food safety across the 
country. 
 
3.17.7  Given the importance of FSSAI's activities, particularly in supporting 
States/UTs, the Committee is of the opinion that a phased funding release tied to 
performance and capacity building should be implemented. Instead of a single annual 
allocation, funds could be released in tranches, contingent upon the timely submission of 
UCs and demonstrable progress in enhancing food safety infrastructure and 
surveillance. In addition, a portion of the funds should be dedicated to capacity-building 
initiatives, such as training programs for food safety officers, procurement of essential 
equipment, and the establishment of robust laboratory facilities at the state and local 

78 
 
levels. This approach will incentivize States/UTs to prioritize food safety initiatives, 
improve their administrative efficiency, and build sustainable food safety systems. 
 
3.17.8  On a specific query, the Committee was informed about the Mobile Food 
Testing Laboratory (MFTL) introduced by FSSAI. MFTL is commonly known as "Food 
Safety on Wheels" (FSW), a multipurpose vehicle designed to disseminate information, 
provide food safety training, and conduct food testing. FSWs are equipped with basic 
infrastructure to enable on-the-spot testing for adulteration in various food commodities. The 
objective is to deploy at least one FSW in each district by 2026, contingent on the 
preparedness of the respective states. To achieve this, FSSAI has sanctioned 497 FSWs, of 
which 97 were approved under the CSS Scheme. Currently, 285 FSWs are operational across 
35 States and Union Territories. By 2025-26, an additional 212 FSWs are planned for 
deployment, subject to state readiness. 
 
3.17.9  For the testing of various food commodities, FSSAI notifies NABL-accredited 
food laboratories under Sections 43(1) and 43(2) of the FSS Act, 2006, for primary and 
referral/appellate sample testing. Currently, there are 243 FSSAI-notified primary laboratories 
and 22 referral laboratories. To broaden the scope of food testing laboratories under the 
FSSR, FSSAI, in collaboration with NABL, has developed the FSSAI-NABL Integrated 
Assessment Scheme of Accreditation, documented in the NABL-127 document. FSSAI has 
provided both financial and technical support to state laboratories, and is encouraging NABL 
Accreditation of State Food Testing Laboratories. Under the scheme "Strengthening of Food 
Testing Systems in the Country, including Provision of Mobile Food Testing Labs 
(SoFTeL)," FSSAI implemented a CSS scheme with a financial outlay of Rs. 613.69 crore 
from 2016-17 to 2023-24, of which Rs. 613.67 crore has already been released.  
 
3.17.10 All States/UTs have a State Food Regulatory System. However, 10 States/UTs 
have a separate Food Safety Department, while the others have a combined department with 
drugs. A detail of States/UTs having a separate Food Safety Department is provided in table 
below:  
 
Table-3.23 
 
Sl No. States/UTs Is there a separate Food Safety 
Department or not?

There are 33 States/UTs with Food Safety Appellate Tribunals. 
 

79 
 
3.17.11 On a specific query, the Committee has been informed that the total sanctioned 
strength in FSSAI is 824, out of which 238 posts are vacant as on 07.02.2025. Also, 75 
employees are engaged on contractual basis. The direct recruitment process to fill the 
remaining positions is scheduled to commence in 2025. 
 
3.17.12 The Committee has been informed that regular need-based assessments and 
reviews of the Food Safety and Standards Rules and Regulations are conducted, resulting in 
various amendments. Impact assessments of the proposed amendments are also carried out 
through draft notifications to invite stakeholder comments, both domestic and international. 
Sections 59(i), 61, and 63 of the FSS Act, 2006, which pertain to punishment for unsafe food, 
punishment for false information, and punishment for carrying out a business without a 
license, were amended via the Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Bill, 2023. In respect 
of Rules and Regulations, the Committee has been informed that the Food Safety and 
Standards (Rules), 2011, and 28 regulations, as specified under Sections 16 and 92 of the 
Food Safety and Standards Act, 2006, have been established by FSSAI. One regulation 
related to genetically modified foods has been draft-notified and is currently in the process of 
finalization. 
 
3.17.13 The Committee notes that FSSAI is actively deploying Mobile Food 
Testing Laboratories (MFTLs) to enhance on-the-spot food testing and awareness, and 
while progress is made in this connection, achieving the target of one MFTL/FSW per 
district by 2026 is contingent on state readiness. The Committee recommends FSSAI to 
implement a structured program to actively assist states in achieving 
readiness/willingness to deploy MFTLs/FSWs. The Committee feels that through 
dedicated State liaisons, FSSAI personnel should work directly with state governments 
to identify and address specific barriers to FSW deployment. The Committee desires 
that targeted training programs and expedited funding mechanisms should be 
established showing proactive approach to prevent delays and ensure consistent food 
safety monitoring across all districts. 
 
3.17.14 FSSAI faces a significant number of vacant positions, which could impact 
its operational efficiency. The Committee believes that to improve the operational 
efficiency of FSSAI and enhance the consistency of food safety regulation across the 
country, FSSAI should be directed to prioritize and expedite the direct recruitment 
process to fill up the significant number of vacant positions. Additionally, FSSAI should 
implement strategies to improve employee retention and recruitment on regular basis 
must be adhered to instead of hiring staff on contract basis as contractual staff lacks 
sense of organizational belongingness and commitment to work and there remain sense 
of being on ad-hoc at work that too without responsibility and accountability. The 
Committee, therefore, believes that efforts be made to grant regularization of 
contractual staff in FSSAI.  
 
3.17.15 Furthermore, the Committee notes that not all States have separate food 
safety departments, which could lead to inconsistencies in implementation, however, 
most of the States have Food Safety Appellate Tribunals.  The Committee, therefore, 
holds that separate Food Safety Departments should be promoted. The Committee 
strongly emphasizes the Department to encourage the remaining states/UTs to establish 

separate Food Safety Departments. Providing guidance and examples of the benefits of separate departments, as well as possible assistance during the transition, will help to increase the number of states with separate departments. 3.18 ALL INDIA INSTITUTE OF M

3.18.3  The Committee notes with concern that six construction projects are 
ongoing in the AIIMS, New Delhi and one construction project related to expansion of 
NDDTC, Ghaziabad AIIMS is proposed to be undertaken whose detailed estimate is in 
progress. The Committee wishes to highlight that the implementation status of the 

82 
 
ongoing six projects is not satisfactory, which can be corroborated by the fact that out of 
the six ongoing projects, one is in tendering process, one is pending due to tree cutting 
and environmental clearance, one has attained just 2.50% of physical progress and the 
other one has attained the 28.6% of physical progress.    
 
3.18.4  The Committee's assessment reveals a concerning trend in AIIMS, New 
Delhi's utilization of budgetary allocation. While the utilization of budgetary funds was 
satisfactory in FY 2023-24, a significant shortfall occurred in FY 2024-25, with only 
81.96% of the revised estimate being spent till the end of January 2025. This 
underutilization is further underscored by the slow progress of ongoing construction 
projects. The key issues include delayed Project implementation, weak project planning 
and execution, delay in tendering process and environmental and regulatory hurdles.  
 
3.18.5  The Committee recommends that AIIMS, New Delhi, establish a robust 
project monitoring mechanism with clearly defined milestones and regular progress 
reviews. This mechanism should include a dedicated team to proactively address 
regulatory hurdles, such as environmental clearances and tree-cutting approvals, by 
engaging with relevant authorities at the earliest stages of project planning. 
Furthermore, AIIMS should streamline its tendering process to minimize/eliminate 
delays, potentially through pre-qualifying vendors and implementing a more efficient 
evaluation system to ensure that projects like the 150 bedded Critical Care block, and 
the super speciality OPD block are not delayed due to avoidable administrative issues. 
 
3.18.6  The Committee strongly recommends a comprehensive review of the 
residential redevelopment project, given the project's extremely slow progress (2.50% 
physical, 3.68% financial). This review should assess the project's feasibility, identify the 
root causes of delays, and explore potential restructuring options, including phased 
implementation or alternative construction methodologies. The Department should get a 
detailed report outlining the findings of this review and the proposed corrective actions. 
The Department should also examine the large amount of fund that has been allocated 
to this project, and its efficient utilization. 
 
3.18.7  The Committee observed that a significant number of patients and their 
attendants wait outside AIIMS, New Delhi, overnight to secure walk-in OPD appointments 
for the next day, facing challenges due to adverse weather and lack of food and washroom 
facilities. In response, AIIMS has implemented several initiatives over the past year to 
address overcrowding and reduce patient waiting time: 
 
1. Provision of Ashray: A meeting chaired by the Medical Superintendent, AIIMS, 
with CRPF and Delhi Police officials led to the arrangement of shifting patients 
waiting outside Gate No. 6 to "Ashray," a shelter installed by CRPF near JPNATC, 
using AIIMS campus e-vehicles. CRPF provides blankets, while ISKCON Temple 
supplies free food. Appointment slips are issued based on arrival order, and patients 
are transported via e-vehicles to the OPD in the morning for appointment 
distribution, streamlining the process and reducing overcrowding. 

83 
 
2. Patient Convenience Measures: AIIMS has introduced several facilities, including 
a Smart Lab Facility, Jan Aushadhi Kendras, e-shuttle service, multilingual 
signages, ramps, wheelchairs, and Patient Care Coordinators to assist in hospital 
navigation. 
3. Streamlining Appointments: The Institute has implemented an online appointment 
system via the Online Registration System (ORS) portal. 
4. Expansion of Specialty Blocks: AIIMS has commissioned new specialty blocks 
and facilities, including the Burns & Plastic Surgery Block, Surgical Block, New 
RAK OPD Block, Mother & Child Block, National Centre for Ageing, vertical 
expansion of JPNATC, and the National Cancer Institute at Jhajjar, with additional 
projects in the pipeline to enhance OPD and IPD capacities. 
5. Provision of Vishram Sadans/Dharmshalas: AIIMS operates Vishram Sadans 
across five locations, offering over 1,500 beds (including Jhajjar campus), clean 
drinking water, mobile charging stations, cafeteria, and restrooms to support 
patients and attendants. 
 
3.18.8  On a query regarding the implementation status of the Master Plan for AIIMS, 
New Delhi, the Committee has been informed that the Union Cabinet in its meeting held on 
28.02.2019 accorded ‘in-principle’ approval for implementation of a Master Plan of AIIMS-
Delhi to convert it into a world class Medical University. The integrated master plan was 
prepared for developing approximately 190 acres of AIIMS campus spread over five lands 
parcels, involving pre-construction activities. The EFC Memorandum with firmed up cost 
estimates of ₹15,558.37 crore was submitted by AIIMS in March 2022 for implementation in 
phases spread over a period of 07 years.  With concurrence of IFD, the Ministry circulated the 
draft EFC Memo to the concerned Ministries including M/o Housing and Urban Affairs. After 
resolution of land related issues and subsequent amalgamation of all land parcels allotted to 
AIIMS, New Delhi by Land & Development Office of MoHUA and Delhi Development 
Authority and requisite payment by AIIMS, MoHUA supported the proposal contained in the 
EFC Memo in July 2024.  In view of the plan for phased implementation and on the basis of 
functional requirement, component of the Master Plan have been taken up for implementation 
in the Institute including construction of new OPD block at Masjid Moth in AIIMS, New 
Delhi at an estimated cost of Rs. 484.51 crore. Another proposal that relates to the 
construction of Hostel accommodation at Trauma centre extention with a tentative cost of 
approximately Rs. 876 crore is under examination in the Ministry. 
   
3.18.9  The Committee notes the AIIMS, New Delhi's efforts to address patient 
overcrowding and improve services which include providing temporary shelter 
("Ashray"), enhancing patient convenience through various facilities, streamlining 
appointments via online systems, expanding infrastructure with new specialty blocks, 
and offering accommodation through "Vishram Sadans." Additionally, the Committee 
desires the AIIMS, Delhi to expand the "Ashray" initiative by providing more 
permanent, weather-protected waiting areas with adequate seating, clean washrooms, 
and access to affordable food options. This will significantly reduce the hardship faced 
by patients and their attendants awaiting appointments. However, significant challenges 
remain, particularly with long wait times for walk-in OPD appointments and substantial 

84 
 
staff vacancies across all cadres. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Institute implements a robust, real-time digital queue management system for walk-in 
OPD appointments. This system should provide patients with estimated wait times via 
SMS or a mobile app, allowing them to wait in designated, comfortable areas rather 
than overnight outside. Keeping in view the fact of long waiting time for urgent 
treatment of even critical patients, the Committee desires that Department should 
explore the possibilities of construction of another AIIMS in NCR region close to New 
Delhi.  
 
3.18.10 The Committee acknowledges that the ambitious Master Plan for AIIMS, 
aimed at transforming it into a world-class medical university, is progressing but faces 
delays due to land acquisition and budgetary approvals. Simultaneously, the 
Department must expedite the approval and allocation of the necessary budgetary 
resources for the AIIMS Master Plan. To ensure timely execution, a dedicated project 
management team should be established to oversee the phased implementation of the 
plan, with regular progress reviews and reporting to the Committee. This will ensure 
that the infrastructure upgrades are completed without further delay, and that AIIMS 
can reach its goal to become a world class medical university.
