3.19.3 The Committee observes with appreciation that PGIMER, Chandigarh 
achieved 100% fund utilization in FY 2023-24. However, in FY 2024-25, only 75.32% of 
the revised estimate (RE) was utilized till the end of January 2025, particularly with low 
spending on capital asset creation. The Committee, however, expresses concern over the 
substantial time overruns in respect of the ongoing projects of the Institute. The 
Committee recommends that the Institute develop a detailed, time-bound action plan to 
utilize the allocated funds for capital asset creation effectively. The low expenditure in 
this area for 2024-2025 is a matter of serious concern. The Committee urges upon the 
institute to prioritize the completion of the ongoing construction projects, addressing the 
root causes of the significant time overruns. Furthermore, the Department should ask 
for a report detailing the reasons for the low expenditure on the capital asset creation 

87 
 
for the 2024-2025 financial year, and the steps to be taken to control declining trend of 
utilization of fund in the future. This will ensure that the infrastructure upgrades are 
completed promptly for improving patient care and overall operational efficiency.  
 
3.19.4  The information w.r.t. Regular Staff and outsourced staff submitted by the 
Department is as under:  
 
Table-3.28 
 
Sr. No. Name of Post Sanction Strength  In-position Vacant 
1.  Regular Staff 7605 5809 1796 
2. Staff engaged on outsource basis 4132 4100 32 
 
 
3.19.5 The Committee observes that there are 1796 vacancies in regular staff of 
the Institute which is 23.62% of the sanctioned strength of the regular staff, however, the 
outsourced staff utilisation is nearly at full capacity. The Committee is of the view that 
staff should be recruited on regular basis and not on contractual basis for continuity of 
work flow. Given the substantial number of vacant regular staff positions, the 
Committee recommends that the Institute launch a targeted recruitment on regular 
basis as contractual appointment of staff leads to ad-hoc sense at work. This drive 
should focus on filling critical roles to alleviate the strain on existing staff and improve 
service delivery. Simultaneously, the Institute should develop and implement a 
comprehensive staff retention strategy, which may include enhanced incentives, career 
development opportunities, and improved work-life balance initiatives. This will help to 
reduce the vacancy rate and ensure a stable and skilled workforce. 
 
3.19.6  On being asked the Department submitted that the Institute while catering to a 
large number of patients faces several challenges, including: - 
 
i. Resource Management: Ensuring adequate staffing, medical supplies, and 
equipment to meet patient needs. 
ii. Patient Flow: Managing the flow of patients to minimize wait times and ensure 
timely care. 
iii. Quality of Care: Maintaining high standards of care while dealing with high 
patient volumes. 
iv. Digitalization: Integrating digital tools and systems to improve efficiency and 
patient care. 
v. Workforce Shortages: Addressing shortages of skilled healthcare professionals. 
vi. Patient Satisfaction: Balancing patient expectations with the realities of 
healthcare delivery. 
 
3.19.7  The Committee has also been informed the Institute is committed to provide 
high quality healthcare services and taken several steps to address the challenges of catering to 
a large number of patients: - 
 
i. Implementing Advanced Technology: PGIMER, Chandigarh is in the advanced 
stage of procuring and installing Hospital Information System 2.0, aimed at 

88 
 
enhancing the efficiency and efficacy of existing processes. This system will 
induce automation and transparency, subsequently resulting in better patient 
satisfaction. The Institute's telemedicine services are already fully operational with 
state-of-the-art technology. Furthermore, the Institute has upgraded telemedicine 
technology for tele-evidences, providing expert opinions in medico-legal cases for 
two Hon'ble courts of India. 
 
ii. Enhancing Staff Training: The Institute encourages its staff to pursue higher 
courses in their respective domains, both within the Institute and externally, to 
adopt best practices from other institutions. In this regard, the Institute has 
extended the scope for its nursing staff to pursue M.Sc. Nursing from any Institute 
of National Importance (INI). Additionally, all staff members, whether medical, 
para-medical, or technical, are encouraged to pursue higher studies from other 
national institutions with varied expert knowledge. 
 
iii. Optimizing Patient Flow: To optimize patient flow, PGIMER has opened three 
Satellite Centres at Sangrur, Una, and Ferozepur. The Satellite Centre in Sangrur is 
fully functional and caters to the local population at par with the excellence of the 
main Institute. Additionally, two new Centers of Neurosciences and Mother & 
Child Care are under construction within the Institute. 
 
iv. Expanding Workforce: The Institute is focused on recruiting and retaining more 
healthcare professionals by offering better incentives and support. 
 
v. Patient Engagement: PGIMER actively involves patients in their care decisions 
and strives to improve communication to enhance patient engagement. 
 
vi. Quality Improvement Initiatives: The Institute continuously monitors and 
improves the quality of care through regular feedback and data analysis. 
 
3.19.8  The Committee notes that PGIMER, Chandigarh faces challenges like 
resource management, patient flow, quality of care, digitalization, workforce shortages, 
and patient satisfaction. The Committee also acknowledges that the Institute is 
implementing solutions, including advanced technology, enhanced staff training, 
optimized patient flow through satellite centers, workforce expansion, patient 
engagement, and quality improvement initiatives. The Committee recommends that the 
Institute implement a more rigorous system for monitoring and reporting project 
timelines and financial utilization. This system should include regular progress reviews, 
detailed reporting on potential delays, and proactive measures to mitigate risks. This 
will improve the infrastructure and facilities in the Institute in an efficient manner 
leading to solutions of many challenges faced by the Institute.

3.21.1  The scrutiny of Demands for Grants highlights that NIMHANS, Bengaluru, in 
the FY 2023-24, was allocated Rs. 721.15 crore at BE stage which was increased slightly to 
Rs. 750.54 crore at RE stage. The Institute has shown complete utilization of funds in the FY 
2023-24 by spending the full amount of Rs. 750.54 crore allocated at RE stage. In the FY 
2024-25, the BE allocation amounting to Rs. 850 crore was reduced to Rs. 780 crore at the RE 
stage. A slightly increased amount to the tune of Rs. 860.00 crore has been earmarked to the 
Institute in 2025-26. The break-up of the allocation for the FY 2025-26 is as under: - 
 
i. Capital - Rs.155 Crores towards Major works, constructions, procurement of 
medical equipments, library books and furnitures. 
 
ii. General – Rs.225 Crores towards Hospital expenses, maintenance expenses, 
NMH Survey activities 
 
iii. Salary - Rs.470 Crore towards salary, NPS, DA rise, Pension liabilities, ect., 
 
iv. SAP - Rs.10 core towards Swachatha activities.  
 
3.21.2  On being asked about the reasons for reduction in budgetary allocation for the 
FY 2024-25 at the RE stage by Rs. 70 crore, the Committee has been informed that the 
procurement of major medical equipments and major infrastructure works worth Rs. 70 crore 
was delayed due to the imposition of the Model Code of Conduct (MCC) during the recent 
election period. As per the guidelines of the MCC, no major tenders or procurement processes 
could be initiated or finalized during this time to ensure neutrality and prevent any undue 
influence on the election process. This resulted in the postponement of the tendering process, 
which in turn caused delays in the procurement. However, with the lifting of the MCC 
restrictions, the institute has immediately resumed the procurement process. These tenders are 
being prioritized and all necessary measures being taken to ensure that the procurement is 
completed as quickly as possible, without compromising quality or compliance.  
 
3.21.3  The Committee observes that NIMHANS demonstrates strong fund 
utilization, spending the full allocated amount in FY 2023-24. It has been submitted that 
the budgetary fluctuations occurred in FY 2024-25 due to the Model Code of Conduct 
(MCC) imposed during election which highlights potential planning issues. The 
Committee, in this connection, would like to highlight that the imposition of MCC 
during election is only for a certain period in the whole duration of a fiscal year. The 
Institute must have been aware of the imposition of MCC in advance and should plan 
the execution of projects and utilization of funds accordingly. The Committee believes 
that the Department should promote enhanced Budgetary Planning and Mitigation 
Strategies among the Institutions under its purview. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that NIMHANS implement a more robust budgetary planning process that 
accounts for foreseeable disruptions, such as the Model Code of Conduct. Specifically, 
NIMHANS should develop contingency plans for procurement and project execution to 
minimize the impact of such events. This includes creating a detailed timeline for major 
projects, prioritizing essential procurements, and establishing clear communication 

93 
 
channels with relevant authorities to anticipate and address potential delays. By 
proactive planning, NIMHANS can ensure consistent and efficient utilization of 
allocated funds, maintaining the continuity of vital projects and services. 
 
3.21.4  On being asked, the Committee has been apprised of the following 
achievements made by the Institute during the years 2023-24 and 2024-25: - 
 
i. NIMHANS maintained its leadership in healthcare, training, and research, 
earning national and international recognition. It received the prestigious 
Nelson Mandela Award for the Promotion of Health from WHO, Geneva, for 
its contributions to public and mental health. 
ii. Ranked 4th in the NIRF 2024 rankings for the fifth consecutive year, 
NIMHANS also won the 9th FICCI Higher Education Excellence Award for 
research, innovation, and entrepreneurship. 
iii. As the first Institute of National Importance (INI) hospital to receive NABH 
accreditation, NIMHANS successfully renewed its accreditation under the fifth 
edition standards, reaffirming its commitment to quality care. 
iv. In 2023-24, NIMHANS treated 6,65,432 patients, conducted 8,254 surgeries, 
and performed 21,39,044 diagnostic tests. New initiatives include the 
Reproductive Care Clinic for women’s health, the PRIMES program for early 
schizophrenia intervention, and an amyloid PET MR study for Alzheimer’s 
and non-AD dementia diagnosis. 
v. Partnering with IIT Gandhinagar and IISc Bangalore, NIMHANS is 
developing an indigenous brain tumour imaging probe and AI/ML-based 
diagnostic algorithms. The Tele-SWABHIMAN project with UNICEF focuses 
on maternal nutrition and mental health. 
vi. New equipment, including the 3T MRI Magnetom Cima.X and advanced 
surgical tools, enhances precision in treatment. The Centre for Addiction 
Medicine (CAM) is the first in India to offer both opioid substitution and free 
Hepatitis C treatment. 
vii. With 28 departments, NIMHANS offers a range of programs, including MCh, 
DM, post-doctoral fellowships, doctoral studies, MPhil, master’s, diploma, and 
select undergraduate courses. Over 400 students enroll annually. 
viii. Specialized programs train administrative medical officers, caregivers, police 
personnel, and media professionals. NIMHANS Digital Academy (NDA) 
provides 30 digital courses, benefiting 25,000+ healthcare professionals. 
ix. Initiatives like MANASWINI (with Piramal Foundation) promote mental 
health awareness among women, while the Stree-Manoraksha project 
strengthens One Stop Centre (OSC) staff training to support women facing 
violence. 
x. As the Apex Coordinating Centre for Tele MANAS, NIMHANS has trained 
303 new counsellors, expanding access to mental health support nationwide. 
xi. Advancing mental health and neurosciences, NIMHANS published 972 
scientific articles in high-profile journals during the review period, 
contributing significantly to knowledge generation and patient care. 

94 
 
3.21.5  The Committee has been informed of the Institute’s contributions in alleviating 
India's mental health burden through various research and community-based initiatives. A 
summary of key contributions is provided below: - 
 
i. Advancing Approaches to Dementia Associated Research (AADAR): 
NIMHANS is leading a central government-funded project to make South 
Bengaluru dementia-friendly. This first-of-its-kind initiative, launched by the 
Department of Biotechnology and the Ministry of Science and Technology, 
investigates dementia prevalence, incidence, risk, and awareness in the region. 
ii. Consortium of Vulnerability to Externalising Disorders and Addictions 
(cVEDA): The cVEDA cohort study has established one of India's largest 
brain development databases and a biorepository of over 9,000 subjects. The 
data has enabled the development of neurodevelopmental trajectories, 
including an Indian brain template, brain growth maps, and identification of 
developmental risks. 
iii. National Mental Health Survey – Phase 2 (Profiling the Megacities of 
India): This survey assesses the true burden of mental health disorders in 
India’s megacities, focusing on socioeconomic correlates and physical 
illnesses. Additionally, it examines COVID-19-related mental health aspects 
and incorporates epilepsy within the broader category of Mental, Neurological, 
and Substance Use (MNS) disorders. 
iv. Stree-Manoraksha: This initiative enhances training and supervision for staff 
at One Stop Centres (OSCs) across India, ensuring comprehensive 
psychosocial and mental health support for women facing violence. Over 1,000 
OSC staff members have received training, strengthening mental health care 
within the existing support framework. 
v. Bengaluru Urban Mental Health Initiative: This urban mental health 
program strengthens healthcare professionals' capacity to manage mental 
disorders through a structured learning system. It has conducted 36 training 
sessions, engaging 54 participants who assessed 155 patients. 
vi. Support, Advocacy & Mental Health Interventions for Children in 
Vulnerable Circumstances and Distress (SAMVAD): Supported by the 
Ministry of Women & Child Development, this initiative enhances 
psychosocial well-being among children and adolescents in vulnerable 
situations. It has reached over 3 million individuals, providing standardized 
training on child protection and adolescent mental health, while also 
emphasizing women’s mental health. 
vii. National Tele Mental Health Program (Tele MANAS): NIMHANS, as the 
Apex Coordinating Centre, has conducted extensive training, stakeholder 
engagements, and international knowledge exchanges. Over the past year, it 
organized 560 training sessions, training 303 new counsellors to strengthen 
India’s mental health support system. 
viii. Geriatric Psychiatry Services: The Vayo Manasa Sanjeevani initiative offers 
geriatric mental health support, training volunteers, and conducting 
psychosocial sessions. The Dementia ECHO program trains physicians in 

95 
 
dementia care, while the Alzheimer’s SEVA initiative has trained 500+ 
volunteers in early diagnosis and assistance for Alzheimer's patients. 
ix. Yuva Spandana: This youth empowerment program, active in Karnataka, 
targets individuals aged 15–30, addressing education, career development, 
health, and relationships. With 39,938 sensitization programs, it has reached 
approximately 5.99 million youth, integrating health promotion into district 
youth empowerment centres. 
x. Clinical Research Centre for Neuromodulation in Psychiatry: This multi-
institutional initiative, supported by the Department of Biotechnology-
Wellcome Trust India Alliance, focuses on advancing neuromodulatory 
interventions for psychiatric conditions in India. 
xi. SURAKSHA Project: This community-based suicide prevention model, 
implemented in Channapatna Taluk, Karnataka, develops an indigenized 
framework for suicide prevention research and surveillance. 
xii. ENIGMA Initiative: In collaboration with the University of Southern 
California, this five-year study examines brain aging factors in 400 voluntary 
participants to assess risks for Alzheimer’s and related disorders in the Indian 
population. 
xiii. Centre for Brain and Mind: A pioneering initiative with NCBS and Rohini 
Nilekani Philanthropies, this centre builds on the ADBS project’s resources to 
study severe mental illnesses, including schizophrenia, bipolar disorder, OCD, 
dementia, and addiction. 
xiv. Tele-SWABHIMAN: This initiative, adopted in Telangana and Chhattisgarh 
in partnership with UNICEF, integrates maternal nutrition and mental health 
care for pregnant and postpartum women using community-led and gender-
transformative approaches. 
xv. Suicide Prevention (N-SPRITE): This newly launched suicide prevention 
centre employs a multi-disciplinary approach with evidence-based 
interventions, digital assessments, and healthcare provider training. The 
Department of Clinical Psychology at NIMHANS continues to contribute to 
mental health care through research, publications, and psychological 
assessments across the lifespan.

efficiency. 3.23.3  The Committee observes that all the five ongoing projects of RIMS, 
Imphal suffer from time overrun and one project under Package-I suffers from cost 
overrun of Rs. 21.4 crore also. The reasons for delay in completion of works includes 
COVID pandemic, Manipur violence, stoppage from local insurgents, Curfew, Internet 
suspension, road blockades & logistic issues, floods and landslides. The Committee 
desires that the Institute should strictly instructed the work agency-HSCC (India) Ltd. 

101 
 
to utilize more manpower and prepare a milestone for each of the projects in order to 
complete the work as per the revised timeframe and cost. 
 
3.23.4  Moreover, RIMS, Imphal, along with the executing agency HSCC (India) 
Ltd., must adopt a strict project management framework to mitigate delays in ongoing 
infrastructure projects. Given that all five projects have experienced time overruns, 
with one also facing a cost overrun, a path-breaking approach with strict monitoring 
should be enforced to yield desirable result. The work agency should be directed to 
mobilize additional resources, ensure close coordination with local authorities to address 
logistical and security concerns, and leverage technology for real-time tracking of 
project progress. Additionally, penalties for further delays and incentives for early 
completion should be considered to ensure adherence to revised timelines. 
 
3.23.5  RIMS, Manipur was upgraded to Regional Cancer Centre in 2006. On being 
asked about the problems being faced by departments related to cancer treatment in RIMS, 
the Committee has been apprised of the following: - 
i. There is inadequate manpower of Physicist and acute shortage of Radiotherapy 
Technologist, which are proposed to be included in the consolidated proposal  
ii. Inadequate posts of Faculties such as medical oncologist, Physicist, 
Technologist to meet increasing patient load  
iii. Absence of dedicated preventive oncology department  
iv. Absence of a dedicated Palliative Medicine department 
 
3.23.6  On a specific query, it has been brought to the knowledge of the Committee 
that the proposal for up gradation to Tertiary Cancer Care Centre was submitted by RIMS to 
the Ministry on 8th March 2014.  However, the proposal could not be materialized as 
contribution of 10% State share as required under the scheme was not forthcoming from the 
State Government. Other hurdles include lack of infrastructure and equipment such as Linear 
Accelerator, independent Departments such as Department of Medical and Surgical 
Oncology. 
 
3.23.7  Further, in reply to a query on sanctioned and in-position strength of the 
workforce in Institute, the Committee has come to know that there exist 613 vacancies 
(31.71% of the sanctioned strength of the staff in the institute) across different Groups of 
posts. In Group C posts, the vacant posts are 53.26% of the sanctioned strength in that 
category. The group-wise details of sanctioned posts, in-position strength and vacancies are

3.23.8  The Committee suggest an urgent workforce augmentation plan to fill the 
613 vacancies (31.71% of sanctioned posts), particularly in Group C, where over 53% of 
positions remain unfilled. Priority recruitment should be initiated for critical roles such 

as physicists, radiotherapy technologists, and oncologists to improve cancer treatment services. Furthermore, considering RIMS' failure to upgrade to a Tertiary Cancer Care Centre due to the State Government’s inability to contribute 10% of the required funds, alternative financial models, such as central funding relaxation or publicpartnerships, should be explored. strength) exist in the institute across all Groups of posts. The Committee expresses 
concern that substantial vacancies exists in key managerial positions of Group A posts 
calculating to 24 vacant posts (37.50% of the sanctioned strength). The Committee 
observes that high vacancy in staff position, particularly in crucial Group A managerial 
positions, impacts operational efficiency. The institute relies on a large number of 
contract staff, suggesting a need for permanent recruitment. The Committee, therefore, 
strongly recommends for expediting recruitment process to fill up the vacant posts, 
sooner the better, for efficient and effective working of the Institute. The Committee also 
urges the institution to prioritize the immediate filling up of vacant positions, 
particularly in Group A, to ensure effective leadership and management. A structured 
recruitment drive should be conducted, and processes streamlined to expedite hiring. 
 
3.24.7  Moreover, the Committee finds with concern that there is significant delay 
in the RIPANS Development Project with extensions needed beyond the initial 
completion date. The Committee, therefore, recommends formulating a detailed 
operational readiness plan for the 100-bed hospital. This plan should include clearly 
defined roles and responsibilities, a robust training program for new and existing staff, 
and a system for monitoring the quality of healthcare services. The Committee also 
urges upon the department to create a plan to convert the contract staff into regular 
staff members, where possible, to increase staff retention, and institutional knowledge.

The Committee highlights that there exist 53 vacant posts (16% of the sanctioned strength) in

(36.21% of the sanctioned strength).  
 
3.25.3  The Committee has been informed that the main challenges faced by the 
institute to cater for a large number of patient care are mainly the lesser number of posts than 
other institutes under the central government and a few posts are vacant despite repeated 
advertisements. To overcome these challenges institute has proposed for up-gradation of pay 
scales of the employees so that it can attract the newer recruits. A proposal for the creation of 
19 nos of essential categories of posts of super speciality departments and the revival of 7 nos 
of posts are under the consideration of the Ministry.  
 
3.25.4  The Committee observes with concern that the core issue relating to the 
institute is reduced budget allocation of Rs. 60 crore (further reduced to Rs. 50 crore at 
RE stage) in FY 2024-25 in comparison to RE 2023-24 of Rs. 72.53 crore and allocation 
of insufficient funds to the tune of Rs. 65 crore in FY 2025-26. Needless to say that this 
reduced budget allocation threatens the institute's ability to cover essential operational 
expenses. The Committee, therefore, recommends an immediate and thorough review of 
the Institute's budget allocation for FY 2025-26. Given the institute's documented 
essential financial requirements, including salaries, pensions, and critical operational 
expenses, the proposed allocation of Rs. 65 crore is deemed insufficient as stated in 
submission to the Committee. The Committee, therefore, strongly advises the Ministry 
of Finance to enhance the budget allocation to adequately meet the institute's financial 
requirements. All such financial estimation and enhancement should be based on a 
detailed analysis of the institute's expenditure patterns and projected operational costs, 
ensuring uninterrupted service delivery and compliance with financial obligations. This 
action is critical to prevent operational disruptions and maintain the institute's ability to 
provide essential healthcare services. 
 
3.25.5  The Committee further observes the significant number of vacant 
positions, particularly in Group A category. Moreover, there exist substantial vacancies 
of the post of Senior Residents (Contractual position). The Committee has also been 
apprised of the institute’s struggle to handle a large patient load due to staffing 
shortages. The Committee recommends the expedited approval and implementation of 
the institute's proposals for pay scale upgrades and the creation/revival of essential 
posts. Addressing the critical vacancies, particularly in Group A positions and Senior 
Resident roles, is paramount. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should prioritize the approval of the proposed 19 new super-specialty posts 
and the revival of the 7 existing posts. Additionally, the committee recommends that the 
institute, in conjunction with the Department, create a focused recruitment strategy that 
highlights the advantages of working at the institute, along with the approved pay scale 
improvements, to attract qualified candidates. A specific recruitment drive should be 
initiated to fill up the senior resident positions as quickly as possible. This combined 
approach of improved pay and targeted recruitment will help to fill up the critical 
staffing gaps, thereby enhancing the institute's capacity to deliver quality patient care. 

107 
 
CHAPTER IV 
 
NATIONAL HEALTH MISSION 
 
4.1 National Health Mission 
 
4.1.1   National Health Mission (NHM) aims for attainment of universal access to 
equitable, affordable and quality health care services, accountable and responsive to people’s 
needs, with effective inter-sectoral convergent action to address the wider social determinants of 
health. NHM enables the country to advance towards meeting Sustainable Development Goal 3, 
i.e. "Ensure healthy lives and promote well-being for all at all ages", including Universal Health 
Coverage. NHM consists of two Sub-Missions, the National Rural Health Mission (NRHM) and 
the National Urban Health Mission (NUHM).  
 
4.1.2   The major objectives of NHM are as under:  
 
(i) Reduction in child and maternal mortality.  
 
(ii) Prevention and control of communicable and non-communicable diseases, 
including locally endemic diseases.  
 
(iii) Access to integrated comprehensive primary health care.  
 
(iv) Population stabilisation, gender equality and demographic balance.  
 
(v) Universal access to public services for food and nutrition, sanitation and hygiene 
and universal access to public health care services with emphasis on services 
addressing women’s and children’s health and universal immunisation.  
 
(vi) Promotion of healthy life styles.  
 
4.1.3   The main programmatic components include Health System Strengthening in 
rural and urban areas, Reproductive-Maternal- Neonatal-Child and Adolescent Health 
(RMNCH+A), and Communicable and Non-Communicable Diseases. Under the NHM, 
technical and financial support is provided to State / UTs for strengthening of their healthcare 
systems based on the proposals submitted by them in their Programme Implementation Plans 
(PIPs).

4.2.3  The Committee has been informed that the NHM is operational from the 2021 
to 2026, i.e. upto 31.3.2026 with total outlay Rs. 1,65,356.63 crore for the 5 FYs during this 
period. The Department has already taken budget of Rs. 1,28,129.71 crore from FY 2021-22 
to FY 2024-25 and the balance, Rs. 37,226.92 has been proposed and allocated for BE in 
2025-26.  The budget increase for the FY 2025-26 in comparison to FY 2024-25 is 3.4%. The 
Department plans to utilize the fund in the manner as indicated in the table below:-

4.2.4  The Committee notes that the National Health Mission (NHM) aims to 
achieve universal access to equitable, affordable, and quality healthcare, aligning with 
Sustainable Development Goal 3. The NHM has two sub-missions, NRHM and NUHM, 
and its objectives include reducing child and maternal mortality, controlling diseases, 
ensuring access to primary healthcare, and promoting healthy lifestyles. The program 
has shown progress in several areas, such as achieving and sustaining Total Fertility 
Rate (TFR) at 2.0 and exceeding targets for Ayushman Arogya Mandirs and full 
immunization coverage. However, challenges remain in reducing Maternal Mortality 
Rate (MMR) and Infant Mortality Rate (IMR) to target levels, and achieving 90% of 
districts meeting TB case notification and treatment success targets. The Committee 
expresses concern on the fact that the MMR and IMR are still above the targeted levels 

112 
 
and, therefore, recommends the Department for a targeted and data-driven approach 
with focused intervention for maternal and infant mortality reduction. This should 
involve detailed micro-level analysis of the regions with the highest MMR and IMR, 
focusing on the specific factors contributing to these rates and chalking out sustained & 
resilient strategy to combat those factors so as to achieve TB elimination, MMR and 
IMR to target level within specified period.  
4.2.5  The Committee recommends the Department that NHM should prioritize 
increased funding and resources for these high-risk areas. Interventions should include 
strengthening facility-based newborn care, improving access to skilled birth attendants, 
enhancing prenatal and postnatal care, and addressing nutritional deficiencies in women 
and children. A feedback loop must be put in place to measure the effectiveness of the 
interventions and allow for quick adjustments. 
4.2.6  The Committee notes that budgetary allocations to NHM have generally 
seen increase in funds, but expenditure in FY 2024-25, as of February 7, 2025, stands out 
to be only 81.28% of the allocated amount. The operational period of NHM is five years 
i.e. upto FY 2026, with a total outlay of Rs. 1,65,356.63 crore from FY 2021 to FY 2026. 
The budget for FY 2025-26 is proposed to be Rs. 37,226.92 crore, a 3.4% increase from 
FY 2024-25. Recognizing the under-utilization of allocated funds in FY 2024-25, the 
Committee strongly recommends implementing a more robust monitoring and 
evaluation system to track expenditure and program implementation. This system 
should provide real-time data on fund utilization and identify bottlenecks in the 
disbursement and expenditure processes. The NHM should streamline administrative 
procedures, provide capacity building for state and district-level health officials in 
financial management, and ensure timely release of funds. The committee further 
recommends that the NHM provides reports quarterly regarding the budget utilization 
and provides the reasoning for any deficit in spending, along with plans to correct the 
deficit. This will allow for more efficient program implementation and ensure that 
allocated resources are effectively used to achieve the NHM's objectives. 
Key Concern Areas 
4.2.7  On a specific query, the Department apprised the Committee of the key 
concern areas identified in implementation of various programmes/components of NRHM and 
NUHM as follows: - 
(i) Health Infrastructure: As per established norms, in rural areas a Sub Health 
Centre (SHC) for a population of 5,000 (in plain) and 3000 (in hilly and tribal area), a 
Primary Health Centre (PHC) for a population of 30,000 (in plains) and 20,000 (in 
hilly and tribal areas) and Community Health Centre (CHC) for a population of 
1,20,000 (in plain) and 80,000 (in hilly and tribal area) is suggested. However, there is 
a gap in SHCs, PHCs and CHCs in State/UTs as referred in Health Dynamics of India 
(HDI) (Infrastructure & Human Resources), 2022-23. 
 
(ii) Human Resources: The shortage of doctors and health workers is exacerbated by 
an uneven distribution of healthcare professionals across states, leading to significant 
disparities in healthcare access and quality. Rural and remote areas are particularly 

113 
 
affected, often relying on a limited number of overburdened medical staff. The 
shortage extends to allied health workers and nurses, further straining the healthcare 
system. 
 
(iii) Lack of Adequate drugs and diagnostics services: There are still issues with 
the availability and distribution of essential drugs in public health facilities. This can 
lead to stockouts and delays in treatment. Also, there are still gaps in the availability 
and quality of diagnostic services for the patients. Drugs and Diagnostics services are 
not provided to poor people on reasonable rates. 
 
(iv) Lack of Service Delivery: The inefficient delivery of healthcare services 
significantly impacts accessibility and quality of care. There are some issue related to 
service delivery are Inadequate Workforce, Poor Supply Chain Management, 
Financial Barriers etc. Addressing these issues requires comprehensive reforms, 
including improved infrastructure, better workforce management, robust supply chain 
systems, increased public funding, standardization of care protocols, and enhanced 
monitoring and evaluation mechanisms. 
 
(v) Urban Rural Divide: The urban-rural divide in the health sector is a significant 
challenge in India, impacting healthcare access, quality, and outcomes. Urban areas 
generally have better healthcare infrastructure, including well-equipped hospitals, 
specialized medical centers, and private healthcare facilities. Urban centers attract 
more healthcare professionals due to better career opportunities, higher salaries, and 
improved living conditions. Whereas, rural areas often lack basic healthcare 
infrastructure, with fewer hospitals, limited medical equipment, and inadequate 
primary health centers. Rural areas face a shortage of healthcare professionals, 
including doctors, nurses, and allied health workers, leading to poor healthcare service 
delivery. 
 
(vi) Digital Portal: There are many portal working in the Ministry for monitoring of 
health services. This portal provides information related to health indicators of India, 
compiled from various sources such as the Health Management Information System 
(HMIS), National Family Health Survey (NFHS), and Census data. Now, all portal 
needs to be integrated. 
 
4.2.8  On being asked, the Department stated the following strategy that the 
Government is adopting to combat the above challenges: - 
 
i. Quality infrastructure and services- National Quality Assurance Standard 
(NQAS) lays down set of quality parameters for public health facilities. These 
standards are linked with well-defined assessment tools and identified gaps are 
traversed through continual quality improvement methods and tools. NQAS are 
currently available for District Hospitals, CHCs, PHCs and Urban PHCs. 
Standards are primarily meant for providers to assess their own quality for 
improvement through pre-defined standards and to bring up their facilities for 
certification. The NQASs are broadly arranged under 8 "Areas of Concern"– 
Service Provision, Patient Rights, Inputs, Support Services, Clinical Care, 
Infection Control, Quality Management and Outcome. The NQAS program aims 
to improve the credibility of public hospitals and recognize facilities that perform 
well. Pradhan Mantri Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM-
ABHIM) with an outlay of Rs. 64,180 Crores aims to support for infrastructure 

114 
 
development for Sub-Health Centres, Urban Health and Wellness Centres, Support 
for Block Public Health Units, Integrated District Public Health Laboratories and 
Critical Care Hospital Blocks. 15th Finance Commission has recommended grants 
aggregating to Rs. 70,051 Crores over the period of five years (2021-2026) 
through local government to strengthen the healthcare system in states. 
 
ii. HR availability- Financial support is provided to States under NHM to strengthen 
the health system including engagement of Nurses, doctors and specialist on 
contractual basis based on the appraisal of requirements proposed by the States in 
their annual Programme Implementation Plans. To increase the availability of 
doctors, several initiatives have been taken to rationalize the norms in medical 
education, such as, relaxation in land requirements, bed strength, increase in 
ceiling for maximum intake for undergraduates, enhancement of teacher-student 
ratio in PG etc which has resulted in substantial increase in number of 
undergraduate and post graduate seats. Government has also approved setting up 
of ANM/GNM Schools in different States besides setting up of Institutes of 
Paramedical Sciences at National and regional levels. In order to encourage the 
States to fill up existing vacancies in remote rural areas, the states are being 
incentivized to ensure rational deployment of health human resource. 
 
iii. Performance Based Incentives: The Government of India has taken number of 
initiatives in the form of incentives and honorarium to the medical professionals 
for encouraging better service delivery in rural and remote areas in the country, 
which include: 
 
a) Hard Area Allowance to specialist doctors for serving in rural and remote 
areas so that they find it attractive to serve in public health facilities in 
such areas. 
b) Honorarium to Gynecologists/ Emergency Obstetric Care (EmoC) 
trained, Pediatricians & Anesthetist/ Life Saving Anaesthesia Skills 
(LSAS) trained doctors to increase availability of specialists for 
conducting Cesarean Sections in rural & remote area. 
c) Special incentives for doctors, incentive for ANM for ensuring timely 
ANC checkup and recording, incentives for conducting Adolescent 
Reproductive and Sexual Health activities. 
d) States are also allowed to offer negotiable salary to attract specialist 
including flexibility in strategies such as “You Quote We Pay”. 
e) Non-monetary incentives such as preferential admission in postgraduate 
courses for staff serving in difficult areas and improving accommodation 
arrangement in rural areas have also been introduced under NHM. 
f) Multi-skilling of doctors is supported under NHM to overcome the 
shortage of specialists. Skill upgradation of existing HR is another major 
strategy under the National Rural Health Mission (NRHM) for achieving 
improvement in health outcomes. 
 
iv. Free Drugs Services Initiative-To ensure availability of essential drugs and 
reduce the Out of Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) of the patients visiting the public 
health facilities, Government has rolled out the Free Drugs Service Initiative under 
NHM. This includes financial support to States/UTs for 106 drugs at Sub-Health 
Centre (SHC) level, 172 at Primary Health Centre (PHC) level, 300 at Community 
Health Centre (CHC) level, 318 at Sub-District Hospitals (SDH) level and 381 

115 
 
drugs at district Hospitals.  
 
v. Free Diagnostics Services Initiative: This Ministry also supports ‘Free 
Diagnostics Service Initiative’ programme under NHM with the aim to provide 
accessible and affordable pathological and radiological diagnostics services closer 
to the community, which in turn reduces the OOPE. Diagnostics services are 
provided free of cost at all levels of public health facilities [14 tests at SHCs, 63 at 
PHCs, 97 at CHCs, 111 test at SDH and 134 tests at District Hospitals]. 
 
vi. Healthcare Services: The National Health Policy formulated in 2017 envisages to 
provide universal access to good quality healthcare services through increasing 
access, increasing affordability by lowering the cost of healthcare delivery and 
equity. The policy envisages as its goal the attainment of the highest possible level 
of health and well-being for all at all ages, through a preventive and promotive 
healthcare orientation in all developmental policies, and universal access to good 
quality healthcare services without anyone having to face financial hardship as a 
consequence. 
 
vii. Teleconsultation - Teleconsultation is expanding the reach to specialist care and 
enabling patients to connect with medical officers and specialists from remote 
locations. Teleconsultation platforms such as eSanjeevani and Tele MANAS 
facilitate real-time communication between patients and healthcare providers, 
allowing accurate and timely diagnosis, treatment planning, and follow-up care. 
This approach enhances the reach and quality of healthcare services, reduces Out-
Of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) for patients with the need of long-distance travel 
in remote areas, and ensures timely consultations. Additionally, teleconsultation 
supports capacity building among primary care providers particularly the 
Community Health Officers (CHOs) and Auxiliary Nurse and Midwife (ANM) at 
the AAM-SHC by enabling them to seek guidance from specialists for handling 
the patients independently in future. 
 
viii. Implementation of National Rural Health Mission: To address the healthcare 
challenges, particularly in rural areas, the National Rural Health Mission (NRHM) 
was launched in 2005 to supplement the efforts of the State/UT governments to 
provide accessible, affordable and quality healthcare to all those who access public 
health facilities. Currently, NRHM is a sub-mission of National Health Mission 
(NHM). NUHM was also launched in 2013 as a sub-mission of NHM. NHM 
support is provided to States/ UTs for setting up of new facilities as per norms and 
upgradation of existing facilities for bridging the infrastructure gaps based on the 
requirement posed by them. 
 
ix. Digital Interface and multiplicity of portal - Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission 
(ABDM) launched in September 2021, aims to create the world’s largest digital 
health ecosystem, supporting universal health coverage and developing the 
backbone necessary to support the integrated digital health infrastructure of the 
country. ABDM envisages to create a secure online platform based on open, 
interoperable digital standards to bridge the existing gap amongst different 
stakeholders of healthcare ecosystem through digital highways. 
 

116 
 
4.2.9  The Committee having micro-analysis of the identified challenges 
(infrastructure gaps, human resource shortages, drug and diagnostic deficiencies, 
service delivery issues, urban-rural divide, and fragmented digital portals) and the 
government's current strategies (NQAS, PM-ABHIM, HR incentives, free drug and 
diagnostic initiatives, tele-consultation, and ABDM), strongly recommends the urgent 
and prioritized establishment of a unified, real-time, and interoperable national health 
data platform. This platform should integrate all existing health portals, including 
HMIS, NFHS, ABDM, and others, to provide a comprehensive, longitudinal view of 
health infrastructure, human resources, drug and diagnostic availability, service 
delivery metrics, and patient outcomes across all levels (rural and urban). This unified 
system will enable data-driven decision-making, facilitate efficient resource allocation, 
streamline service delivery, enhance monitoring of program implementation, and 
ultimately bridge the urban-rural divide by providing equitable access to healthcare 
information and services. The Committee believes that the implementation of these steps 
would maximize the impact of ongoing programs and enhance the credibility of public 
hospitals. 
Reproductive, Maternal, Newborn, Child, Adolescent Health and Nutrition (RMNCAH 
+ N)  
4.2.10  The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare supports all States/UTs in 
implementation of Reproductive, Maternal, Newborn, Child, Adolescent Health and Nutrition 
(RMNCAH + N) strategy under National Health Mission (NHM) based on the Annual 
Program Implementation Plan (APIP) submitted by States / UTs. The Department submitted 
the information on new policies or programs in place to improve maternal and child 
healthcare in 2025-26 as follows: - 
A. New programs to improve maternal healthcare across the country:- 
 
i. Pradhan Mantri Surakshit Matritva Abhiyan (PMSMA) provides pregnant 
women a fixed day, free of cost assured and quality antenatal check up by a 
Specialist/Medical Officer on the 9th day of every month. Extended PMSMA 
strategy ensures quality antenatal care (ANC) to pregnant women, especially to high-
risk pregnant (HRP) women and individual HRP tracking until a safe delivery is 
achieved by means of financial incentivization for the identified high-risk pregnant 
women and accompanying ASHA for extra 3 visits over and above the PMSMA visit. 
 
The number of beneficiaries benefited during last three years:

laying emphasis on detection of danger signs in mothers and incentivization of

periodic Regional and State Level Review meetings and field visits in various 
States/UTs including Common Review Mission. 
b) Communications through multiple channels with State Governments including 
letters, video conferencing, etc. 
c) Information Education & Communication (IEC) & Behaviours Change

d) Budget provision in Program Implementation Plan (PIP) under National Health 
Mission (NHM) to all States/UTs.  
 
B. The interventions for Child survival are as below: 
 
i) Neonatal Intensive Care Units (NICUs)/Special New-born Care Units (SNCUs) are 
established at Medical College and District Hospital, New-born Stabilization Units 
(NBSUs) are established at First Referral Units (FRUs)/ Community Health Centres 
(CHCs) under Facility Based Newborn Care(FBNC) Program to provide specialized 
care to sick and small newborns at district and sub-district levels, addressing neonatal 
health complications. Mother Newborn Care Units (MNCUs) are established as a new 
initiative with the aim of ‘zero separation’ of mother and baby including small and 
sick babies who require newborn care. As of now, 150 new MNCUs have been 
approved.

4.2.11  The Committee acknowledges the initiatives taken by the Government to 
improve maternal healthcare like PMSMA and extended PMSMA for antenatal care, 
showing a significant increase in beneficiaries, and Optimizing Postnatal Care with 
ASHA incentivisation, showing stable post-partum check-up numbers. The Committee 
recommends strengthening the Optimizing Postnatal Care program by focusing on 
targeted training for ASHAs in the early detection of danger signs in postpartum 
mothers. While postpartum check-up numbers are stable, the Committee suggests 
implementing a digital tracking system to monitor the individual progress of high-risk 
postpartum mothers, similar to the extended PMSMA's approach for high-risk 
pregnancies. This system should integrate with existing digital health platforms to 
ensure seamless data flow and timely intervention. The Committee also recommends the 
Department to conduct specific audits of the post-partum care, to assess the quality of 
the care that is given. This will ensure that the ASHAs are correctly identifying the 
danger signs, and that the mothers are getting the needed care. 
4.2.12  The Committee takes into account the initiatives taken by the Department 
to improve child survival like Facility-Based Newborn Care (FBNC) through 
NICUs/SNCUs/NBSUs and new MNCUs, with increasing newborn treatment numbers; 
Home-Based Newborn Care (HBNC) and Home-Based Care of Young Children (HBYC) 
by ASHAs, with fluctuating but generally high coverage; Diarrhea campaigns (ORS and 
Zinc), showing consistent coverage; and Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakram (RBSK) 
for screening and treatment, demonstrating significant growth in children screened and 
treated. The Committee recommends the Department to make public awareness 
campaign about these programmes in rural, semi-urban and tribal & hilly areas. The 
Committee desires that the Department should assess the success rate of these 
programmes in underserved areas and after taking stock of ground realities launch 
special drive to achieve the cherished objectives in stated areas.    
4.2.13  The Committee recommends prioritizing the strengthening of District 
Early Intervention Centres (DEICs) to match the significant increase in children 
screened and treated under the Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakram (RBSK). While 
screening numbers are rising, ensuring timely and effective treatment at DEICs is 
crucial. The Committee suggests a comprehensive review of DEIC functionality, 
including staffing, equipment, and referral systems. Based on the review, the 
Department should provide targeted support to DEICs, including increased funding, 
training for personnel, and streamlined referral pathways. This will ensure that children 
identified through RBSK receive the necessary interventions promptly, maximizing the 
program's impact on child health outcomes. Furthermore, the committee suggests that 
the Department create a system of feedback from the families that have used the DEIC 
services, so that the DEIC's can be improved based on the users feedback.

121 
 
4.3.3 The Committee recommends the urgent implementation of a proactive, 
structured engagement strategy with all States and Union Territories well in advance of 
each fiscal year's budget cycle to enhance the demand for the Strengthening of State 
Drug Regulatory System (SSDRS) scheme. This strategy should focus on providing clear 
guidelines, technical assistance, and regular communication to ensure that States 
understand the SSDRS scheme's requirements, and to assist States in developing 
comprehensive, eligible proposals. This process should also involve regular monitoring 
of fund utilization by states, providing early feedback and support to address any 
bottlenecks hindering the 75% utilization requirement. Given the scheme's demand-
driven nature and the observed pattern of reduced allocations due to insufficient or non-
compliant state proposals, actively facilitating state participation will mitigate the risk of 
underutilization and ensure the crucial strengthening of drug regulatory systems, 
particularly as the scheme's extension is being considered. Specifically, the department 
should establish a dedicated team to conduct workshops, provide templates for 
proposals, and track state progress, thereby ensuring that the allocated funds are 
effectively utilized to improve drug quality and accessibility across the nation. 
4.3.4 On being asked about the steps that can be taken to further strengthen the State Drug 
Regulation system, the Department submitted that during September 16 to 20, 2024 a team of 
international experts from various countries, led by WHO (HQ) in Geneva, has conducted a 
comprehensive and in-depth scientific review of India’s vaccine regulatory system. Safety, 
efficacy, and quality are three basic parameters of assessment of vaccines. The WHO NRA 
re-benchmarking was aimed to assess and document the status of the India regulatory system 
in the area of vaccine regulation, re-benchmark the status of the India vaccine regulatory 
system against the WHO NRA Global Benchmarking Tool (GBT) and measuring the maturity 
of the system. India has been declared ‘functional’ against all the core regulatory functions of 
the WHO Global Benchmarking Tool Version VI. India retains Maturity Level 3 with highest 
marks in several functions. India being pharmaceutical hub of the world the same level of 
benchmarking/ capacity building of States/UTs in the field of medicines/drugs is the need of 
the hour. This requires regulatory capacity building up gradation of the States in 
medicines/drugs/API /formulations. Besides, State audit will be conducted in a challenging 
mode to encourage the States/UTs to develop regulatory capability as per international 
standard like WHO/ICH. Consultation will be organized with all stakeholders for identifying 
the components for such activities and necessary approval will be obtained from the 
competent authority. The Department submitted that the following steps have been taken to 
strengthen the Drugs Regulation System: 
 
i. In order to assess the regulatory compliance of drug manufacturing premises in 
the country, the Central Drugs Standard Control Organization (CDSCO) along 
with State Drugs Controllers (SDCs) had initiated risk-based inspections of Drug 
manufacturing firms from Dec 2022. Risk-based inspections of more than 500 
premises have been conducted so far. Drug manufacturing firms have been 
identified based on risk criteria like number of drugs declared as Not of Standard 
Quality, complaints, criticality of the products etc. Based on findings of 
inspections, more than 400 actions like issuance of show cause notices, stop 
production order, suspension, cancellation of licenses /product licenses etc., have 

122 
 
been taken by the State Licensing Authorities as per the provisions of the Drugs 
Rules 1945.  
 
ii. Central Government has amended the Drugs Rules 1945 vide G.S.R. 922 (E)  
dated 28.12.2023 to revise the schedule M to the said rules related to Good 
Manufacturing Practices and requirements of premises, plant and equipment for 
pharmaceutical products. Revised Schedule M has become effective for the drug 
manufacturers with turnover> Rs. 250 crores from 29.06.2024. However, for 
manufacturers having turnover of less than Rs. 250 Cr, draft rules have been 
published vide GSR 10(E) dated 04.01.2025 regarding extension of timelines till 
31st December, 2025. 
 
iii. On 18.01.2022, the Drugs Rules, 1945 were amended vide G.S.R. 20 (E) 
providing that every Active Pharmaceutical Ingredient (bulk drug) manufactured 
or imported in India shall bear Quick Response Code on its label at each level of 
packaging that store data or information readable with software application to 
facilitate tracking and tracing. The stored data or information shall include the 
minimum particulars including unique product identification code, Batch Number, 
Manufacturing date, Expiry Date etc. 
 
iv. On 11.02.2020, the Drugs Rules, 1945 were amended vide G.S.R. 101 (E), 
providing that with effect from 01.03.2021, any marketer who sells or distributes 
any drug shall be responsible for quality of that drug as well as other regulatory 
compliances along with the manufacturer under these Rules. 
 
v. The Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940 was amended under Drugs & Cosmetics 
(Amendment) Act, 2008 to provide stringent penalties for manufacture of 
spurious and adulterated drugs. Certain offences have also been made cognizable 
and non-bailable. 
 
vi. Central regulator coordinates activities of State Drug Control Organizations and 
provides expert advice through the Drugs Consultative Committee (DCC) 
meetings held with State Drugs Controllers for uniformity in administration of the 
Drugs and Cosmetics Act. 
 
vii. Central government is providing regular residential, regional training and 
workshops to officials of CDSCO and State Drug Regulatory Authorities on Good 
Manufacturing Practices. In the Financial Year 2023-24 CDSCO has trained 
22854 persons while in Financial Year 2024-25, so far 13007 persons have been 
trained. 
 
In addition to the above, the Department also apprised the Committee that CDSCO has 
developed and launched Online National Drug Licensing System (ONDLS) portal for 
submission of applications and issuance of licenses by the States through this portal. Central 
regulator coordinates activities of State Drug Control Organisations and provides expert 
advice through the Drugs Consultative Committee (DCC) meetings held with State Drugs 
Controllers for uniformity in administration of the Drugs and Cosmetics Act. 
 
4.3.5  To ensure a consistently high standard of drug regulation across India, 
mirroring the success achieved in vaccine regulation, the Committee recommends the 
immediate implementation of a comprehensive, nationwide, and phased "Medicines 

123 
 
Regulatory Maturity Enhancement Program." This program should be modelled after 
the WHO NRA re-benchmarking process used for vaccines, involving a detailed 
scientific review of state and union territory drug regulatory systems against the WHO 
Global Benchmarking Tool. It should include: i) mandatory, challenging-mode state 
audits conducted by a central team of international and national experts, focusing on the 
quality, safety, and efficacy of medicines, APIs, and formulations; ii) targeted regulatory 
capacity building and infrastructure upgrades in states/UTs, informed by the audit 
findings and stakeholder consultations; iii) the establishment of clear, time-bound 
milestones for achieving and maintaining higher maturity levels, with progressive 
implementation based on the turnover of the manufacturers as done with GMP 
revisions; iv) continuous professional development and training for state drug 
regulatory officials, leveraging existing CDSCO training programs and expanding to 
incorporate international best practices; and v) the integration of the Online National 
Drug Licensing System (ONDLS) with the benchmarking process to track and monitor 
progress, thereby ensuring uniformity and efficiency in drug regulation across the 
country. This systematic approach, coupled with stringent enforcement measures like 
risk-based inspections and amended Drugs Rules, will elevate the entire drug regulatory 
framework to international standards, safeguarding public health and reinforcing 
India's position as a global pharmaceutical leader. 
4.3.6  The Committee is of the view that India being pharmaceutical hub of the 
world, the same level of benchmarking/ capacity building of States/UTs in the field of 
medicines/drugs is the need of the hour. This requires regulatory capacity building up-
gradation of the States in medicines/drugs/API /formulations. The Committee welcomes 
the launching of Online National Drug Licensing System (ONDLS) portal for 
submission of applications and issuance of licenses by the States through this portal. 
4.4 FUNCTIONING OF CENTRAL DRUGS STANDARD CONTROL 
ORGANIZATION (CDSCO) 
 
4.4.1  The Central Drugs Standard Control Organization (CDSCO) is the Central 
Drug Authority for discharging functions assigned to the Central Government under the 
Drugs and Cosmetics Act. CDSCO functions under the Directorate General of Health 
Services. CDSCO has six zonal offices, four sub-zonal offices, 13 port offices and seven 
laboratories under its control. The major functions of CDSCO include regulatory control over 
the import of drugs, approval of new drugs and clinical trials, meetings of Drugs Consultative 
Committee (DCC) and Drugs Technical Advisory Board (DTAB), approval of certain licenses 
as Central License Approving Authority (exercised by the CDSCO headquarters). 
 
4.4.2  Under the Drug and Cosmetics Act, the regulation of manufacture, sale and 
distribution of Drugs is primarily the concern of the State authorities while the Central 
Authorities are responsible for approval of New Drugs, Clinical Trials in the country, laying 
down the standards for Drugs, control over the quality of imported Drugs, coordination of the 
activities of State Drug Control Organisations and providing expert advice with a view of 
bring about the uniformity in the enforcement of the Drugs and Cosmetics Act. Drug 

Controller General of India is responsible for approval of licenses of specified categories of Drugs such as blood and blood products, I. V. Fluids, Vaccine and Sera.  
4.4.3  On being asked about the basis of classification of medical devices in different categories, the Department submitted that medical devices other than medical devices are classified based on parameters specified in Part I of the First Scthe following classes, viz. (i) low risk moderate high risk- Class C; and (iv) high riskmedical devices are classified based on parameters specified in Part II of the First Schedule, in the following classes, viz. (i) low risk moderate high risk- Class C; (iv) high riskthe methodology of classification is like the guidelines published by the International Medical Devices Regulatory Form (IMDRF) which is managed by members of Australia, Russia, Brazil, Singapore, Canada, South Korea,States of America, and Japan. India became an affiliate member of IMDRF in 2024.  4.4.4  On a specific queryunder the medical devices category who havthe Department stated that the applicants/complaints/entities under the medical devices category who have applied for import license including the date of applbeing maintained in the format it being requested for. a tabulated statement of the date of application/ date of first query raised / date of second queries and subsequent queries raised / date of reply submitted/ date of still further queries along with reasons for the query raised / date of issue of import license issued, maintained in the format it is requested for. 
124 
Controller General of India is responsible for approval of licenses of specified categories of Drugs such as blood and blood products, I. V. Fluids, Vaccine and Sera.  
On being asked about the basis of classification of medical devices in different categories, the Department submitted that medical devices other than in-vitromedical devices are classified based on parameters specified in Part I of the First Sc(i) low risk - Class A; (ii) low moderate risk- Class B; (iii) Class C; and (iv) high risk- Class D. Similarly, in-vitromedical devices are classified based on parameters specified in Part II of the First Schedule, (i) low risk - Class A; (ii) low moderate risk- Class C; (iv) high risk- Class D. The Department, further, submitted that the methodology of classification is like the guidelines published by the International Medical Devices Regulatory Form (IMDRF) which is managed by members of Australia, Russia, Brazil, Singapore, Canada, South Korea, China, United Kingdom, European Union, United States of America, and Japan. India became an affiliate member of IMDRF in 2024. 125 
 
 
4.4.5  On a specific query that whether different timelines have been adhered to 
exactly similar product being issued import licenses for different applicants, the Department 
submitted that there may be different timelines in processing of applications of similar 
category of medical devices. Each application is a distinct application and is processed based 
on completeness of the documents required to be submitted and queries are raised based on 
the information provided in the application. A generalization with regard to exactly same 
product cannot be drawn since CDSCO ensures safety, efficacy and quality of medical 
devices being manufactured or imported in the country.  
 
4.4.6  On a query related to number of levels at which the file is processed for issue 
of licenses for medical devices and at what levels maximum delays have been observed, the 
Department replied that there is a delegation of authority for issues of licenses for medical 
devices. Licenses for Class A and B medical devices are issued by State Licensing authority 
concerned and Class C and D by CDSCO. Initially all Class A medical devices were under 
licensing regime. Chapter III B was introduced in the Rules for registration instead of 
licensing of class A non-sterile and non-measuring medical device vide GSR 777 (E) dated 14 
October 2022. This has eliminated the licensing requirements for a large part of Class A 
devices. Now, license is not needed for this category.  
 
4.4.7  The Department also made a submission that India has been accepted as an 
affiliate member of IMDRF in 2024 which is a collaborative group of global medical device 
regulators dedicated to accelerating the harmonization and convergence of international 
medical device regulations, based on a detailed study of process of the Indian NRA. This will 
give an impetus to the effort to make India a manufacturing power house for medical devices. 
Further, the Department apprised the Committee that CDSCO has implemented MD Online 
which is an online portal for submission of the various applications related to manufacturing 
and import of medical devices and their clinical investigation to CDSCO in India. MD Online 
provides paperless and completely faceless grant of various clearances by CDSCO. 
  
4.4.8  The Committee is having the concerns that centralization of authority at 
any level may result in creating a situation for arbitrary/ discretionary decision making. 
This may have an adverse impression that India is keen on over regulation since last two 
years to create a business of licensing rather than making it a manufacturing 
powerhouse for medical devices. The Department should ensure faster documentation 
processes including reduction of human interface to facilitate the growth of medical 
devices industry in the country. The Committee is of the view that the Department 
should promote start-ups in medical devices by streamlining and overhauling the 
licensing system and preventing the deliberate delays by following a transparent process 
with minimum room for discretion/human inference. The Committee believes that these 
steps would result into India becoming a hub of the medical devices’ manufacturing and 
alone generating an over one trillion dollar industry in medical devices.  
 
4.4.9  The Department apprised the Committee that the Medical Devices Rules, 2017 
have comprehensively revised the medical device regulations with emphasis on ease of doing 

126 
 
business while ensuring the safety and efficacious quality of medical devices. Medical 
devices are regulated under the Medical Devices Rules, 2017 (MDR, 2017), which prescribe 
the specific documents required for the grant of a license to ensure the quality, safety and 
performance of medical devices including in-vitro diagnostic devices being manufactured or 
imported in the country. Accordingly, applicants need to submit the requisite documents as 
outlined in the said rules. For, an application that has been completed in all respects with all 
requisite documents as per MDR 2017, the license is being issued well before the stipulated 
timeline prescribed in MDR 2017. However, in cases where the required documents are found 
deficient, compliance is ensured with MDR, 2017 by giving opportunity to the applicants. 
Also, in the said Rules provision is there that if a free sale certificate has already been issued 
in respect of any medical device by the national regulatory authority or other competent 
authority of any of the countries namely, Australia, Canada, Japan, European Union 
Countries, or the United States of America, a licence can be granted to the applicant without 
carrying out clinical investigation. Further, to streamline the regulation various measures have 
been taken as under: 
 
(i) A single window system, the MD Online portal has been developed for 
submission, processing, and approval of various licenses in a time-bound manner 
(ii) In accordance with MDR 2017, phase-wise regulation of all medical devices has 
been implemented to ensure the safety, efficacy and quality of medical devices. 
(iii) Initially all Class A medical devices were under the licensing regime. Chapter III 
B was introduced in the Rules for registration instead of licensing of class A non-
sterile and non-measuring medical devices vide GSR 777 (E) dated 14 October 
2022. This has eliminated the licensing requirements for a large part of Class A 
devices. A license is not needed for this category. 
(iv) Provision introduced in the Rules for a separate Registration certificate to sell, 
stock, exhibit or offer for sale or distribute a medical device including in vitro 
diagnostic medical device vide GSR 754(E) dated 30th September 2022 
(v) In light of the recent development of bringing all medical devices under 
regulation, a large number of applications were received. To address such issues, a 
Medical Device Vertical has been created separately, Recruitment Rules published 
vide GSR 689 (E) dated 06.11.2024 for the post of Drugs Inspector (Medical 
Devices), Assistant Drugs Controller (I), Deputy Drugs Controller (I), Joint Drugs 
Controller (I), and Additional Drugs Controller (I). The Recruitment process has 
already been initiated to fill such positions. 
(vi) The Rules also provide for the registration of Medical Devices Testing 
Laboratories which are responsible for testing medical devices on behalf of 
manufacturers. Currently, 73 laboratories are registered with CDSCO for this 
purpose. Also, CDSCO has registered 14 notified bodies to perform their duties 
and functions concerning Class A and Class B medical devices.  
(vii) Various guidance documents, Frequently Asked Questions(FAQ), specimen 
proforma for submission of applications, and checklists for various applications 
have been published for transparency and ease of document submission by the 
applicants.  

127 
 
(viii) Trainings are conducted on the regulatory process and stakeholder meetings are 
held periodically.  
(ix) Dedicated public relations offices are established in CDSCO HQ and zonal offices 
with a mandate to act as an interface between CDSCO and its stakeholders, guide 
stakeholders on regulatory pathway including startups/new entrepreneurs, for 
disposal of grievances of stakeholders by addressing issues including at the level 
of Drugs Controller General of India. 
 
4.4.10  The Committee, on examination of the submissions made by the 
Department, expresses concerns that the CDSCO's current licensing process is plagued 
by delays, inconsistent timelines, and a lack of transparency, hindering the growth of the 
medical device industry. The Committee expresses serious concern that due to rigid 
process and inordinate delays by CDSCO, a large number of manufacturing units have 
gone to Vietnam and Malaysia.  Rather, several medical devices manufacturers are 
scared to set up a unit in India due to delaying tactics of the CDSCO.  The Committee 
recommends the implementation of a fully digitized, time-bound, and trackable licensing 
system. This system must include: (a) clearly defined timelines for each stage of the 
licensing process, with automatic notifications to applicants; (b) a standardized, publicly 
accessible database detailing all applications, queries, and approvals, allowing for real-
time tracking; (c) the utilization of AI and automated systems to pre-screen applications 
for completeness, reducing the need for repeated queries and human intervention, 
especially for globally certified, non-invasive products; (d) a system that records all 
queries and the reason for the query along with the date of the query and the date of the 
reply. The Committee is of the belief that this will allow for easy tracking of the process, 
and will allow for analysis of where the process is being slowed. This will help to identify 
bottlenecks. This will promote transparency, reduce discretionary decision-making, and 
foster a more efficient and predictable regulatory environment.  
 
4.4.11  The Committee further observes that the frequent and delayed queries 
raised by CDSCO are a major obstacle for applicants, particularly startups and 
entrepreneurs. The Committee recommends a significant overhaul of the query process. 
This includes: (a) establishing a "single query" policy, where all necessary queries are 
consolidated and raised at once, rather than in multiple rounds; (b) implementing a 
system of conditional approvals within 45 days, based on self-declaration and 
submission of necessary documentation, especially for products with existing 
international certifications (e.g., CE, US FDA); (c) providing detailed, standardized 
guidelines and FAQs to minimize ambiguity and reduce the need for queries; (d) 
creating a dedicated support team to assist applicants with the query process and 
provide timely clarifications. The Committee believes that this approach will expedite 
the approval process, encourage innovation, and allow businesses to adapt to the rapidly 
evolving technology landscape. 
 
4.4.12  The Committee acknowledges that the CDSCO faces challenges related to 
capacity and expertise, contributing to delays and inconsistencies in the licensing 
process. The Committee opines the acceleration of the recruitment of qualified 

128 
 
personnel, particularly Drugs Inspectors and other key positions in the Medical Device 
Vertical; and exploring the lateral entry of professionals from the medical device 
industry to bring in specialized expertise and a more industry-friendly perspective. The 
Committee urges upon the Department to implement a system of regular, mandatory 
training programs for CDSCO officials to enhance their understanding of the latest 
technologies, regulatory best practices, and their role as facilitators of industry growth; 
and implement a system of rotating officials to prevent the development of vested 
interests and ensure a fresh perspective. The Committee believes that these measures 
will strengthen CDSCO's capabilities, improve efficiency, and foster a more 
collaborative relationship with the industry.  
 
4.4.13  The Committee would like to express that the perception of CDSCO as a 
"license raj" highlights a critical need for improved communication and collaboration 
with the medical device industry. The Committee, therefore, recommends the 
establishment of an independent Industry Advisory Board, comprising representatives 
from manufacturers (including startups), importers, healthcare providers, and experts. 
This board would provide regular feedback to CDSCO on regulatory challenges and 
potential improvements; participate in the development of new guidelines and policies; 
and serve as a platform for resolving disputes and addressing industry concerns. 
Furthermore, CDSCO should implement a system of regular stakeholder consultations, 
including open forums, workshops, and online platforms, to gather feedback and 
address concerns proactively. This will foster a more transparent and collaborative 
regulatory environment, build trust between CDSCO and the industry, and ensure that 
regulations are aligned with the needs of a rapidly evolving sector, moving CDSCO from 
a perceived roadblock to a true facilitator. This will also create a clear channel for 
industry concerns to be heard and addressed, and allow for a more dynamic and 
responsive regulatory approach.

4.5.1  Tertiary Care Programs provides for transfer to states for implementation of 
Tertiary Care Programme/ Schemes viz. Tobacco Control, National Mental Health 
Programme, Assistance for Capacity Building for Trauma Centres, National Programme for 
Prevention and Control of Cancer, Diabetes, Cardiovascular Diseases and Stroke, Health Care 
for the Elderly, National Programme for Blindness and Tele medicine.
