4.5.4  In the FY 2023-24, the BE amounting to Rs. 289.62 crore was slightly 
reduced to Rs. 276.39 crore at RE stage, however, the actual expenditure remained Rs. 
258.83 crore. The Committee observes with concern over low trend of underutilization 
of funds, even during the FY 2024-25, out of the allocation of Rs. 322.90 crore at RE 
stage, only Rs. 170.08 crore till 31.1.2025 have been utilised which translates into just 
52.67% utilisation of fund. The Committee wishes to highlight the fund utilization 
within the National Mental Health Programme (12.46% of RE 2024-25 till 31.01.2025), 
National Programme for Prevention and Management of Trauma & Burn Injury 
(35.05% of RE 2024-25 till 31.01.2025), National Programme for prevention & Control 
of Non-Communicable Diseases (45.60% of RE 2024-25 till 31.01.2025) and Health Care 
for the Elderly (1.61% of RE 2024-25 till 31.01.2025) where the percentage utilization is 
below 50%. The Committee also acknowledges that the National Tobacco Control 
Programme & National Drugs De-addiction Programme have achieved 100% of the RE 
which shows that this program is working well.  
4.5.5  The Committee is of the opinion that the BE for 2025-26, amounting to Rs. 
483.54 crore, shows a substantial increase, indicating a commitment to these programs, 
however, this increased allocation will be wasted if the fund utilization issues are not 
addressed. The Committee recommends a detailed, program-specific review of the 
factors contributing to the significant underutilization of funds. This review should 
include analyzing the specific bottlenecks in fund disbursement and program 
implementation at the state level; conducting on-site assessments of program activities 

131 
 
and infrastructure; implementing a real-time monitoring system with clear performance 
indicators to track fund utilization and program progress; providing targeted technical 
assistance and capacity-building support to states with low utilization rates; and 
specifically for the NMHP, the Department should investigate why the money allocated 
for the Centers of Excellence, and PG programs are not being used. This proactive 
approach will ensure that the increased budget allocation for 2025-26 translates into 
tangible improvements in service delivery. 
4.5.6  The Department apprised the Committee of achievements made under the 
components of TCPs as under: - 
(i) Strengthening of Tertiary Cancer Care Centres Facilities Scheme: 
 
To enhance the facilities for tertiary care of cancer, the Central Government is implementing 
‘Strengthening of Tertiary Cancer Care Centres Facilities’ Scheme. Under the scheme, 
support is provided to States/UTs for setting up of State Cancer Institutes (SCIs) and Tertiary 
Care Cancer Centres (TCCCs) in different parts of the country. The financial assistance is for 
procurement of radio therapy equipment, diagnostic equipment, surgical equipment, 
enhancement of indoor civil work and patient facility for cancer and such other purposes 
relevant for diagnosis, treatment and care of cancer. The maximum permissible assistance for 
SCI is Rs. 120 crores and for TCCC Rs. 45 crores. This is inclusive of State share of 40% (for 
North East and Hill States 10%). Upto a maximum of 30% of the sanctioned amount will be 
permitted to be used for civil/electrical work (including renovation), and improvement of 
infrastructure. Till date 39 institutions (19 SCI and 20 TCCC) have been approved.  
 
(ii) Tabaco Control Programme & Drugs De-addiction Programme (DDAP) - Under 
National Tobacco Control Programme (NTCP), setting up of a tobacco cessation centres in 
district hospitals across the country. Under DDAP, improved availability of treatment 
facilities for those affected by alcohol and drug use disorders (addictions). The Programme 
also aims for building the capacities of health care staff in recognition and management of 
substance use disorders. 
 
(iii) National Mental Health Programme (NMHP) - The objectives of NMHP are as 
under:  
 
a) To increase the availability of trained manpower in the field of mental health care by 
creating PG level seats in the four mental health disciplines viz. (a) Psychiatry (b) 
Clinical Psychology (c) Psychiatric Nursing (d) Psychiatric Social Work.  
 
b) To enhance the capacity of tertiary care mental health facilities by physical 
infrastructure development and increasing the availability of specialized human 
resource;  
 
c) To integrate delivery of mental health services in healthcare system at district, sub 
district, health and wellness centres and sub centres;  
 
d) To generate knowledge and evidence through research on issues related with mental 
health. 
 

132 
 
The Government is implementing the National Mental Health Programme in the country since 
1982.  The Programme was revised in 1996 to include the District Mental Health Programme 
(DMHP).  Subsequently, the Manpower Development Scheme was added to the programme 
in 2009 with the following components: - 
 
A. Manpower Development Schemes: 
 
a) Centre of Excellence (Scheme-A): Scheme-A includes financial support 
towards construction, technical & non-technical equipment’s, library and faculty 
salary to the existing Central and State Mental health Institutions. The Scheme was 
initiated in the year 2009 and continued during the 12th Five Year Plan period. 11 
Centres of Excellence were established during the 11th Plan period with financial 
assistance of Rs. 30 crore per Centre. For 12th Plan, 10 more Centres of Excellence 
approved with financial assistance up to Rs. 33.70 crores per centre. Beyond the 12th 
Five Year Plan, four more Centre of Excellence approved. A total of 25 Centres of 
Excellence have been established/ approved till date. 
 
b) Strengthening/Establishment of PG departments in mental health 
specialties (Scheme-B): Scheme-B includes financial support towards capital works 
and faculty support to the existing Central and State Mental health Institutions for 
strengthening/establishment of Post Graduate Departments in mental health specialties 
viz. Psychiatry, Clinical Psychology, Psychiatric Social Work and Psychiatric Nurse. 
The Scheme was initiated in the year 2009 and continued during the 12th Five Year 
Plan period. 27 Post Graduate Departments were established during the 11th Plan 
period. Strengthening/establishment of 13 more PG Departments was approved with 
financial assistance in the range of Rs. 0.85 crores to Rs. 0.99 crores per Department 
during 12th Plan. Beyond the 12th Five Year Plan, 7 additional PG Departments have 
been approved for strengthening/establishment. A total of 47 PG Departments have 
been established/ approved till date in 19 institutions. 
 
(iv) National Programme for Prevention and Management of Trauma & Burn Injury 
(NPPMT&BI):- The targets sets for the program for the year 2024-25 inter-alia include 
making 15 each Trauma Care Facilities and Burn Units functional; Capacity Building of 
Medical / Paramedical professional; and Conduct workshops related to Strengthening of 
Trauma and Burn Care.  
 
(v) Programme for Strengthening eHealth & Telemedicine Services (PSETS) 
 
a) eSanjeevani- Platform of Telemedicine called eSanjeevani, being handled by eHealth 
division is already developed and matured over 34.18cr consultations in 131133 
spokes and 16931 hubs has been done. Besides 629 online OPDs are functional. 
Further strengthening of this is undertaken through integration with other projects 
such as ABHA integration, ABDM M1, M2 and M3 integration, BHASHINI 
integration for multiple Indian languages, Tele MANAS integration, etc. 
b) SAKSHAM- To strengthen the structure and content of online learning and training 
platform 
c) National Medical College Network (NMCN)- to enhance e-Education , e-CME and  e-
Healthcare delivery. 

133 
 
d) HMIS-Referral Modules for Continuum of Care, Operational Management Modules 
and Platform Migration for better implementation and effective functioning of e-
Hospital would be executed with the help of Next Gen. 
4.5.7  Given the importance of Tertiary care programs the Committee is of view 
that there has been considerable rise in mental health issues in the country. The 
Committee, accordingly, recommends the Department to take effective measures to 
increase the outreach of mental healthcare services and integrate psychological support 
services into primary health care. Further, considering the prevalence of non 
communicable diseases, the Committee feels that concerted efforts must be taken to 
tackle lifestyle diseases. The budgetary allocation under NPCDCS must be increased 
considerably, the Department, however, must improve the absorption capacity under 
various Schemes. The Committee in its 139th Report had assessed the status of NPCDCS 
Scheme and noted that the screening of common Cancer under NPCDCS program must 
be done nationwide as seriously as it should be to prevent the killer disease and unstable 
the fiscal foundation of Household management. The Committee had recommended that 
cancer must be dealt separately, with specific programmes and intended budgetary 
allocation and enhanced and forensic inputs of early detection and prevention of cancer. 
and must not be grouped under other lifestyle diseases. The Committee reiterates its 
recommendation that the Department must devise a targeted plan for cancer control.

4.6.5  The Committee has been apprised of the following achievements under this 
scheme as under :- 
(i) Under the Centrally Sponsored Scheme for establishment of new medical 
colleges attached with district/referral 157 medical colleges have been approved in 
the country. Out of these, 131 medical colleges hare functional as on date, including 
15 medical colleges in 2023-24, 23 medical colleges which have become functional 
in the year 2024-25. This has added about 1500 MBBS seats in FY 2023-24 and 
about 2300 MBBS seats in FY 2024-25.  
 
(ii) Under CSS for increase of MBBS seats in existing Government medical 
college, a total of 4977 MBBS seats have been approved till date, out of which 300 
seats were approved in FY 2023-24. 
 
(iii) Under Strengthening and up-gradation of State government Medical colleges 
for increasing PG seats, the overall target of 8058 PG seats in two phases has 
already been achieved. Out of these, 142 PG seats were approved in FY 2023-24. 
 
4.6.6  The Committee recommends that the Department of Health and Family 
Welfare implement a proactive, multi-pronged strategy to ensure consistent and 
efficient utilization of allocated funds for the Human Resource for Health & Medical 
Education schemes by enhancing the absorption capacity. Specifically, given the 
recurring issues of underutilization due to lack of state demand, delayed state share 
release, and Real-time System of Integrated Quick Transfers (SNA SPARSH) 
compliance delays, the Department should establish a dedicated task force to provide 
intensive, institutionalised support to each state and union territory. This task force will 
conduct pre-emptive assessments of state needs, offer hands-on assistance with SNA 
SPARSH compliance, and facilitate timely release of state matching funds through 
regular communication and problem-solving workshops. Furthermore, the Department 
should implement a quarterly review system that incorporates direct feedback from 
states, allowing for real-time adjustments to funding allocations and program 
implementation. This approach will not only address the immediate issue of 
underutilization but also build stronger collaborative relationships with states, ensuring 
the long-term success of expanding medical education infrastructure and increasing the 
availability of medical professionals. 
 
 
4.7 PRADHAN MANTRI AYUSHMAN BHARAT HEALTH INFRASTRUCTURE 
MISSION (PM-ABHIM) 
 
4.7.1  Pradhan Mantri Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM-
ABHIM) is a Centrally Sponsored Scheme with some Central Sector component which has an 
outlay of Rs. 64,180 crores spread over 5 years from 2021-22 to 2025-26 for implementation 
of the Atmanirbhar Bharat Package for Health Sector announced by the Prime Minister as part 

of the series of Government measures for providing stimulus to the economy. of the scheme is to fill critical gaps in health infrastructure, surveillance and healtspanning both the urban and rural areas so that the communities are Atmanirbhar in managing such pandemic/ health crises. The measures under the scheme focus on developing capacities of health systems and institutions across the continuum of csecondary and tertiary and on preparing health systems in responding effectively to the current and future pandemics/ disasters. It is envisaged in the scheme to build an IT enabled disease surveillance system by developing district, regional and national levels, in Metropolitan areas & strengthening health units at the Points of Entry, for effectively detecting, investigating, preventing, and combating Public Health Emergencies and Disease Outbreaks. 
 4.7.2  The Committee, on examination of the Demand for Grants, observes that the BE allocation for the FY 2023-24 amounting to Rs. 4200 crore was reduced to Rs. 2100 crore at RE stage, against the actual expenditure of Rs. 1805.78 crore. 137 
 
infrastructure initiative, has capex projects like setting up of Critical Care Blocks, BPHUs, 
IPHLs involving lead time/gestation period, leading to slow pace of expenditure during the 
initial period of the scheme. Accordingly, the pace of expenditure is expected to expedite with 
the regular monitoring of the scheme through virtual meeting by the senior officials and 
visiting States/UTs for physical progress on the ground along with visit during the Common 
Review Mission (CRM). 
 
4.7.4  The Committee expresses concern over the declining trend of fund 
utilisation under PM-ABHIM during the last two financial years 2023-24 and 2024-25 
i.e. 42.99% and 62.74% respectively, of the BE allocation that casts aspersion on 
absorption capacity of Scheme and the professional proficiency of implementing agency 
of the Scheme. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends a more streamlined and 
front-loaded disbursement process for the PM-ABHIM scheme as the scheme contain 
visionary aim and objective of Government to fill critical gaps in health infrastructure, 
surveillance and health research – spanning both the urban and rural areas so that the 
communities are Atmanirbhar in managing such pandemic/ health crises. The data 
clearly shows consistent underutilization of allocated funds, particularly in the initial 
phases of each fiscal year, despite the capital intensive infrastructure project under the 
scheme. While the Department attributes this to the lead time of capital expenditure 
projects, the significant reductions from Budget Estimates to Revised Estimates indicate 
a predictable pattern of slow pace of expenditure. To address this, the Committee 
suggests that the Department implement a system where a larger portion of the annual 
budget is released during the first quarter, contingent on states submitting detailed 
project implementation plans and timelines. This would allow for early initiation of 
infrastructure projects, reducing the lag time and ensuring more consistent expenditure 
throughout the fiscal year. Moreover, the Department should focus on providing 
technical assistance and capacity building to states to expedite project approvals and 
implementation, thereby mitigating delays and ensuring the timely achievement of the 
scheme's objectives. 
 
4.7.5  On being asked about the steps taken/that can be further taken to ensure the 
optimal utilization of funds allocated to the scheme, the Department submitted that during the 
last 6 months (July 2024 – January 2025), PM-ABHIM scheme has made significant progress 
in achieving the physical deliverables. Work initiation for Building-less SHC-AAM in rural 
areas has increased by 22%, while work completion has risen by 47%. Additionally, 30% 
more Urban-AAMs have been operationalized during the same period. BPHUs have seen a 
28% rise in the initiation of construction work and a remarkable 116% increase in civil work 
completion. Furthermore, there has been a 23% increase in work started for IPHL and a 30% 
increase for CCBs. Similarly, IPHL work completion has grown by 42%, and 10 CCB units 
have completed civil work in the last two quarters. 
 
4.7.6  With a view to enable the States/UTs to initiate the capital infrastructure works 
up to FY 2025-26 for optimal utilization of funds allocated to the scheme. All the States/UTs 
were advised to pre-pone the activities up to FY 2025-26 under the scheme. The pre-
ponement of activities enables the achievement of the envisaged deliverables by the State/UT 

138 
 
within scheme period and will assist the States in effective implementation of the PM-
ABHIM scheme. Further, funds released to the States/UTs have to be utilized holistically for 
the total approved physical units / deliverable (scheme period) without linking/ earmarking 
funds released against year wise approvals. Regular review meetings for expenditure status 
and regional conferences were held to review the progress under the scheme. CRM review 
and visits are organized for regularly to monitor the progress of the scheme.  
 
4.7.7  The Department apprised the Committee that under the CSS components of the 
scheme, following are the five activities where support is provisioned to the State/UTs during 
the scheme period (FYs 2021-26): 
 
i) Construction of 17,788 Building less Sub-Centres as Ayushman Bharat- Health & 
Wellness centres, now known as Ayushman Arogya Mandir (AAM); 
ii) Establishment of 11,024 Health & wellness Centres, now AAM in Urban areas with a 
focus on slum and slum like areas are envisioned; 
iii) Establishment of 3382 Block Public Health Units (BPHUs) at the block level; 
iv) Establishment of 730 District Integrated Public Health Labs in the country, wherein 
each district will have one such lab; and  
v) Establishing 602 Critical Care Hospital Blocks in all districts with population more 
than 5 lakhs.

4.7.8  On being asked whether PM-ABHIM would be completed in 2025-26 
implementing the Atma Nirbhar Bharat Package for health sector as announced by the Prime 
Minister as part of the series of Government measures for providing stimulus to the economy, 
the Department submitted that PM-ABHIM is set to be completed by 2025-26, implementing 
the Atma Nirbhar Bharat Package for the health sector, as announced by the Prime Minister, 
as part of the series of government measures for providing stimulus to the economy. The 
scheme’s focus on strengthening health infrastructure, surveillance, and local health systems 
contributes significantly to the government’s efforts to revitalize the economy, while ensuring 
that communities are self-reliant in managing health crises. By enhancing critical healthcare 
facilities, disease surveillance, and emergency response capabilities, PM-ABHIM lays a 
strong foundation for a healthier and more resilient India, supporting the vision of "Viksit 
Bharat by 2047. CCB component of the scheme are 50 bedded and 100 bedded infrastructure 
projects with lead time of 2 years. The identification of the land and site for these bigger 
infrastructure projects are a big limiting factor. 
 
4.7.9  On a query enquiring about to what extent the focus on developing capacities 
of health systems and institutions across the continuum of care at all levels, primary, 
secondary and tertiary has been achieved under the programme, the Department submitted 
that the PM-ABHIM scheme aims to fill critical gaps in health infrastructure, surveillance and 
health research, spanning both the urban and rural areas. The measures under the scheme 
focus on developing capacities of health systems and institutions across the continuum of care 
at all levels viz. primary, secondary and tertiary and on preparing health systems in 
responding effectively to the current and future pandemics/ disasters. These institutions will 
also be beneficial for Public health functions of surveillance of the infectious diseases and 
robust monitoring of such infectious diseases with upward and downward linkages. The 
Department also submitted the following achievements under the scheme:  

140 
 
 
i) 3019 building-less SHC-AAM constructed and 2085 U-AAM operationalized to 
deliver comprehensive primary health care, including preventive, promotive, curative, 
palliative, and rehabilitative services. 
ii) Civil work for 405 BPHUs completed to achieve integration between clinical services 
and public health functions & Improved disease surveillance. 
iii) 163 District IPHL work completed to link the labs with block, state and regional 
public health and veterinary labs to support multi-sectoral collaboration for clinical 
management and public health surveillance. 
iv) 10 CCBs civil work completed to augment the capacity of the district for assured 
treatment and management of patients with infectious diseases or critical illnesses as 
well as Supportive and ancillary services. 
 
 
4.7.10  The Committee notes the progress made under the PM-ABHIM scheme. 
Although there is advancement in work initiation and completion across various 
components [Secondary Health Care-Ayushman Arogya Mandir (SHC-AAM), U- 
Ayushman Arogya Mandir, Block Public Health Unit (BPHU), Integrated Public Health 
Laboratories (IPHL)], however, the Critical Care Blocks (CCBs) are significantly 
lagging the pace of execution, with only 3% work completion. The Department has 
taken steps to accelerate progress, including pre-poning activities and conducting 
regular reviews. However, land acquisition for CCBs remains a major bottleneck. The 
scheme's focus on strengthening health infrastructure across all levels is well taken, but 
the timelines for completion of project need closer monitoring. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department prioritize and implement a focused strategy 
to address the land acquisition and construction challenges facing the Critical Care 
Blocks (CCBs). This strategy should include (a) establishing a dedicated task force at the 
state and district levels to expedite land identification and allocation for CCBs; (b) 
providing technical assistance and streamlined approval processes to States/UTs to 
overcome bureaucratic hurdles in construction; (c) exploring alternative construction 
models, such as modular construction, to reduce lead times; and (d) implementing a 
strict monitoring system with milestone based review of each CCB project to ensure 
timely completion of the project. Given the critical role of CCBs in pandemic 
preparedness and critical care, immediate and decisive action is crucial to ensure the 
timely completion of the project. 
 
4.7.11  The Committee recommends the establishment of a robust, real-time 
monitoring system to track not only the physical construction progress but also the 
timely operationalization of completed health facilities, particularly U-AAMs. This 
system should include regular audits to verify the functionality and service delivery of 
operationalized units, mechanisms to ensure adequate staffing and resource allocation 
for newly completed facilities, review of impact on community health outcomes, and 
creation of a public dashboard that displays the current status of each project, to 
increase transparency and accountability. The department should create a system to 
track the time taken between civil work completion and full operational status to ensure 

141 
 
that completed projects are made operational with delay. By focusing on operational 
effectiveness, the Department can ensure that the investments made in infrastructure 
translate into tangible and timely improvements in healthcare access and quality. 
 
4.8 AYUSHMAN BHARAT-PRADHAN MANTRI JAN AROGYA YOJANA (AB-
PMJAY) 
 
4.8.1  Ayushman Bharat- Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB-PMJAY) is a 
flagship scheme of the Government which provides health cover of Rs. 5 lakhs per family per 
year for secondary and tertiary care hospitalization to approximately 55 Cr beneficiaries 
corresponding to 12.37 Crore families constituting economically vulnerable bottom 40% of 
India’s population. AB-PMJAY aims to expand service availability across the country by 
empaneling not only public but also private healthcare facilities. AB-PMJAY provides 
significant flexibility by allowing states to choose the mode of implementation, packages 
offered, package pricing and reservation according to their level of development and stage of 
epidemiological transition. The scheme provides cashless and paperless inpatient Secondary 
and Tertiary medical care in public as well as private empanelled hospitals. The scheme 
covers 1961 procedures across 27 medical specialties through a network of 30,201 
empanelled Public and Private Hospitals.  
 
4.8.2  The key objectives of the scheme have been informed as follows:  
 
i) Address unmet health needs: Actively identifying and taking steps to provide 
necessary healthcare services to beneficiaries who currently lack access to 
adequate treatment due to factors like availability, affordability or awareness 
of required care, etc.  
ii) Comprehensive coverage: Ensure coverage for a wide range of illnesses 
iii) Financial protection: Reduce catastrophic health expenditures 
iv) Healthcare accessibility: Enhance access to healthcare facilities across the 
country 
v) Ensure adaptability: Empowers states to adapt implementation based on local 
needs, including various health insurance/assurance schemes in the states/UTs. 
 
 
4.8.3  With respect to the scheme uptake the Department submitted that AB-PMJAY 
has been implemented since September, 2018. Over the last 5 years of scheme 
implementation, 34 States/UTs have joined the scheme. Initially, the scheme uptake was low 
in comparison to what was anticipated. However, it has gradually increased over the years. 
Recently, the scheme has been expanded to cover 6 crore senior citizens of age 70 years and 
above belonging to 4.5 crore families under AB-PMJAY Vay Vandana scheme, irrespective 
of their socio economic status. As on date, except for West Bengal, all the States across the 
nation have been on-boarded under AB-PMJAY. The Department submitted the details on 
scheme uptake as follows: -

4.8.6  On a specific query, the Department submitted information on its projection to 
fully utilise funds to the tune of Rs. 9406.00 crores in 2025-26 under the Scheme that the total 
projected expenditure across all regions is approximately ₹8,918 crores. Allocations vary by 
mode—insurance, trust, or hybrid—ranging from ₹1.15 crores for Lakshadweep to ₹867.74 
crores for Uttar Pradesh, with significant expenditures for states like Bihar, Maharashtra, and 
Tamil Nadu. On 13th January 2025, an MoU has been signed with the Govt of Odisha for 
implementation of AB PM-JAY. It is anticipated that an estimated expenditure of ₹448 crore 
will be incurred during the financial year 2025-26.  This also includes an allocation of 
₹155.00 crores for ASHA/Anganwadi Workers/Anganwadi Helpers and ₹1,443.00 crores for 
treatment of Sr. Citizens of the age 70 years and above holding Ayushman Vay Vandana 
Cards. In addition to miscellaneous and contingency expenses, ₹150 Cr. have been earmarked 
for card creation activities across all state and UTs and ₹100 Cr have been set aside for 
undertaking several IEC activities to create beneficiary awareness under the scheme. 
 
4.8.7  The Committee is of the opinion that the Ayushman Bharat-Pradhan 
Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB-PMJAY) is a pivotal and visionary health initiative 
aiming to provide financial protection and healthcare access to a large number of 
India's vulnerable population. While the scheme has seen substantial growth in terms of 
state participation, card creation, and hospital admissions, several key areas require 
attention like Budget Utilization and State Participation. The Committee observes a 

144 
 
persistent trend of underutilization of allocated funds, particularly in the previous year 
as well as in the current financial year. The Committee observes that, in the FY 2023-24, 
an amount of Rs. 7200.00 crore was allocated as BE 2023-24 which was reduced to Rs. 
6800.00 crore at RE stage, however, the actual expenditure was Rs. 6670.47 crore.  In 
the FY 2024-25, Rs. 7300.00 crores was made in BE 2024-25 which was revised to Rs. 
7605.54 crores at RE 2024-25, however, the actual expenditure was to the tune of Rs. 
5034.03 Crores till 09.01.2025. In FY 2025-26, the enhanced amount of Rs. 9406 crore 
has been allocated as BE 2025-26.  This indicates potential inefficiencies in fund 
disbursement or implementation bottlenecks. The Committee, therefore, recommends a 
thorough review of the fund release mechanisms to states/UTs. This review should focus 
on identifying and eliminating bottlenecks that contribute to underutilization. 
Specifically, the Department should implement a system of proactive monitoring of fund 
utilization by states, coupled with targeted support to address any implementation 
challenges. This support could include technical assistance, capacity building, and 
simplified reporting procedures. Furthermore, the committee suggests that the release of 
funds be linked to the performance of states in terms of card creation, hospital 
admissions, and beneficiary feedback, ensuring that funds are directed to areas of 
greatest need and impact. The Department should also create and publish a clear and 
concise guideline for states, explaining the process of fund requests, and the expected 
timelines. To ensure that the allocation of fund to the tune of Rs. 9406.00 crore in BE 
2025-26 is utilized, a quarterly review system may be set up to avoid any delays in 
release of fund to States/UTs. 
 
4.8.8  The Committee is of the view that besides the large number of empanelled 
hospitals and issuance of cards, the crucial factor for successful implementation of the 
Yojana in public awareness about the Yojana is public awareness about the Yojana. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends a focused campaign, high-level engagement with the 
respective state governments, addressing their specific concerns and highlighting the 
benefits of the scheme for their populations. The Department should intensify its efforts 
to enhance beneficiary awareness, particularly in rural and underserved areas. This can 
be achieved through targeted Information, Education, and Communication (IEC) 
campaigns, utilizing local languages and community-based approaches. Leveraging 
ASHA and Anganwadi workers, as well as digital platforms, to disseminate information 
and facilitate card creation is crucial. The Committee also recommends that the 
Department conduct regular beneficiary feedback surveys to assess the effectiveness of 
awareness campaigns and identify areas for improvement. A dedicated helpline and 
easily accessible grievance redressal mechanism should also be established to address 
beneficiary concerns promptly. Further, public displays of information regarding 
hospital empanelment and available procedures should be made mandatory at all public 
health centers. 
 
4.8.9  The Committee appreciates that the Government has recently expanded 
the AB-PMJAY to cover 6 crore senior citizens of age 70 years and above belonging to 
4.5 crore families under AB-PMJAY Vay Vandana scheme, irrespective of their socio 
economic status. The Committee observes that an amount to the tune of Rs. 1443.00 

145 
 
crore has been earmarked for treatment of senior citizens of the age of 70 years and 
above holding Ayushman Vay Vandna Cards.  The Committee is of the view that age 
criteria of 70 years and above for Ayushman Vay Vandna Cards should be rationalized 
to 60 years and above irrespective of their socio-economic status for widening the 
coverage of the scheme in the better interest of common masses. 
 
4.8.10  The Committee also recommends the Department to launch public 
awareness campaign about AB-PMJAY so that intended beneficiaries of the Yojana are 
not left out from coverage. 
 
4.8.11  The Committee, keeping in view huge expenditure on the critical 
healthcare, should revise healthcare coverage from existing Rs. 5 lakh per family per 
year to Rs. 10 lakh per family per year.  
 
4.8.12   The Committee observes that several high-end interventions/procedures, 
and even high-end diagnostics are not included in AB-PMJAY. The Committee 
recommends that the number of packages/procedures covered under the Yojana should 
be reviewed and the fresh packages/procedures related to treatment of critical illness 
with high cost of treatment and expensive high-end investigations/diagnostics like 
radiological diagnostics (CT, MRI, imaging including Nuclear Imaging) be incorporated 
in AB-PMJAY instead of leaving the same to be booked as Add-on packages.  
 
4.8.13   The Committee has come across the instances of delay in settlement of 
claims of empanelled hospitals under AB-PMJAY and that the package rates have not 
been reasonably revised in many States resulting into inconvenience caused to the 
patients due to denial of treatment by many empanelled hospitals. The Committee, 
therefore, considers that the States/UTs which have not revised the package rates may 
be persuaded by the Department to revise the package rates on the similar lines of rates 
revised by some of the States in 2022. In addition to the above, the Committee 
recommends that the Department review and streamline the process related to 
settlement of claims submitted by the empanelled hospitals within the specified time.  
 
4.8.14  The Committee has been given to understand that the operational period 
of National Health Mission (NHM) is from FY 2021 to FY 2026, therefore, coming to an 
end on 31.03.2026. The Committee, considering the pivotal significance of the Mission, is 
of the considered view that the operational period of NHM must be extended beyond 31st 
March 2026 and cover at least the duration of 16th Finance Commission to enable the 
Department to achieve the remaining goals and objectives of health indicators under the 
Mission.  
 
 
 
 
 

146 
 
CHAPTER V 
 
ISSUES RAISED 
 
5.1 SUBMISSION BY THE DEPARTMENT TO THE 16TH FINANCE COMMISSION 
 
5.1.1  In respect of the demand pertaining to the health sector projected by the 
Government to the 16th Finance Commission, the Department submitted that a presentation 
was made before the Commission highlighting the progress made in the health sector under 
Central Sector and Centrally Sponsored Schemes. The issues discussed with 16th Finance 
Commission include- 
 
i. Enhancement of diagnostic infrastructure in States and UTs, focusing on improving 
healthcare services at the grassroots level.  
ii. Support provided for infrastructure upgrades, enabling them to meet the Indian 
Public Health Standards (IPHS) 2022.  
iii. Addressing key infrastructure gaps in healthcare facilities to ensure better health 
outcomes by aligning with National Quality Assurance Standards (NQAS).  
 
 
5.1.2  The Committee has been apprised that the Department has presented 
before the 16th Finance Commission the status/progress made in the health sector, 
focusing on infrastructure improvements. In the discussion with the Commission, the 
focus was on enhancement of diagnostic capabilities, up-gradation of infrastructure to 
meet Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS) 2022, and addressing the key 
infrastructure gaps in healthcare facilities to align with National Quality Assurance 
Standards (NQAS). The Committee recommends the Department to conduct a 
comprehensive, year-wise projection of health sector demand for the 16th Finance 
Commission. This projection should especially quantify the financial resources required 
to achieve the goals of enhancing diagnostic infrastructure, meeting IPHS 2022, and 
aligning with NQAS which would facilitate more effective resource allocation and 
ensure sustained progress in the health sector. 
 
5.2 ELDERLY CARE 
 
5.2.1  On a query on Elderly Care, the Department submitted that for BE 2025-26 Rs 
95.38 was sought, however, Rs 50 Cr has been allocated. The demand was raised as the 
project for National Centre for Ageing (NCA) is under construction in Banaras Hindu 
University (BHU) at cost of Rs 150 Cr. Rs 47.20 Cr has already been sanctioned. Next year, 
Rs 35 Cr will be released initially and if demand for additional funds arises, the amount will 
be sought at RE Stage 2025-26. 
 
5.2.2  The Committee observes that the primary issue is the significant shortfall 
in funding for the National Centre for Ageing (NCA) at Banaras Hindu University 

147 
 
(BHU). While a demand of Rs 95.38 crore was made for the 2025-26, only Rs 50 crore 
has been allocated. Given that the NCA is under construction with a total cost of Rs 150 
crore, and Rs 47.20 crore has already been sanctioned, the remaining funds are crucial 
for timely completion. To ensure continuity and prevent project delays, the Committee 
recommends that the Ministry prioritize the release of the remaining Rs 35 crore as 
initially planned. Furthermore, the Committee explicitly advocates for a proactive 
monitoring mechanism to assess the project's progress during the first half of 2025-26. 
This will allow for the timely identification of any additional funding needs. This 
approach ensures the NCA's timely completion, benefiting elderly citizens. 
 
5.3 EMISSION GAP REPORT 2020 UN ENVIRONMENT PROGRAMME 
 
5.3.1  On a query related to observation of the Emission Gap Report 2020 UN 
Environment Programme that India has seen over 6% more Green House Gas Emission than 
previous year, the Committee is apprised that the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare, 
Government of India in year 2019 launched the National Programme on Climate Change and 
Human Health (NPCCHH) under the National Health Mission. This program aims to address 
health challenges arising from climate change by developing State Action Plans and 
promoting inter sectoral coordination. Additionally, MoHFW has been working closely with 
other ministries, such as the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC) 
and NITI Aayog, to develop multi-sectoral strategies for mitigating environmental health 
risks. NPCCHH has developed an organizational structure at all levels-National, State and 
District level.  
 
i. National Level- Prime Minister’s Council on Climate Change; National 
Steering Group (NSG) under the chairmanship of Secretary (HFW); and 
Health Sectoral Working Group under the co-chairmanship of  AS, PHP & 
DGHS. 
ii. States level-Governing Body on Climate Change and Human Health  under 
Hon’ble Health Minister (State);  Multi-Sectoral Task Force on Climate 
Change and Human Health under Secretary (Health); State Nodal Officers 
(NPCCHH);  and State Level Environment Health Cell. 
iii. District Level- District level Multi-Sectoral Task Force 
5.3.2  All 36 states have developed State Action Plan on Climate Change and Human 
Health and were launched by Hon’ble PM on 29th Oct 2024.District Nodal Officers-Climate 
Change under NPCCHH identified for all 36 States/UTs. 
5.3.3  Keeping in view of the observation of Emission Gap Report 2020 UN 
Environment Programme that India has seen over 6% more Green House Gas Emission 
than previous year, the Committee feels that it is high time for India to establish an 
Environmental Health Regulatory Agency for Comprehensive and Cohesive 
Environmental Governance to control detrimental health effects of exposure to air, 
water and soil pollutants to prevent wide range of non-communicable diseases. The 
Committee appreciates that the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare (MoHFW) has 

148 
 
been working closely with other ministries, such as the Ministry of Environment, Forest 
and Climate Change (MoEFCC) and NITI Aayog, to develop multi-sectoral strategies 
for mitigating environmental health risks and National Programme on Climate Change 
and Human Health (NPCCHH) has developed an organizational structure at all levels-
National, State and District level. The Committee recommends that the Department 
significantly enhance the regulatory authority and scope of the State Level Environment 
Health Cells within the NPCCHH framework, especially, these cells should be 
empowered with clear mandates for enforcement and monitoring of environmental 
health standards, including air, water, and soil pollution, and be given the resources to 
conduct independent assessments and issue binding directives. This expansion would 
leverage the established structure of the NPCCHH, particularly the state-level Multi-
Sectoral Task Forces, to ensure a more robust and proactive approach to mitigating the 
detrimental health effects of environmental pollution, thereby strengthening the existing 
framework ensuring continuity and efficient utilization of existing resources while 
addressing the urgent need for stronger environmental health governance. 
 
5.4 REGULAR NURSING CADRE 
5.4.1  On a query asking for creation of regular nursing cadre, the Department 
submitted that (i) Under the component ‘Training of Nurses’ of the Central Sector Scheme 
“Development of Nursing Services”, Central Government provides financial assistance of Rs. 
2,28,500/- per course for training of nurses in a batch of 30 nurses per course of 7 days to 
State Government/State Nursing Council/Central Government Institutions/TNAI to update 
their knowledge and skills in the different nursing specialty. Further, it is stated that Rs. 1.74 
crore is earmarked in the Revised Estimates for the year 2024-25. 
(ii) As per the directions of Hon’ble Supreme Court a Committee of Experts was constituted 
under the Chairpersonship of Director General of Health Services, Ministry of Health and 
Family Welfare to look into the working conditions and pay of nurses working in private 
hospitals and nursing homes. The following recommendations of the Committee have been 
forwarded by this Ministry to all the States and Union Territories. 
i. In case of >200 bedded hospitals, salary given to private nurses should be at par 
with the salary of State Govt. nurses given in the concerned State/ UT for the 
similar corresponding grade. 
ii. In case of >100 bedded hospitals, salary given to private nurses should not be 
more than 10% less in comparison of the salary of State Govt. nurses given in the 
concerned State/ UTs for the similar corresponding grade. 
iii. ln case of 50-100 bedded hospitals, salary given to private nurses should not be 
more than 25% less in comparison of the salary of State Govt. nurses given in the 
concerned State/UTs for the similar corresponding grade. 
iv. Salary given to private nurses should not be less than Rs. 20000/- pm in any case 
even for <50 bedded hospitals. 

149 
 
v. Working conditions viz. leaves, working hours, medical facilities, transportation, 
accommodation etc. given to nurses should be at par with the benefits granted to 
State Govt. nurses working in the concerned State/UTs. 
 
5.4.2  The Committee recommends a significant increase in the earmarked 
funds for nursing training programs, coupled with the establishment of a dedicated, 
centrally managed nursing cadre. There is need for advanced medical technology and 
emergency care training to the nurses. Therefore, the Committee advocates for a 
substantial rise in funding for expansion of training programs to include specialized 
modules on cutting-edge medical technologies and emergency response protocols. 
Furthermore, to ensure continuity and attract and retain skilled nurses, a regular 
nursing cadre should be created, offering standardized pay scales, benefits, and career 
progression opportunities across both public and private healthcare sectors, building on 
the existing Supreme Court expert committee recommendations. This would not only 
improve working conditions and incentives but also establish a stable and skilled 
nursing workforce capable of handling complex medical challenges. 
 
5.5 OPPORTUNITY FOR GLOBAL HEALTH LEADERSHIP 
5.5.1  On being asked about how the Government perceive the opportunity for global 
health leadership by comprehensive engagement with WHO in the light of the US 
withdrawing from the global body, and about the strategic move for India in store to grab the 
opportunity for a more robust biomedical and public health ecosystem within the country, the 
Department made a submission that globally, India is the sixth largest voluntary contributor to 
WHO after the US, UK, Germany, Norway and France. India has been recognized as a 
lighthouse country at various multilateral forums, for its digital health initiatives such as 
Ayushman Bharat Digital Health Mission, e-Sanjeevani, Integrated Health Information 
Platform (IHIP), Co-Win, U-Win etc. The Global Initiative on Digital Health (GIDH)was 
launched during India’s G20 Presidency. India is ready to offer its Digital Public 
Infrastructure (DPI) with countries in need through GIDH, intending to strengthen their 
digital healthcare ecosystems and enhance health outcomes. The Government of India over 
the last few years has taken several landmark initiatives for reforming and re-hauling the 
medical education sector in the country. India looks forward to organising the exposure visits 
and capacity-building programmes as a unique and exclusive opportunity for healthcare 
professional development in other countries through access to India's educational and training 
institutions. India has signed the Memorandum of Understandings and organised Joint 
Working Group Meetings on cooperation in the field of health and medicine with many 
countries to promote health equity, strengthen the health infrastructure and improve health 
outcomes.    
5.5.2  Given the fact of US withdrawal from the WHO and India's existing 
strong engagement and digital health leadership, the Committee recommends a focused 
strategy to solidify India's global health leadership by leveraging its digital health 
expertise and educational capacity. The Committee recommends that India should 
proactively establish a dedicated "Global Digital Health Partnership Initiative" under 

150 
 
the WHO framework that would certainly streamline the offering of India's Digital 
Public Infrastructure (DPI), like Ayushman Bharat Digital Health Mission and Co-Win, 
to developing nations through Global Initiative on Digital Health (GIDH), coupled with 
robust capacity-building programs and educational exchanges. By actively promoting its 
successful digital health models and expanding its medical education offerings, India can 
not only fill the leadership vacuum but also foster a more robust domestic biomedical 
ecosystem and become a premier healthcare training hub for Asian-African countries, 
thus maintaining continuity with its existing contributions and maximizing the 
opportunity presented by the changing global health landscape. 
 
5.6 REVIEW OF NATIONAL HEALTH POLICY 2017 
5.6.1  On being asked about the process followed in formulation of the National 
Health Policy 2017 and its review, the Department submitted that the National Health Policy 
2017 was formulated through extensive stakeholder consultations, with the draft placed in the 
public domain in December 2014 and endorsement from the Central Council for Health & 
Family Welfare in February 2016. 
5.6.2  The Committee notes that almost 8 years have been passed since the 
formulation of the National Health Policy 2017. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
establishing a formal, periodic review mechanism for the policy's implementation and 
impact assessment. This mechanism should mandate consistent, transparent engagement 
and feedback collection from various stakeholders and the public. The review of the 
policy and publishing the findings can ensure that the policy remains responsive to 
evolving healthcare needs and maintains the initial broad consensus achieved through 
its formulation. This will guarantee continuity between the policy's foundational 
principles and its ongoing adaptation of global practices in the field of healthcare. 
 
5.7 HEALTH AND EDUCATION CESS  
5.7.1  On asking the details of utilisation of the Health and Education Cess, the 
Department apprised of the Committee that Union Government has levied Health & 
Education Cess at 4% payable on the amount of Direct Tax payable plus surcharge. Pradhan 
Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Nidhi (PMSSN) was introduced in the Union Budget 2020-21. 
PMSSN is 25% of total Health Cess proceeds (i.e. 1% out of 4% of Health and Education 
Cess).  PMSSN has been approved to fulfil the commitment of the Government to provide 
and finance the Quality Health Services under Section 2(13) of the Finance Act, 2011. The 
accruals into the PMSSN is to be utilized for the following Centrally Sponsored schemes of 
the Department namely, 
a) Flexible Pool for RCH & Health System Strengthening, National Health Programme 
and National Urban Health Mission (component of NHM) 
b) Infrastructure Maintenance (Component of NHM) 
c) Human Resource for Health, and 
d) Medical Education (HRH&ME). 

151 
 
5.7.2  The Committee notes that 25% part of the Health and Education Cess 
collected is allocated to the Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Nidhi (PMSSN). This 
PMSSN funding is then designated for utilisation in specific centrally sponsored health 
schemes viz. a) Flexible Pool for RCH & Health System Strengthening, National Health 
Programme and National Urban Health Mission (component of NHM) b) Infrastructure 
Maintenance (Component of NHM) c) Human Resource for Health and Medical 
Education (HRH&ME). The Committee urges upon the Government to increase the 
share of PMSSN in the corpus accumulated through collection of Health and Education 
Cess.  
 
5.8 ISSUANCE OF ABHA CARDS 
5.8.1  On discussion related to issuance of ABHA Cards on the basis of 2011 census, 
the Department informed the Committee that ABHA (Ayushman Bharat Health Account) is a 
unique health ID under the Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM), which focuses on 
creating a digital health ecosystem. ABDM was launched on 27 Sep 2021. It enables 
individuals to access and share their health records securely with their consent. However, 
ABDM does not provide any financial health coverage or insurance. Ayushman Bharat 
Pradhan Mantri - Jan Arogya Yojana (AB PM-JAY) is a scheme which provides health cover 
of Rs. 5 lakhs per eligible beneficiary family per year for secondary and tertiary care 
hospitalization. Under AB PM-JAY initially, 10.74 crore eligible households were identified 
under the scheme from SECC 2011 based on certain deprivation and occupational criteria in 
rural and urban areas respectively. Further, in January 2022, the Government of India revised 
the beneficiary base to 12 crore families, considering the decadal population growth rate of 
11.7% and gave the flexibility to States/UTs to use other databases of schemes meant for 
similar socio-economic profile which are Aadhaar verified. In March 2024, 37 lakh families 
of Accredited Social Health Activists (ASHAs), Anganwadi Workers (AWWs) and 
Anganwadi Helpers (AWHs) were also included in the scheme. Further, on 29th October 
2024, the Government has expanded AB PM-JAY to provide free treatment benefits of up to 
₹5 lakh per year to all senior citizens aged 70 years and above along with their families, 
irrespective of their socio-economic status. 
 
5.8.2  The Committee notes the expansion of coverage from different segments 
of population under AB-PMJAY scheme from time to time, however, the Committee 
recommends the Department for  the immediate and consistent integration of updated 
demographic data into the ABHA card issuance and PMJAY beneficiary identification 
processes. Specifically, while the PMJAY scheme has made efforts to expand its 
coverage beyond the 2011 census, the fundamental reliance on outdated data for initial 
identification and ABHA card linkage creates potential exclusion and inaccuracy. This 
continuous updating will ensure that the schemes remain relevant and equitable, 
reflecting the true health needs of the population. 
 
 
 

152 
 
5.9 AYUSHMAN BHARAT- TRIBAL AREAS AND MIGRANT POPULATION 
 
5.9.1  On the issues of Ayushman Bharat in tribal areas not effective because of 
migrating population and lack of blood storage facility in PHCs and gynaecologists in rural 
and tribal areas, the Department apprised of the Committee that AB-PMJAY has a unique 
feature called ‘portability’ and under this feature, eligible beneficiaries can access healthcare 
services at any empanelled hospital across the country irrespective of their place of residence. 
This feature is especially beneficial for migrant workers. Further, NHA is actively working on 
reaching out to eligible beneficiaries through a massive IEC campaign and Ayushman Card 
creation drive. In this direction, initiatives like Aapke Dwar Ayushman campaign have been 
undertaken by NHA since January 2021 which enlisted the services of grassroots 
functionaries for beneficiary mobilization and card creation. Aapke Dwar Ayushman 2.0 was 
launched in 2022 which resulted in approximately 10 crore Ayushman cards created during 
2022-23 and 11.58 crore cards during 2023-24. Now, Aapke Dwar Ayushman 3.0 campaign 
has been relaunched with Self-registration feature enabling beneficiaries to create Ayushman 
card for himself/herself along with his/her family. Special card drives were also being 
undertaken under Pradhan Mantri Janjati Adivasi Nyaya Maha Abhiyaan (PM- JANMAN), 
Viksit Bharat Sankalp Yatra (VBSY), Pradhan Mantri Janjatiya Unnat Gram Abhiyan (PM-
JUGA) Dharti Aaba Janjatiya Gram Utkarsh Abhiyan etc. for card creation. Further field level 
workers like ASHA, Anganwadi Worker and Anganwadi Helpers are also incentivized for 
creation of Ayushman Cards. As on date, more than 36.58 Crore Ayushman Cards have been 
created under the scheme. Further, NHA has issued directions to the States/UTs to undertake 
extensive IEC activities to raise awareness about the scheme. 
5.9.2  The Committee is of the view that initiatives like "Aapke Dwar 
Ayushman" campaign should not be confined to creation of Ayushman Cards as 
highlighting vast number of creation of card is not the only objective of the scheme. 
Rather the Department should make efforts to ensure establishing robust healthcare 
infrastructure and service delivery in tribal and rural areas. While the portability 
feature and card creation drives are valuable, the core issue remains the lack of essential 
medical facilities like blood storage in PHCs, and availability of specialists like 
gynaecologists, particularly in regions with migrating populations. Therefore, the 
Committee urges upon the co-ordination between different components of the 
Ayushman Bharat scheme. The National Health Authority (NHA) should integrate these 
critical healthcare components with the "Aapke Dwar Ayushman" framework. This 
would entail deploying mobile medical units equipped with basic ICU facilities, blood 
storage capabilities, and specialist services like gynaecology to remote areas during the 
campaign. Additionally, the campaign should facilitate the establishment of sustainable 
blood collection and storage systems at PHCs and prioritize the recruitment and 
deployment of specialists like gynaecologists to these underserved regions, to bridge the 
gap between awareness and accessible, comprehensive healthcare delivery. 
 
 

153 
 
5.10 GIG WORKERS UNDER PMJAY SCHEME 
5.10.1  On being asked about the roadmap/plan to cover Gig workers under PMJAY 
Scheme, the Department informed that to successfully extend AB-PMJAY benefits to 10 
million gig workers, the initial step is to identify eligible beneficiaries and regularly update 
the dynamic database of eligible gig workers. Since AB-PMJAY relies on an Aadhaar-seeded 
database for verification and enrolment, it is essential to ensure seamless integration with the 
E-Shram portal, where gig workers will be registered under the Ministry of Labour and 
Employment (MoLE). Additionally, to enhance efficiency and minimize exclusions, NHA is 
taking up the matter with MoLE for identified following steps: - 
i. Data Integration: Establish a real-time data-sharing mechanism between the E-
Shram portal and the AB PM-JAY database to streamline verification. 
ii. Awareness and Outreach: Conduct awareness campaigns to encourage gig workers 
to register on the E-Shram portal and update their Aadhaar details. 
iii. Collaboration with Platforms: Engage with gig economy platforms (such as 
delivery and ride-hailing services) to ensure their workers are registered under E-
Shram. 
 
5.10.2  The Committee, to ensure the effective inclusion of 10 million gig workers 
under the PMJAY scheme, recommends the Department for immediate establishment of 
a dedicated, automated, and real-time data synchronization system between the E-
Shram portal and the AB-PMJAY database. This system should be designed to facilitate 
seamless, continuous, and error-free data transfer, enabling instant verification and de-
duplication exercise to identify and eliminate any overlap. This focused approach to 
data integration, alongside the planned awareness campaigns and platform 
collaborations, will minimize enrollment delays and ensure that all eligible gig workers 
receive timely access to essential healthcare benefits, while preventing double counting 
of beneficiaries. 
5.11 COVERAGE OF OPD SERVICES AND COST OF MEDICINE UNDER THE 
AYUSHMAN BHARAT 
5.11.1  On being enquired about the coverage of OPD services and cost of medicine 
under the Ayushman Bharat under PMJAY Scheme, the Department made a submission that 
AB-PMJAY provides health cover of Rs. 5 lakhs per eligible beneficiary family per year for 
secondary and tertiary care for in-patient care only i.e. hospitalization. OPD services are not 
covered under the scheme however, certain day care packages are included in the package 
master of the scheme like surgery for cataract, dialysis, animal bites etc. Further, the treatment 
packages under the scheme are comprehensive and includes pre-hospitalization expenses 
including diagnostics, pre-medication, pre-anesthetic examination, and consultation relevant 
to the present disease for up to 3 days and the package amount also covers post-
hospitalization expenses including medicine expenses for up to 15-days under this scheme.   
5.11.2  The Committee recommends the phased inclusion of essential OPD 
services and a broader range of post-hospitalization medication coverage within the 

154 
 
Ayushman Bharat PMJAY scheme. While acknowledging the scheme's focus on 
secondary and tertiary inpatient care, the current exclusion of routine OPD services and 
limited post-hospitalization medication coverage creates a significant gap in accessible 
healthcare. To ensure continuity and build upon the existing framework, the 
recommendation should propose a phased approach, starting with the inclusion of 
essential OPD services for chronic diseases and commonly reported ailments. 
Furthermore, extending post-hospitalization medication coverage beyond the current 
15-day limit, particularly for conditions requiring prolonged treatment, would 
significantly reduce the financial burden on beneficiaries while addressing a need for 
comprehensive healthcare access. 
5.12 MISUSE OF FUND UNDER AB-PMJAY SCHEME 
5.12.1  The Committee expresses concern over misuse under AB-PMJAY scheme 
through fraudulent activities by some hospitals as a business model, and requirement of 
necessary regulation to check the same. The Department replied that NHA has adopted a zero-
tolerance policy towards fraud and abuse and has been taking steps for prevention, detection, 
and deterrence of different kinds of fraud that could occur in AB-PMJAY at different stages 
of its implementation. National Anti-Fraud Unit (NAFU) has been established at NHA and 
works in close coordination with State Anti-Fraud Units (SAFU) to investigate and take joint 
action against issues related to fraud and abuse. Suitable actions including de-empanelment of 
hospitals (1113), levying penalty on errant hospitals (1504) worth Rs 122 Cr, and suspension 
of hospitals (596) have been taken against fraudulent entities as reported by the States/UTs. 
 
5.12.2  With regard to the State of Gujarat, the Department stated that suitable penal 
actions have been undertaken against hospitals and doctors involved in malpractices under the 
scheme. As on date, a total of 9 doctors and 71 hospitals have been suspended under the 
scheme. Additionally, 2 hospitals have been de-empanelled, and 1 hospital has been 
blacklisted. Further, as part of the enforcement measures, financial recoveries and penalties 
have also been imposed to deter further violations. A total recovery of ₹15.08 crore has been 
made, while penalties amounting to ₹19.90 crore have been imposed. 
 
5.12.3  Further, guidelines have been issued for medical camps conducted by 
empanelled hospitals under AB-PMJAY, mandating prior intimation to the district health 
officer, the presence of a government medical officer, and prohibiting enrolment activities. 
Additionally, new state-specific triggers have been implemented to enhance oversight. To 
further curb misuse and abuse of the scheme, additional pre-investigation documents have 
been made mandatory for pre-authorization in cardiology and pediatric procedures. 
 
5.12.4  The Committee recommends the National Health Authority (NHA) 
implement a proactive, data-driven, and transparent monitoring system focusing on 
early detection of potential misuse patterns in AB-PMJAY. This system should go 
beyond reacting to reported fraud by continuously analyzing claim data, hospital 
activity, and patient feedback to identify statistical anomalies and emerging trends 
indicating potential abuse. Specifically, the NHA should develop and publicly report on 

155 
 
key performance indicators related to fraud detection, including the frequency of pre-
authorization requests for specific procedures, the ratio of medical camp referrals to 
hospital admissions, and the rates of hospital de-empanelment and penalties by state. In 
the collection and analysis of statistical data and feedback, the extensive use of AI and 
digital tools should be considered upon by the Department. This transparent, data-
driven approach will not only allow for quicker intervention but also serve as a 
deterrent, ensuring the scheme's resources are used for their intended purpose and 
maintaining public trust.  
 
5.13 PRADHAN MANTRI NATIONAL DIALYSIS PROGRAMME (PMNDP) 
5.13.1  The Pradhan Mantri National Dialysis Programme (PMNDP) was launched in 
FY 2016-17 with the objective of having a dialysis centre in all district hospitals across India. 
The program includes provisions for scaling up of dialysis services to lower facilities up to 
the Community Health Centre level, based on the chronic kidney disease load and saturation 
of district hospitals. Under the PMNDP, State has to have One centre at DH level, however 
provision are there for expansion, if services have reached its saturation level. This expansion 
has to be decided by the State after reviewing the status. 
 
5.13.2  PMNDP is implemented in 36 States/UTs, covering 748 districts with 1,594 
HD centres and 11,011 HD machines as on 31st January, 2025. A portal launched in May 
2022 integrates all dialysis centres nationwide. In 2024, 60,489 BPL and 16,976 APL patients 
were registered for dialysis services. Under PMNDP Dialysis services are free for patients 
who are Below Poverty Line (BPL) but for other patients too services are available at the 
subsidized rates. The PMNDP portal has facility to register the Below Poverty Level (BPL) 
and Above Poverty Level (APL) financial status based on the entitlement card issued by the 
States /UTs at the time of new patient registration.  A total of 60,489 BPL patients have been 
registered in the portal. 
 
5.13.3  The Committee is of the view that the Dialysis facility under PMNDP 
should be given to the needy persons. The facility is being availed by persons who can 
afford treatment from their own pocket in private hospitals. The scheme provisions need 
to be reviewed. The Committee finds that the dialysis centres are at the District level at 
present, however, the Committee desires the Department should make strategy for 
setting up of  Dialysis facility at the Taluka level also as kidney patients are increasing. 
 
5.14 CANCER DAY CARE CENTERS 
5.14.1  Pertaining to the Committee’s query regarding infrastructure is available in 
Day Care centres and the type of critical care being given in Day Care Centres, the 
Department informed the Committee that as per the proposal, DCC will provide counselling 
services, Patient Drop back facility and follow up for Chemotherapy after the patient is 

156 
 
referred by TCCC/SCI. Training of Healthcare staff is also to be done at the Centre. A 
provisions of 4-6 beds are proposed in Day Care Centres for cancer Chemotherapy. 
 
5.14.2  Chemotherapy and medicines are proposed to be provided to the patients in 
Day Care Centres. Human Resources (including health professional and nurse) are provided 
by District Hospital for smooth functioning of the centre. Following staff is proposed in DCC 
for providing Chemotherapies: - 
 
S.No Staff Number 
1.  Oncologist/Trained MOs 01 
2.  Nurse 02 
3.  Pharmacist 01 
4.  Counsellor 01 
5.  Ward Boy/MTS 01 
 
As per IPHS 2022, 29 Medical officers are essential at the District Hospital (200 bedded). 
These medical officers are proposed to cover the functions of Cancer Day Care centres as 
well. 
 
5.14.3  The Committee has come across that a provision of 4-6 beds are proposed 
in Day Care Centres for cancer Chemotherapy and Chemotherapy and medicines are 
proposed to be provided to the patients in Day Care Centres. The Committee 
recommends that the said proposals gets actualised on the ground level, sooner the 
better. The Committee also desires that separate human Resources (including health 
professional and nurse) may be provided instead of seeking from the pool of District 
Hospital for smooth functioning of the centre since there are already scarcity of health 
professionals in the District Hospitals where such professionals must made available. 
 
5.15 HUB & SPOKE MODEL FOR CARDIAC AND STROKE  
5.15.1  The National Programme for Non-Communicable Diseases (NPNCD) focuses 
on strengthening infrastructure, human resource development, health promotion & awareness 
generation for prevention, early diagnosis, management and referral to an appropriate level of 
healthcare facility for treatment of the Non-Communicable Diseases. For effective 
management of patients with ST Segment Elevated Myocardial Infarction (STEMI) and 
Stroke, Hub and Spoke model is advised. States have to identify the Tertiary care hospitals as 
‘Hub’ based on infrastructure, experts and laboratory and intervention services and Spokes for 
early interventions. The Ministry of Health & Family Welfare provides support for stroke 
prevention and control under the National Programme for Prevention and Control of Non-
Communicable Diseases. 
 

157 
 
5.15.2  NCD screening are integral component of NPNCD programme. Currently for 
population above 30 years plus as well as opportunistic screening are being undertaken for 
NCDs like Diabetes, Hypertension, Oral cancer, Breast cancer and cervical cancers. A 
nationwide National campaign is also being launched from 20th Feb 2025. 
 
5.15.3  The Committee endorses the views of Department for strengthening 
infrastructure, human resource development, health promotion & awareness generation 
for prevention, early diagnosis, management and referral to an appropriate level of 
healthcare facility for treatment of the Non-Communicable Diseases. The Committee 
desires that for effective management of patients with ST Segment Elevated Myocardial 
Infarction (STEMI) and Stroke, Hub and Spoke model may be implemented. The 
Department may persuade the States have to identify the Tertiary care hospitals as 
‘Hub’ based on infrastructure, experts and laboratory and intervention services and 
Spokes for early interventions.  
 
5.15.4  The Committee understands that NCD screening are integral component 
of NPNCD programme, therefore, the population aged 30 years and above must be 
undertaken for screening of NCDs like Diabetes, Hypertension, Oral cancer, Breast 
cancer and cervical cancers under National campaign launched from 20th Feb 2025. 
 
5.16 TRAUMA AND EMERGENCY CARE SERVICES 
5.16.1  A programme was started on pilot mode under the 9 th & 10th Five Year Plan 
(FYP) as “Pilot Project for strengthening emergency facilities along the highways”. During 
11th Plan, the programme was named as “Assistance for capacity building for developing 
trauma care facilities in Govt. hospitals on National Highways”. 116 Trauma Care Facilities 
were created under the scheme. 
 
5.16.2  During 12th plan, the scheme was extended as “Capacity building for 
developing Trauma Care Facilities in Govt. Hospitals on National Highways”, and 80 new 
trauma care facilities were created. Total 196 Trauma Care Facilities have been sanctioned. 
Further, “Pilot Programme for Prevention of Burn Injuries” (PPPBI) was approved during 
11th FYP. 9 Burns Units were created. During 12th FYP, the scheme was continued as 
“National Programme for Prevention & Management of Burn Injuries”. Total, 47 burn 
units have been sanctioned. Beyond 12th plan, there is no provision of identification of new 
trauma care facilities and the scheme was extended till March, 14 2020 and subsequently up 
to March, 2021. The Programme “NPPMT&BI” continues beyond 12th Five Year Plan and 
has been approved under the umbrella Scheme-Tertiary Care Programme from 2021-22 to 
2025-26. The financial support is in terms of release of the balance share of Central assistance 
within the total approved cost of the approved facilities during the 11th and 12th FYP. 
  
5.16.3  The Committee understands that NPPMT&BI scheme aims at reducing 
the mortality and morbidity occurring due to injuries (including trauma and burns), by 

158 
 
developing a system of services at pre- hospital & hospital level and continuum of care 
through rehabilitation. The Committee desires that adequate funds may be allocated for 
Trauma and Emergency Care Services to achieve the significant goals of the scheme that 
includes creating center of excellence from trauma and Burn Care in existing 
Government Medical Colleges. 
5.17 HIGH MALARIA CASES IN KOKRAJHAR, ASSAM 
5.17.1  On being enquired about the action taken to contain the high Malaria cases in 
Kokrajhar, Assam (especially in Bhutan Border areas), the Department submitted that as per 
the data available with National Centre for Vector Borne Diseases Control (NCVBDC) of the 
MoHFW, there were 3,808 malaria cases and 4 cases of deaths due to malaria reported in 
Kokrajhar district in Assam till December 2024, wherein all 4 blocks of Kokrajhar districts 
have reportedly witnessed an increase in cases in 2024, with the majority of cases from the 
northern part of the district. 
5.17.2  The Department submitted that the following actions have been taken to 
contain the disease: - 
 
i. The district administration and health authorities launched emergency 
interventions, including enhanced vector control measures and public 
awareness campaigns. 
ii. With the technical guidance of the NCVBDC tam, the State team 
immediately conducted field visits to the affected area for ensuring 
supportive supervision and monitoring of ongoing activities. 
iii. The Central Teams (Senior Regional Director, Guwahati and NCVBDC) 
conducted field visits on December 2 and during December 25-28, 2024 and 
observed that the majority of cases are concentrated in remote, forested areas. 
It was recommended to intensify surveillance and implement programme 
activities throughout the year, along with improving Long Lasting 
Insecticidal Net(LLIN_ distribution expanding Indoor Residual Spraying 
(IRS) coverage, and enhancing capacity building for field workers. 
Accordingly, LLINs distribution was initiated and IRS planned for the 
district. 
iv. All antimalarial and RDT kits were made available in sufficient quantities in 
all health facilities. 
v. State government was advised to devise and implement a concrete and robust 
monitoring mechanism to ensure follow-up for treatment completion for 
malaria positive cases. 
vi. While 34,268 LLINs were distributed in the Kokrajhar district, the State 
Government has been required to ensure proper usage of LLINs, conduct 
timely IRS activities by following correct procedure, and implement fogging 
in high transmission area. 
5.17.3  The Committee expresses concern that all 4 blocks of Kokrajhar districts 
have reportedly witnessed an increase in cases in 2024, with the majority of cases from 

159 
 
the northern part of the district. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that 
the Department should persuade the State Government to undertake concrete steps for 
combating the menace of malaria in Kokrajhar district, at the earliest. The Committee 
further recommends that the Department must ensure strengthening of treatment 
follow-up and capacity building mechanism of healthcare professionals to eliminate 
malaria from the country. 
5.18 PUBLIC PRIVATE PARTNERSHIP (PPP) MODEL FOR PROMOTING 
MEDICAL TOURISM 
5.18.1  As per the Government of India (Allocation of Business) Rules, 1961, the 
subject matter of 'Development and Promotion of Tourism' is allocated to the Ministry of 
Tourism. As such, the matter of boosting Medical Tourism falls under the purview of 
Ministry of Tourism. Additionally, the Ministry of External Affairs handles the ‘Medical and 
Ayush Visa Portal’ launched to streamline and regulate entry of genuine foreigners and 
ensure that only genuine hospitals are inviting the genuine patients for treatment by pre-
validating Medical Visa. This portal enables hospitals, on-board the portal ‘Medical and 
Ayush Visa Portal’, to generate Medical Visa invitation Letter and Medical Visa Extension 
Letter for Medical Visa/Medical Attendant Visa/Ayush Attendant Visa using the following 
URL: https://indianfrro.gov.in/frro/medicalvaluetravel. The pre-validation of Medical Visa is 
pre requisite to acquire Medical Visa and Medical Visa will be issued after pre-validation. 
 
5.18.2  The Committee understands that the promotion of medical tourism 
requires multi-disciplinary and multi-pronged approach, thereby seeks the involvement 
of Ministry of Tourism and the Ministry of External Affairs but no one can exclude the 
pivotal role of the Department of Health and Family Welfare because the subject health 
where upon the focus is to be made for promoting medical tourism. The Committee 
perceives that the rate of medical tourism growth is much higher than the normal 
tourism growth, therefore the role of the Department of Health and Family Welfare is of 
pivotal importance. The Committee observes that growth of medical tourism in private 
sector is better than the public sector, therefore, there is enormous scope for promoting 
medical tourism in public sector. The Committee further recommends that the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare, in tandem with the Ministry of Tourism and 
the Ministry of External Affairs, leave no stone unturned in emphasising Public Private 
Partnership (PPP) model for promoting medical tourism.  
5.19 ACCESSSIBLE AND AFFORDABLE  TREATMENT OF RARE DISEASES 
5.19.1  The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare has formulated National Policy for 
Rare Diseases (NPRD), 2021. As per the policy, Rare Diseases are categorized into three 
groups: - 
 
a. Rare diseases requiring one time curative and definitive treatment (e.g. Disorders 
amenable with Hematopoietic stem Cell Transplantation (HSCT) and organ 
transplantation). 

160 
 
b. Rare Diseases requiring life-long treatment with good response on therapy: (e.g. 
Phenylketonuria, tyrosinemia type-1 and 2, Maple Syrup Urine Disease, Prader Willi 
Syndrome etc.) 
c. Rare Diseases requiring life-long disease stabilization and prolongation of life is only 
possible (only ambulatory improvement): e.g. Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy, Spinal 
Muscular Atrophy, Pompe Disease, Gaucher’s Disease, Cystic Fibrosis etc.). 
 
5.19.2  As per NPRD, 2021, 13 Centres of Excellence have been identified, which are 
premier Government tertiary hospitals for diagnosis, prevention and treatment of rare 
diseases. Currently, 63 Rare Diseases are covered under the policy. However, this list is not 
exhaustive and is reviewed periodically based on the recommendations of Central Technical 
Committee for Rare Diseases (CTCRD). 
 
5.19.3  The Committee notes that 63 rare diseases are covered under National 
Policy for Rare Diseases (NPRD) which of course needs to be reviewed and expanded 
from time to time. In order to make the treatment of rare diseases accessible and 
affordable, the Department may make provision for cheaper diagnosis, prevention and 
treatment. The Committee also desires that the Department should launch national 
campaign/awareness programme for seeking treatment of the rare diseases.  
5.20 OTHER RECOMMENDATIONS OF THE COMMITTEE  
(i) The sanctioning of Medical colleges should be on the basis of population and not 
district-wise as this creates imbalance in Hospital to population ratio. Moreover, the 
quality of Medicine and Nursing education should be as per National Quality Assurance 
Standards (NQAS) and the global best practices;  
(ii) Private medical education should be made cheaper and the Capitation fees needs 
to be regulated to make the medical education more affordable and accessible to merit 
candidates with poor financial background; 
(iii) Expediting the process of consideration of HPV vaccine roll-out plan and 
bringing the same under the Universal Immunisation Programme (UIP); 
(iv) Making availability of Doctors at PHC/CHC level and lucrative pay and perks 
for specialist doctors ensuring their retention in the Government Hospitals. Department 
may persuade the States/UTs to overcome the shortage of doctors at PHCs/CHCs 
through various measures including the creation of separate Medical Services 
Recruitment Boards, specialist cadres, and providing flexibilities under NHM for 
recruitment and retention; 
(v) Provision of sufficient staff, essential diagnostics and quality generic medicines 
must be made available in Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (AAMs); 
(vi) Strengthening healthcare facilities upto District Hospital level so that patient 
need not be referred to tertiary hospitals;  

161 
 
(vii)  Strengthening infrastructure including Advanced Life Support (ALS) 
ambulances to handle cardiac cases and making available essential medicines and para-
medical staff at rural health centres as per the norms of the Indian Public Health 
Standards (IPHS) 2022 for strengthening/improving rural health infrastructure. Efforts 
should be made that all Health Schemes should be implemented at ground level 
especially in rural, semi-urban, tribal and hilly areas; 
(viii) Keeping in view the pivotal role of ASHA workers in the effective 
implementation of the various schemes of the Government on the ground level but being 
paid ranging from Rs. 5000- 9000 per month which is, of course, not sufficient for
