earning bread of two times for a single person, leave apart maintaining good health and 
basic needs, there is urgent need to enhance the honorarium of ASHA workers; 
(ix) Appreciating the decision of establishment of the Day Care Cancer Centres in all 
districts, particularly to provide the services to rural areas, in next three years, the 
Committee apprehends about the practical implementation of the provision on the 
ground level provided adequate budgetary allocation is made for the purpose. Although 
the Committee is uncertain about the Corporate Hospitals coming forward to join the 
programme, however, in case some Trust Hospitals come forward to participate in the 
programme, the Department should welcome the move and extend assistance & 
facilitate all kind of collaboration, co-operation and co-ordination network; 
(x) Keeping in view that the Department has formulated National Sickle Cell 
Anaemia Elimination Mission setting goal for screening of 7 crore population in the 
country. The Committee desires to know the number of population covered so far 
towards this goal and the number of persons given sickle cell genetic status card, along 
with the strategy chalked out to achieve the goal during 2026. The Committee wishes to 
highlight the incidence of sickle cell anaemia in tribal areas of the country, including 
Goa, where the Committee had recently visited and came across the prevalence of sickle 
cell anaemia in 1.5 lakh tribals, but without any certain provision for elimination of the 
disease. The Committee has further come across that even if ‘PSA’ Test Kit is available 
at the health centre, it is not opened till sufficient number of patients visit for full 
utilisation of the kit. The Committee is of the view that the health centre may be directed 
to open the ‘PSA’ Kit even if single patient visits the centre;  
(xi) Simplification of procedures/norms, rules and regulations for establishing 
nursing, occupational therapy & physiotherapy and dental colleges for ensuring their 
availability at the CHC and District Hospital level.  Arrangements be made for study of 
occupational therapy & physiotherapy in Government Hospital Colleges for producing 
sufficient number of occupational therapists and physiotherapists ensuring their 
services at the Block Level; and  
(xii) Continuous Glucose Monitors (CGMs) and diabetic medicines are essential for 
effective diabetes management which is a chronic disease condition. However, the high 
cost of CGMs, their limited lifespan and the lack of long-term insurance coverage for 
these devices and medicines create significant challenges for patients. The Committee, 

162 
 
therefore, recommends increasing the lifespan of CGM sensors, implementing price 
controls and price caps on CGM sensors, cardiac stents, etc., mandating insurance 
coverage for CGMs and diabetic medicines on a long term basis to ensure affordability 
and accessibility. The Committee further desires that ‘Make in India’ initiative should 
be promoted to reduce prices and achieve self-sufficiency in CGM production.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

163 
 
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS OF THE COMMITTEE-AT A GLANCE 
VISION MISSION AND OBJECTIVES 
 
1. The Committee takes note of the vision, mission and objectives of the Department 
of Health and Family Welfare which determines the roadmap for activities of the 
Department. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should 
undertake the projects/schemes/plans in consonance with the vision and mission of the 
Department and execute the same with the intention to achieve the cherished objectives. 
The Committee believes that the Department would move forward with Management by 
Objective (MBO) rather than Management by Activities (MBA) to achieve the targets 
set in National Health Policy (NHP) 2017 and under specific projects.  
(Para 1.3.2) 
INSTITUTIONAL MECHANISM 
 
2. The Committee is of the view that Institutions/Organisations are the organs of 
the Department which play pivotal role in the successful functioning of the Department, 
therefore, efficient and effective working of the organisations/institutions is 
indispensable for result-oriented implementation of the undertaken projects/schemes. 
The Committee, therefore, desires that the officers at the top of the Department should 
ensure to set up dynamic working equilibrium amongst various institutions in order to 
achieve the objectives of the Department. The Committee feels that a streamlined 
monitoring mechanism with Always Better Control (ABC) would further ensure the 
desirable Rate of Returns (RoR). The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should keep constant Strength, Weaknesses, Opportunities and Threats 
(SWOT) Analysis of its organs/institutions for better results.  
(Para 1.4.3) 
FLAGSHIP SCHEMES 
 
3. The Committee takes notes of the flagship schemes of the Department, successful 
implementation thereof ultimately rates the success of the Department as a whole. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should enhance the executing 
capacity of the implementing agency so that the absorbing capacity of the scheme is 
increased to the optimal level. This would ensure the optimum utilisation of the allocated 
funds under the schemes which would enable the Department to seek the budgetary 
allocation as per the projected demand in the ensuing financial years.  
(Para 1.5.2) 
 
STAFF STRENGTH IN THE DEPARTMENT 
 
4. The Committee observes that the vacancy under the different Divisions of the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare is 428 against the sanctioned strength of 1486 
for the year 2024. The Committee is concerned to note that there has been negligible 
improvement in filling up the vacant posts from 2023-24 when a total of 454 posts were 
reported to be vacant. The Committee notes that there is shortage of Officers/Staffs at 

164 
 
various levels in the Department of Health and Family Welfare which may impact the 
effective administrative functioning of the Ministry. The Committee, therefore, 
reiterates its recommendation made in the 143rd Report that the Ministry needs to take 
appropriate steps to fill up the vacant positions at various levels and implement better 
work management practices in the Department. The Committee recommends the 
Ministry to take up the matter of vacancies with the Cadre Controlling Authorities and 
recruiting agencies to expedite the recruitment of officers/Staffs at all levels in the 
Ministry. 
(Para 1.6.2) 
5. The Committee acknowledges the increasing integration of technology in the 
health sector and emphasizes that modernizing healthcare services is essential for 
ensuring sustainable and affordable care. In this context, it is crucial for the workforce 
overseeing National health initiatives to actively engage with technological 
advancements. Therefore, the Committee recommends that the Ministry of Health and 
Family Welfare expand the use of technology and implement measures to strengthen 
the digital proficiency of its officials and staff. 
(Para 1.6.3) 
BUDGETARY ALLOCATION 
 
6. The Department apprised the Committee that the budgetary allocation depends 
on the overall Government budgetary resources availability, the current demand by 
various Programme Divisions of the Department, availability of funds as per annual 
envelope of the schemes and pace of expenditure of the previous financial year. Ministry 
of Finance, accordingly, allocate ceiling of Budget Estimates. The Committee notes that 
a sum of Rs. 95,957.87 crore has been allocated to the Department of Health and Family 
Welfare for 2025-26, representing 1.89% of the total Union Budget estimate and 0.27% 
of GDP (at current prices). A review of past trends indicates that actual expenditure has 
often fallen short of the budgeted allocation, remaining at just 0.27% of GDP in both 
2022-23 and 2023-24. 
(Para 2.1.3) 
 
7. The Committee notes the 10.83% increase in the Budget Estimate (BE) for 2025-
26 as compared to the Revised Estimate (RE) for 2024-25, but the Committee expresses 
concern over trend of the health sector's budget allocation, as a percentage of GDP and 
the total Union Budget that has been consistently declining since 2022-23. There is a 
need to accelerate the rate of improving healthcare to global standard and proportional 
to the rate of population growth. India being the most populous country, and given the 
inflationary pressures and the National Health Policy's target of increasing government 
health expenditure to 2.5% of GDP by 2025, the Committee believes that the allocation 
to the health sector, particularly to the Department of Health and Family Welfare, 
should have been much higher. The Committee, therefore, urges the Department to take 
concrete steps to enhance the utilization of allocated funds effectively so that the targets 
set under a specific scheme are achieved. 
(Para 2.1.4) 

165 
 
BUDGETARY PROJECTION VIS-A-VIS ALLOCATION AND EXPENDITURE 
 
8. Over the past five years, it has been observed that the demand projected by the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare has been consistently low and has declined 
significantly compared to the 2021-22 level. Furthermore, budgetary allocations have 
continuously fallen short of the Department’s projected Demands for Grants, averaging 
a meager 0.3% of GDP at current prices. 
(Para 2.2.2) 
 
9. For the fiscal year 2025-26, the projected demand has increased to ₹1,00,702.79 
crore. However, given the inflation rate of 5.2% in December 2024, the population 
growth of the country and the National Health Policy’s goal of raising government 
health expenditure to 2.5% of GDP by 2025, the Committee believes that the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare must reassess its policy framework. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends the Department to request the Ministry of Finance 
for higher allocations for significantly enhancing healthcare infrastructure and 
healthcare services nationwide. This effort must be undertaken with urgency, 
particularly in light of the challenges faced during the COVID-19 pandemic, to ensure 
that the country is better prepared for the post-COVID era and for any future 
pandemics. 
(Para 2.2.3) 
 
10. The Committee notes that since FY 2022-23, the Revised Estimates have 
consistently been lower than the Budget Estimates. This trend undermines the 
credibility of the Department’s demand for higher fund allocations. Moreover, the 
concern is further reinforced by the fact that, except for FY 2023-24, the actual 
expenditure has remained below the Revised Estimate, which itself is already lower than 
the Budget Estimate. This raises concerns about the effective utilization of allocated 
funds in real terms. 
(Para 2.2.4) 
 
11. The Committee notes that, as of January 31, 2025, the Department had utilized 
81.32% of the total Revised Estimate, leaving 18.68% of the funds, approximately 
₹16,000 crores, available unspent for the remaining two months. The Ministry of 
Finance has repeatedly issued guidelines to regulate expenditure within a financial year. 
In its 143rd Report, the Committee recommended that the Department of Health and 
Family Welfare cap its expenditure in the final month of the financial year in 
accordance with existing guidelines and closely monitor fund utilization during the last 
quarter. This is crucial to ensuring the prudent and efficient use of allocated funds. 
(Para 2.2.5) 
 
12. Public health plays a crucial role in driving the country’s economic development. 
Given the inadequacy and overstretched nature of public health infrastructure, 
investments in the health sector must be increased. With inflation expected to remain 
high in the near term, the Department should consider for requesting higher budgetary 

166 
 
allocations for the health sector at the Revised Estimate stage and ensure the optimal 
utilization of allocated funds. 
(Para 2.2.6) 
 
13. The Committee notes that the primary determinant for revenue and capital 
outlay in healthcare budgeting is the annual, recurring demands from program divisions 
within the Department of Health & Family Welfare, coupled with the progress of 
ongoing projects. While the social service nature of the Department inherently limits 
capital expenditure, a recent push for infrastructure upgrades is evident, with "GIA-
Capital Creation" (grants-in-aid for capital creation) playing a significant role in 
boosting effective capital spending, despite being categorized under revenue. Analyzing 
the five-year trend reveals to the Committee that revenue expenditure consistently 
dominates, averaging over 90% of the total budget. However, the effective capital 
expenditure, including GIA-Capital, shows a fluctuating pattern in the period from FY 
2021-22 to FY 2025-26, decreasing consistently since the FY 2023-24, from 27.22 % in 
2022-23 to 15.60% in 2025-26 of the total allocation. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends establishing a formalized, multi-year strategic capital expenditure plan, 
distinct from annual recurring demands, that clearly outlines infrastructure 
development priorities and targets, including specific allocations for GIA-Capital, to 
ensure sustained and predictable growth in healthcare capital assets, thereby creating a 
more balanced approach between revenue and capital spending. 
(Para 2.2.8) 
 
GOVERNMENT HEALTH EXPENDITURE IN INDIA 
 
14. National Health Policy (NHP), 2017 prescribed increasing Government Health 
Expenditure (GHE) as a share of GDP to 2.5% by 2025. Following the objective of NHP, 
2017, GHE, as a share of GDP, has increased from 1.35% in 2017-18 to 1.84% in 2021-
22, indicating progress in health sector investment. The Committee notes that the 
upward trend in GHE is positive; however, further acceleration is required to meet the 
2.5% GDP target by 2025. The current pace of increase may not be sufficient to achieve 
this goal within the remaining timeframe. A strategic focus on public health 
infrastructure, preventive care, and efficient resource utilization will be crucial in 
ensuring long-term improvements in healthcare accessibility and quality. 
(Para 2.3.3) 
 
15. A close scrutiny of the Health Budget reflects the lack of priority assigned to 
health. The Committee observes that the declining trend in health budget allocation in 
terms of BE as % of GDP (at Current Price) between 2020-21 and 2024-25 (refer to 
Table 3 above) do not align with the target set in National Health Policy. The Committee 
is of the opinion that adequate health financing forms the pillar of a well-functioning 
health system which is crucial for reduction of Out-of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) in 
health. The pandemic further reaffirmed the need of a sustainable health financing 
ecosystem especially in tackling a sudden public health crisis. The Committee 
recommends the Government to prioritize Health Sector in Budget Allocation by 

167 
 
increasing the annual budget allocation for healthcare to maintain the momentum and 
ensure sufficient resources for infrastructure, medical research, and service delivery. 
(Para 2.3.4) 
 
16. The Committee, in its 126th Report on DFG (2022-23), had also recommended the 
Government for increasing its health expenditure to 5% of GDP by 2025. However, with 
such stagnant allocation to health and the slow pace of increase in allocation, the goal of 
achieving the NHP target of health expenditure to 2.5% seems a distant dream.  
(Para 2.3.5) 
 
17. The Committee is of the view that strengthening of public health infrastructure,  
enhancing preventive and primary care services, leveraging Public-Private Partnerships 
(PPP), exploring additional revenue sources, innovation in health, robust monitoring 
and evaluation mechanism to track the utilization of health funds and assess the impact 
are crucial for establishing an affordable and accessible healthcare delivery system, 
especially for a developing economy like India. The need of the hour is to recognize 
health as a key sector for economic as well as social development. The Committee, 
therefore, strongly recommends the Government to make health a priority and increase 
its investment in the health sector.  
(Para 2.3.6) 
 
18. The Committee acknowledges that various Government schemes have 
progressively expanded the scope of social security and broadened the list of 
beneficiaries. This sustained intervention has contributed to a notable decline in Out-of-
Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) on healthcare, which has reduced from 48.8% in 2017-18 
to 39.4% in 2021-22. The Committee is quite optimistic that the current figures for 
OOPE might be lower than 39.4. However, the Committee apprehends that 39.4% 
OOPE out of THE continues to be a significant barrier to accessing quality healthcare. 
As per the Global Multidimensional Poverty Index 2024, India is home to approximately 
234 million poor people, the highest in the world. Also, in the five countries with the 
largest number of people living in poverty, India is the only country with medium 
Human Development Index (HDI) and the remaining four are low HDI. Given this large 
population deprived of basic amenities, increased Government health spending is crucial 
to ensuring affordable healthcare access.  
(Para 2.3.10) 
 
19. The Committee observes that GHE as % of GDP of India (1.84%) is very low 
when compared to the developed countries like USA, UK, Japan and Germany. Hence, 
there is ample inspiration for the Government to make sustained efforts to increase the 
GHE which would have the declining effects on OOPE. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Government intensify efforts to increase GHE and further reduce 
OOPE. The Committee urges the Government to adopt best practices from High-
Income Countries (HICs), where nearly 70% of healthcare expenditure is publicly 
funded, and ensuring greater financial protection for citizens. 
(Para 2.3.11) 

168 
 
 
20. The Committee understands that increasing the public investment on health 
depends on several factors such as State Government’s health budget depending on their 
allocations, capacities and fund position, transfer to states on the recommendation on 
Finance Commission, allocation made under Centrally Sponsored Schemes by Union 
Government based on total budgetary resources. The Committee appreciates that the 25 
States and the Union Territories of Delhi and Puducherry as per the NHA Estimates for 
FY 2021-22 have achieved the National Health Policy target of 8% of their budget 
allocated to health. The Committee recommends that other State/UT Governments 
should follow the same. 
(Para 2.3.13) 
 
21. The Committee, in its 134th Report, had highlighted the existing wide inter-State 
and intra-State disparity in healthcare infrastructure across the country. The 
Committee observes that States with poor financial resources and inefficient fiscal 
capacity face umpteen challenges in establishing a robust health infrastructure. The 
Committee, accordingly, reiterates its recommendation that the Union Government 
must persuade the remaining State/UT Governments to achieve the prescribed 
budgetary health expenditure to 8% of State General Government Expenditure. 
(Para 2.3.14) 
 
22. The Committee observes that the data on General Health Expenditure (GHE), 
Total Health Expenditure (THE), Out-of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE), and other 
relevant indicators is only available up to the year 2021-22, with the National Health 
Accounts (NHA) Estimates for 2021-22 being released in September 2024. The 
Committee expresses concern over delay in updating data as old and obsolete data 
significantly hampers the assessment of the progress made by various government 
schemes and policies. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Government to 
update data pertaining to GHE, THE, OOPE, etc. without delay, sooner the better.  
(Para 2.3.15) 
 
23. In today's digital age, where Information Technology and Artificial Intelligence 
play a key role, it is crucial to leverage these tools for the efficient collection and real-
time processing of data. Therefore, the Committee recommends that the Government 
take immediate steps to integrate digital tools into the data collection process. 
Additionally, the Government should ensure that data for a given financial year is made 
available by the last quarter of the following financial year. This approach will 
rationalize data analysis, enabling more effective evaluation and improvement of 
government schemes and policies, ensuring they are aligned with the intended objectives 
and targeted effectively. 
(Para 2.3.16) 
15th FINANCE COMMISSION HEALTH GRANTS 
 
24. The Committee observes that, as of January 30, 2025, only 39.42% of the funds 
approved by the 15th Finance Commission for the States have been released, with only 

169 
 
the funds for the fiscal year 2021-22 being fully disbursed. The States have reported 
spending only 59.31% of the released funds. These funds were specifically allocated to 
address gaps in primary healthcare facilities across the country. However, the States' 
low utilization of these funds undermines the purpose of this financial support. In light 
of these concerns, the Committee strongly recommends that the Government conduct a 
comprehensive study to determine the underlying causes of this under-utilization and 
implement measures to resolve these issues. Furthermore, the Union Government should 
establish a dedicated communication channel with the States to facilitate continuous 
dialogue and ensure timely and effective resolution of challenges, thereby optimizing the 
utilization of these critical funds for primary healthcare. The Committee expresses 
serious concern that the Department must persuade the states for optimal utilization of 
funds released to the States. 
(Para 2.4.4) 
 
CENTRAL GOVERNMENT HEALTH SCHEME (CGHS):  
 
25. The Committee acknowledges the Government's efforts to digitize healthcare 
resources through the Healthcare Management Information System (HMIS), establish 
new Wellness Centres in tier-II cities, and promote Ayush Centres. However, the 
examination of budget allocation and utilization under Revenue Head reflects a 
significant gap between the RE allocation of Rs. 2245.82 crore for CGHS and the actual 
expenditure of Rs. 1842.51 crore, representing only 82% utilization as of February 7, 
2025. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department conducts a thorough 
and immediate review of the CGHS expenditure process, specifically focusing on 
identifying and addressing the bottlenecks causing the underutilization of allocated 
funds. By streamlining administrative procedures, improving procurement processes, 
and ensuring timely disbursement of funds, the Department can ensure that the 
allocated resources are effectively used to enhance healthcare services for beneficiaries, 
thereby maintaining the intended continuity between budget allocation and service 
delivery. 
(Para 3.1.4) 
 
26. In its 155th Report, the Committee observed that, as of August 2023, 26% of the 
340 allopathic wellness centres (spread across 80 cities) were concentrated in the Delhi 
NCR region. Additionally, only six states (excluding Delhi NCR) have more than ten 
CGHS centres, while Arunachal Pradesh and the Union Territories of Ladakh, 
Andaman & Nicobar Islands, and Lakshadweep have none. The Committee noted that 
CGHS services are unevenly distributed, limiting accessibility in many regions. The 
Committee, therefore, wishes to highlight that numerous cities and towns with a 
significant population of Central Government employees, pensioners, and their 
dependents lack CGHS wellness centres. As a result, beneficiaries—particularly 
retirees—have to travel long distances for medical treatment, causing financial strain 
and other hardships. To address this, the Committee urges the Ministry to proactively 
advocate for new CGHS centres in underserved areas, particularly in suburban regions 
with a substantial number of serving and retired beneficiaries. The Committee 

170 
 
recommends that the Ministry engage with the Ministry of Finance for speedy approval 
of new CGHS Wellness Centres. Moreover, the Committee suggests that the 
Government should consider revising existing norms or introducing new ones to 
facilitate the establishment of wellness centres in rural, hilly, remote, and Northeastern 
regions. 
(Para 3.1.5) 
 
27. The Committee notes the Government’s initiative to integrate the Indian system 
of medicine with the CGHS wellness centre network. However, in its 155th Report, the 
Committee notes that the number of Ayush centres increased from 85 in March 2014 to 
107 in August 2023, a modest 25% growth over nine years. As of February 2025, this 
number has just risen slightly to 111. The Committee observes with concern that such 
limited slow pace of expansion does not align with the Government’s vision to promote 
indigenous healthcare to under-served regions especially in remote areas. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends the speedy operationalisation of the remaining 23 
Ayush units out of the targeted 53 and urges the Government to establish additional 
Ayush centres nationwide while actively promoting traditional Indian medicine among 
CGHS beneficiaries. 
(Para 3.1.6) 
 
28. The Committee takes into account the proposal to establish a State-of-the-Art 
Multi-facility Centre in Mumbai. The Committee reiterates its recommendation, as 
made in its 155th Report, for setting up at least ten CGHS hospitals with advanced 
facilities and research capabilities, particularly for critical illnesses such as cancer, 
cardiovascular diseases, and renal failure. The Committee further desires that at least 
one such advanced multi-facility centre be established in major cities across the country. 
(Para 3.1.7) 
 
29. The Committee wishes to highlight with concerns over the quality of medicines 
dispensed at dispensary/pharmacy counters. At several time, apprehensions and 
complaints have been raised regarding the effectiveness of medicines procured through 
MSO and JAP, underscoring the critical importance of quality in healthcare services. 
Ensuring compliance with the World Health Organization’s (WHO) Good 
Manufacturing Practice (GMP) standards is essential, and any quality-related 
complaints must be addressed seriously and quickly to generate faith towards quality of 
medicines delivered at dispensary counters. 
(Para 3.1.9)  
 
30. The Committee takes into account the acknowledgement of the Department 
about the quality assurance of drugs, and the need for stronger quality assurance 
measures viz. implementing a systematic drug testing program at the Wellness Centre 
level. The Directorate is also considering empanelling the Quality Council of India for 
enhanced monitoring. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Government 
establish a rigorous quality control mechanism without delay, ensuring systematic drug 
testing at the Wellness Centre level. Additionally, the Department should conduct 

171 
 
surprise audits by randomly testing medicine batches through an independent NABL-
accredited or internationally reputed laboratory. 
(Para 3.1.10) 
 
31. The Committee continues to recommend the Department to simplify referral 
procedures further as there is need to significantly reduce the burden on patients and 
their families by eliminating the requirement for repeated visits to dispensaries during 
illness. The Committee notes the provision made allowing CGHS pensioner beneficiaries 
aged 70 and above to directly consult specialists without requiring a CGHS referral, 
however, the Committee reiterates its recommendation made in the 155th Report to 
further review the guidelines and extend the benefit to all CGHS 
pensioners/beneficiaries aged 60 and above for continuing medical care to CGHS 
beneficiaries. Allowing direct specialist consultations at empanelled private hospitals 
without a CGHS referral would ensure consistency and continuity of care with the 
existing system, where pensioners/beneficiaries aged 60 and above already receive 
cashless treatment from these hospitals. 
(Para 3.1.12) 
 
32. The Committee observes significantly high number of vacancies in CGHS 
dispensaries, with 2,023 positions (28.81% of the sanctioned strength) unfilled in 
Allopathic Dispensaries and 111 positions (26.12%) in Ayush Dispensaries. Needless to 
say that the vacancies negatively impact the doctor-to-beneficiary ratio, increase patient 
waiting times, and reduce the quality of care. To address this, the Committee 
recommends that the Government should promptly fill up all vacant positions to ensure 
adequate staffing. The Department should evaluate the required number of doctors in 
CGHS dispensaries nationwide and maintain a sufficient doctor-to-population ratio in 
all CGHS cities. A minimum doctor-per-day consultation criterion should be established 
based on the number of beneficiaries at each Wellness Centre and the criterion must be 
strictly enforced. Ensuring adequate staffing is essential for the effective functioning of 
CGHS dispensaries and improving patient care. 
(Para 3.1.14) 
 
33. The Committee wishes to express an observation regarding queue-management 
of patients seeking treatment in the CGHS dispensaries. It has been seen that in some of 
the private/public establishments, the queue of patients is managed through electronic 
token system displaying the queue number of the patient with corresponding token slip 
with the patient. The Committee would like the Department to establish electronic token 
system in all dispensaries of CGHS displaying the name and queue number of the 
patient to facilitate better queue management of patients in order to avoid high patient 
load at the doctors cabin and give convenience to critical patients waiting for their turn. 
(Para 3.1.15) 
 
 
 

172 
 
SAFDURJUNG HOSPITAL (SJH) & VARDHMAN MAHAVIR MEDICAL 
COLLEGE (VMMC) 
 
34. The Committee takes note of delay in the construction of the lecture theatre and 
boys' hostel of the SJH and VMMC Institute, where tree transplantation work has been 
kept on hold in compliance of directions of the Hon’ble High Court of Delhi. To address 
this issue, the Committee desires that the Department promptly pursue the matter with 
court and take necessary steps to resolve the litigation on priority. This will help prevent 
any adverse impact on project timelines. Additionally, the Committee suggests that a 
review of such legal challenges be incorporated into policy-making processes to mitigate 
similar obstacles in future projects. 
(Para 3.2.4) 
 
35. The Committee acknowledges the increase in capital outlay from ₹119.15 crore in 
RE 2024-25 to ₹238.50 crore in BE 2025-26. However, it notes that the shortfall in 
capital allocation during FY 2024-25 adversely impacted the progress of key 
infrastructure projects, including the construction of the MCH block, Auditorium, Boys' 
Hostel at VMMC, Dwarka Housing Project, Raj Nagar Staff Quarters, and 
Dharamshala Project. The Committee urges the Department, subject to the progress of 
pending court cases, to enhance the capital outlay further at RE stage for timely 
completion of all infrastructure projects in the FY 2025-26. 
(Para 3.2.5) 
 
36. Given that the Dwarka Housing Project has achieved 24% physical progress as of 
February 2025, with a tentative completion date of 31.03.2026, the Committee urges the 
Government to ensure the timely completion of all these projects. If additional funds are 
required, they should be provided at the RE stage to prevent further delays. Timely 
completion is essential to enhance facilities and improve the overall functioning of the 
Institute. The Committee in its 134th and 143rd Reports had flagged the issues related to 
slow pace of construction activities and recommended the Department to adhere to the 
timeline of 27 months for completion of the Dwarka Project. 
(Para 3.2.6) 
37. The Committee notes that almost 32.6% of posts in Group A, 87.5% in Group B 
Gazetted, 16.5% in Group B Non-Gazetted and 35.3% in Group C are lying vacant in 
SJH and VMMC. Large vacancies in workforce will have an impact on the smooth 
functioning of the Institute. The Committee observes that the significant shortages in 
staff numbers across various groups in SJH and VMMC are impacting the institute’s 
optimal functioning. To address this, the Committee recommends that the Department 
take immediate steps to expedite recruitment across all vacant positions, ensuring that 
administrative manpower aligns with the increasing number of UG and PG seats, as well 
as teaching faculty requirements. Additionally, vacancies in non-faculty positions, 
particularly in the Academic Section and VMMC Administration, must be filled up 
without delay to support smooth operations. 
(Para 3.2.8) 

173 
 
DR. RAMMANOHAR LOHIA HOSPITAL (RML), NEW DELHI AND ABVIMS 
 
38. The Committee notes the increase in budget allocation for Dr. RML Hospital and 
ABVIMS in BE 2025-26, particularly the 44.84% rise in capital outlay, which is essential 
for completing infrastructure projects, addressing space constraints, and upgrading 
diagnostic and medical facilities to global standards. The Committee, however, expresses 
concern over the trend of fund under-utilization. In FY 2023-24, RML and ABVIMS 
was allocated BE amounting to Rs. 1272.18 crore which was increased to Rs. 1304.10 at 
RE stage, the actual expenditure was Rs. 1195.29 crore. Hence, despite an increased 
allocation at the RE stage, actual expenditure fell short by ₹108.81 crore. Similarly, in 
FY 2024-25, the allocated budget of Rs. 1610 crore at BE stage was significantly reduced 
to Rs. 1220 crore at the RE stage, particularly in capital outlay the decrease was by 58% 
(from Rs. 558.17 at BE stage to Rs. 233.43 at RE stage), and the actual spending 
remained even lower than the revised estimate. This pattern highlights inefficiencies in 
fund utilization, particularly under the capital head. Given the hospital's critical role in 
serving patients from both Delhi and other regions, the Committee underscores the 
urgent need for optimal fund utilization. 
(Para 3.3.3) 
 
39. Budgetary provisions have been made for constructing a Super Specialty Block 
to house existing and new specialties such as Medical and Surgical Oncology, Nuclear 
Medicine, Radiotherapy, Organ Transplant Medicine, and Pediatric Cardiology. The 
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that the Department take concrete steps to 
ensure the optimal and timely utilization of the allocated funds, particularly for capital 
projects. This should include strengthening Project Planning and Execution, regular 
Monitoring & Review, streamlining Administrative Approvals, and enhanced 
Coordination with Stakeholders. By implementing these measures, the Department can 
ensure that the increased budgetary allocation translates into tangible improvements in 
hospital infrastructure and patient care. 
(Para 3.3.4) 
 
40. The Ministry has identified the major challenges faced by the institution as 
Staffing Shortages, Space Constraints and Infrastructure Delays Project 
Implementation Status. While examining the implementation status of projects 
undertaken by the institute, the Committee wishes to highlight its 
observation/recommendation contained in 143rd Report that the Super Specialty Block 
(SSB) was originally scheduled for completion on March 10, 2022, later extended to 
November 28, 2023. The revised deadline of April 27, 2024 was not met and the new 
expected handover date is May 31, 2025. Further, in respect of the Medical College, the 
Committee notes that the tender, floated by the consulting agency HITES, is yet to be 
opened, leading to further delays. The Committee reviews the Statement of Expenditure 
(as of February 7, 2025) and finds poor and inefficient fund utilization for the ongoing 

174 
 
projects. The Committee is deeply concerned about the repeated extensions and 
inefficiencies in project execution. 
(Para 3.3.6)  
 
41. To address these issues related to delay in projects and inefficient utilization of 
funds, the Committee strongly recommends the Department to adopt a robust project 
management strategy by implementing advanced tools and best practices, strengthen 
accountability mechanisms and strictly adhere to the new completion timeline to avoid 
further extensions. By implementing these measures, the Institute can improve 
efficiency, avoid further delays, and ensure timely project completion. 
(Para 3.3.7) 
42. The Committee notes with concern that nearly 23% of posts across various 
cadres remain vacant. Needless to say that such significant workforce shortage hinders 
the smooth functioning of the Institute. The Committee, specifically, observes that 
staffing deficiencies in Dr. RML Hospital and ABVIMS are affecting their operational 
efficiency. To address the issue, the Committee recommends the Department to take 
immediate measures to expedite the recruitment process for filling up of all vacant 
positions. It is essential to ensure that the administrative and teaching faculty strength 
keeps pace with the increasing number of undergraduate and postgraduate seats. 
(Para 3.3.9) 
43. Additionally, on a specific query, the Committee has been informed that the 
institute currently lacks collaborations with other colleges to develop a stronger 
technical workforce. The Committee, in this connection, recommends that the institute 
explore partnerships with other institutions to facilitate the sharing of infrastructure 
and knowledge. Leveraging IT and digital tools can further enhance these efforts. 
(Para 3.3.10) 
44. Furthermore, the Committee notes that premier hospitals like Dr. RML Hospital, 
particularly in metropolitan cities, are struggling with high patient inflow in OPD, 
facing challenges in managing large patient volumes leaves long waiting periods for 
surgeries, and delays in diagnostic tests. The Committee recommends that these critical 
issues be prioritized in policy-making to ensure accessible, affordable, and high-quality 
healthcare for the public. 
(Para 3.3.11) 
 
45. While examining the Demands for Grants for 2025-26, the Committee observes 
that the total budgetary allocation of Rs. 750 crore at the Budget Estimate (BE) stage for 
2024-25, which included a capital outlay of Rs. 101 crore, was reduced to Rs. 630 crore 
at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage, with the capital outlay declining to Rs. 51.50 crore. 
When asked about the reasons for this variation, the Department attributed it to delays 
in obtaining approvals from the competent authority in the early part of the financial 
year. The Committee is particularly concerned that the capital outlay was reduced by 

175 
 
49% at the RE stage in 2024-25. To address this issue, the Committee recommends that 
the Department should streamline the approval process and establish a Standard 
Operating Procedure (SOP) to ensure efficiency and reduce delays. Additionally, 
monitoring of fund utilization and evaluation of infrastructure projects should be 
strengthened to enhance effective implementation. 
(Para 3.4.2) 
 
46. The Committee observes that there is significant manpower crunch across 
various groups in the LHMC and SKH institutions impacting the institute’s optimal 
functioning. The in-position strength is only 64% of the sanctioned strength which also 
includes the contractual staff. Some of the critical cadre like Neurology and Cardiology 
have zero in-position strength. The Committee take a serious view of this manpower 
crunch and recommends the Department to take immediate steps to streamline the 
recruitment process and fill up the vacant positions at the earliest. 
(Para 3.4.4) 
 
KALAWATI SARAN CHILDREN’S HOSPITAL (KSCH) 
 
47. The Committee, specifically, expresses concern on high under-utilisation of funds 
under capital head which is pivotal for up-gradation of infrastructure and maintenance 
of machinery and equipments in the hospital. The Committee, therefore, urges the 
Department to ensure optimal utilisation of budget allocated to the institution by 
assisting the institute in review of the reasons for under-utilisation and in timely 
approval of the proposals/projects under consideration. 
(Para 3.5.2) 
   
48. The Department provided the details of sanctioned strength vis-à-vis in position 
(contractual and regular) strength across various cadres of the Kalawati Saran 
Children’s Hospital (KSCH) that of the sanctioned strength of 762, there exist 139 
vacant posts which is 18% of the sanctioned strength. The Committee, therefore, finds 
that there is manpower crunch in the institution which should be addressed on priority 
basis to fill up the vacant posts.  
(Para 3.5.4) 
 
49. The Committee recommends that the Government prioritize addressing the 
infrastructure-related challenges of KSCH and expedite the approval and 
implementation of the proposed up-gradation project in a time-bound manner. 
(Para 3.5.6) 
 
50. The Committee has noted that Central Hospitals face major challenges, 
primarily related to inadequate infrastructure and manpower shortages. The 
Government should implement strategic measures to address these challenges by 
expanding and upgrading hospital facilities, strengthening workforce capacity through 
recruitment and training, and improving hygiene standards and waste management 
systems. 
(Para 3.5.7) 

176 
 
PRADHAN MANTRI SWASTHAYA SURAKSHA YOJANA (PMSSY) 
 
51. The Committee expresses concern over the significant reduction in the PMSSY 
Scheme's budget allocation from Rs. 2200 crore in Budget Estimates (BE) 2024-25 to Rs. 
1736 crore in Revised Estimates (RE) 2024-25. While noting the actual expenditure of 
Rs. 1552.36 crore as of 31.01.2025, indicating 89.42% of the RE allocation and 70.56% 
of the BE, the Committee emphasizes the importance of consistent and adequate funding 
for the effective implementation of the PMSSY Scheme. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends the Ministry to ensure the judicious and optimal utilization of the 
remaining allocated funds by the end of the financial year. The Ministry should 
investigate and implement processes to increase the efficiency of spending, to ensure the 
maximum benefit from the allocated funds towards achievement of set 
targets/objectives/goals under PMSSY. 
(Para 3.6.3) 
 
52. Recognising the allocation of Rs. 2200 crore (Rs. 60 crore Revenue and Rs. 2140 
crore Capital) in BE 2025-26, the Committee urges upon the Ministry to even utilization 
of funds throughout the financial year, thereby avoiding significant reductions at the RE 
stage, unless absolutely necessary and supported by compelling reasons/justifications. 
The efficient utilization of funds is indispensable to ensure the effective implementation 
of the PMSSY Scheme and achieve its intended healthcare objectives. 
(Para 3.6.4) 
 
53. The Committee recommends that the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, in 
collaboration with state governments, establish a standardized and expedited process for 
land acquisition and obtaining statutory clearances for AIIMS projects. This should 
include formulating clear guidelines and timelines for state governments regarding the 
provision of providing encumbrance-free land; creating a fast-track mechanism for 
obtaining environmental and other necessary clearances; and establishing a dedicated 
coordination cell to address site-related hindrances. Delays in land acquisition and 
statutory clearances are significant bottlenecks in the timely completion of AIIMS 
projects. As evident by the situations in Darbhanga, Madurai, and Rewari, these delays 
lead to cost escalations and hinder the delivery of essential healthcare services. 
(Para 3.6.9) 
 
54. The Committee takes into account various challenges confronting the 
Department that result into delay in implementation of projects and strategy measures 
chalked out to combat those challenges for effective utilization of funds earmarked for 
various institutions under PMSSY and timely completion of projects though the 
Committee understands that certain challenges are really a Himalayan barrier, the 
resolution of which is of course herculean task. Despite that the Committee believes that 
the Pro-Active Governance and Timely Implementation (PRAGATI) Portal, reviewed 
by the Prime Minister for expeditious resolution of various issues like land acquisition, 
utility shifting, contains the strength of mount-melting capacity with quick and effective 
decision-making for speedy resolution of all vexing issues/challenges. The Committee 

177 
 
feels that the two components of PMSSY i.e. setting up of new AIIMS and up-gradation 
of Government Medical College/Institutions equipped with basic infrastructural 
facilities and equipments contain capacity measures for correcting regional imbalance in 
the availability of affordable/reliable tertiary healthcare services and to augment 
facilities for quality medical education in the country. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends the Government for identifying more GMCs (beyond already identified 75 
GMCs) and setting up of more AIIMS-like institutions as requests received by the 
Department from Karnataka, Kerala, Goa, Tripura, Sikkim, Mizoram, Manipur, Uttar 
Pradesh, Bihar (in Kosi region) and Maharashtra in the next phase of PMSSY keeping 
in view the density of population, distance from existing AIIMS and regions from where 
patients inflow to AIIMS, New Delhi for treatment, increasing its workload and pushing 
up the waiting time even for critical patients.  
(Para 3.6.16) 
 
55. The Committee notes the provisons made by the Department for augmenting 
faculty and non-faculty strength in the newly set-up AIIMS viz. Standing Selection 
Committees,   27.  Centralised Nursing Officer Recruitment Common Eligiblity Test 
(NORCET), CRE, rolling advertisement for recruitment, INI-CET, INI-SS and the 
Committee hopes that measures so undertaken would yield outcome in a time-frame 
manner. The Committee urges upon the Ministry to implement comprehensive 
strategies for the recruitment and retention of faculty and staff in new AIIMS, 
particularly in remote locations. The Department should offer attractive compensation 
packages and incentives to attract and retain qualified personnel; developing robust 
mentoring programs; addressing the challenges of creating adequate living conditions 
and infrastructure in remote areas. The Committee is of the considered view that 
shortages of faculty and staff, especially in remote AIIMS, certainly impede the delivery 
of quality healthcare and medical education, therefore, addressing these human 
resource challenges is crucial for imbibing professional pursuits in new AIIMS for the 
successful functioning of these institutions. 
(Para 3.6.17) 
 
56. The Committee recommends that the Ministry further strengthen its 
monitoring and coordination mechanisms for PMSSY projects, including both AIIMS 
and GMC upgrades. This should involve enhancing the effectiveness of Project Review 
Committees and utilizing the PRAGATI portal for timely issue resolution; conducting 
regular site visits by senior ministry officials to monitor progress and address 
challenges; improving coordination with state governments and executing agencies to 
ensure seamless implementation. The Committee considers that effective monitoring and 
coordination are essential for ensuring the timely completion of PMSSY projects and the 
efficient utilization of funds. Strengthening these mechanisms will help mitigate delays 
and improve overall project outcomes. 
(Para 3.6.18) 
 
57. The Department presented the status of the PMSSY which aims to 
upgrade Government Medical Colleges/Institutions (GMCIs). Out of 75 approved 

178 
 
GMCIs, 70 have been completed. However, 5 remain either partially completed or have 
not started at all. Specifically, PMCH Patna (91% complete), GMC Surat (73.5% 
complete), and STCIMST Kerala (99.9% complete, scheduled for inauguration) are in 
advanced stages. However, UCMS-GTB Hospital in Delhi and SCBMCH Cuttack, 
Odisha have not commenced up-gradation due to funding issues and location changes, 
respectively. The Committee strongly recommends that the Department of Health and 
Family Welfare, in collaboration with the respective state/UT governments, establish a 
strict, time-bound action plan with clear milestones and regular monitoring to expedite 
the completion of the remaining five GMCIs under the PMSSY. 
(Para 3.6.19) 
 
NATIONAL AIDS AND STD CONTROL PROGRAMME (NACP) 
 
58. While the National AIDS and STD Control Programme (NACP) program has 
allocated funds to the tune of Rs. 2858 crore in RE 2024-25, the actual expenditure in 
2024-25 upto 4th February, 2025 is Rs. 2256.47 crore, just 79% of the revised estimate 
(RE). The reduction is especially remarkable for "Supplies & Materials", indicating 
potential inefficiencies or delays in procurement and distribution. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends conducting a detailed analysis of the "Supplies & Materials" 
expenditure to identify and address bottlenecks in procurement and distribution. The 
Committee, in nutshell, recommends the Department for optimal utilization of funds 
under NACP. 
(Para 3.7.3) 
 
59. The NACP program highlights the importance of integrated health campaigns 
among high-risk and bridge populations for early HIV identification. The Committee 
recommends the Department to increase the frequency and scope of integrated health 
campaigns in select high-risk districts, focusing on early HIV detection and STI/RTI 
management; the strengthening of link worker schemes by providing enhanced training 
and resources, ensuring they can effectively reach and engage with high-risk 
populations; increase in the usage of the SOCH MIS system to better track the 
beneficiaries and services offered to them; and to complete and utilize the data from the 
round 2 of programmatic mapping and population size estimation to refine targeted 
interventions. 
(Para 3.7.7) 
 
60. The Committee highlights that NACP emphasizes index testing campaigns for 
partners and biological children of HIV-positive persons and Lost-to-follow-up tracking 
campaigns are crucial to ensure continuity of treatment. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends the Department for institutionalizing and expanding index testing 
campaigns, providing incentives and support to HIV-positive individuals to encourage 
partner notification. A robust, technology-driven lost-to-follow-up tracking system, 
utilizing mobile technology and community health workers to reconnect individuals with 
treatment services should be implemented to achieve the objective of the Programme.   
(Para 3.7.8) 

179 
 
 
61. The Committee takes into consideration the Intensify and Tailor IEC Campaigns 
and Community Engagement to Reduce Stigma and Discrimination and Increase 
Service Uptake. NACP recognizes the importance of 360-degree multimedia campaigns 
and community outreach with reducing stigma and discrimination as a key objective. 
The 30. Committee, therefore, recommends developing culturally sensitive IEC 
campaigns that address local stigma and misconceptions about HIV/AIDS; expanding 
community engagement through partnerships with local NGOs, community leaders, and 
faith-based organizations; and providing targeted training and sensitization programs 
for key stakeholders, including Self-Help Groups, Anganwadi workers, ASHAs, etc. to 
promote understanding and reduce stigma. 
(Para 3.7.9) 
 
GLOBAL FUND GRANT UNDER COVID-19 RESPONSE MECHANISM 2021 
 
62. Given that the Rs. 355.07 crore has been allocated in BE 2025-26 under the 
Global Fund Covid-19 Response Mechanism is time-limited, expiring in December 2025, 
and aimed at strengthening health systems for future pandemic preparedness and TB 
elimination, the Committee recommends expedited and efficient utilization of these 
funds with a focus on sustainable capacity building and infrastructure development. The 
Committee is of the view that immediate procurement of handheld X-rays for TB 
screening and strengthening of oxygen infrastructure should be prioritised. 
Simultaneously, integrated public health laboratory enhancements and staff training 
should also be accelerated. The Department should also create a plan for how these 
systems will be maintained and funded after the global fund grant expires. 
(Para 3.8.3) 
 
FAMILY WELFARE SCHEMES 
 
63. The Committee has been informed that the main reason for projecting less 
amount under BE 2025-26 over BE 2024-25  is less allocation  of funds under WHO 
supported National Polio Surveillance Project (NPS) due to non  finalization of 
continuation of this component by Expenditure Finance Committee (EFC) at time of 
finalization of BE 2025-26. Now, this programme has now been approved in SFC, the 
additional funds shall be sought at the time of finalization of RE 2025-26. The 
fluctuation of the WHO-NPS project budget due to the EFC process, shows the need for 
better long term financial planning. The Committee is of the view that the Government 
should prefer to implement a long term financial planning system, that reduces the 
impact of delayed approval processes on program funding. 
(Para 3.9.3) 
 
64. The Committee observes that, for the FY 2024-25, BE was Rs. 694.72 
crore which was revised to Rs. 619.37 crore at RE stage and the total amount released 
till 31.01.2025 is Rs.   360.56 crore which is 58.21 % of RE. The budget utilization trends 
reveal discrepancies between approved budgets and actual spending, indicating 

180 
 
potential inefficiencies or shortfall in fund allocation. The Committee recommends the 
Department to strengthen data-driven program management facilitating real-time 
monitoring of key performance indicators, enabling timely identification of program 
gaps and areas requiring intervention. The Department should utilize this data to 
conduct regular, evidence-based reviews of program effectiveness and resource 
allocation, ensuring that funding is aligned with program priorities and achieving 
desired outcomes. Further, the department should create a system that tracks the 
percentage of program goals achieved, versus the percentage of budget spent ensuring 
effective and optimal/rational utilization of funds. 
(Para 3.9.4) 
 
65. The Committee notes that the success of the Family Welfare Scheme depends on 
the synergistic interaction between its various components (SNA, Contraceptive 
Distribution, PNDT Act Awareness, etc.). Further, the general public awareness of the 
programs is vital to the programs’ success. The Committee recommends the Department 
to develop a comprehensive communication strategy that integrates messaging across all 
components of the Family Welfare Scheme. The Department should leverage a variety 
of communication channels, including digital media and community outreach programs 
to reach diverse populations focusing on raising public awareness about the availability 
of services, promoting healthy behaviors, and addressing social and cultural barriers to 
accessing care. The Committee believes that the co-ordination and collaboration among 
various programs should be further enhanced to increase their effectiveness due to co-
location of activities, and caution the Department that the programs do not progress in 
silos. 
(Para 3.9.5) 
 
ESTABLISHMENT AND STRENGTHENING OF NCDC BRANCHES AND HEALTH 
INITIATIVES, INTER SECTORAL CO-ORDINATION FOR PREPARATION AND 
CONTROL OF ZOONOTIC DISEASES AND OTHER NEGLECTED TROPICAL 
DISEASES, SURVEILLANCE OF VIRAL HEPATITIS, ANTI MICROBIAL 
RESISTANCE 
 
66. The Committee recommends a sustained and expanded focus on capacity 
building across all programs, with a particular emphasis on AMR containment and 
zoonotic disease management. Leveraging the existing network of laboratories, training 
sites, and regional coordinators, the program should prioritize consistent, high-quality 
training delivery to strengthen expertise and ensure standardized practices. For AMR 
containment, the Committee recommends the expansion of laboratory networks must be 
accompanied by rigorous, standardized training in bacteriology and HAI surveillance, 
ensuring quality-assured testing and evidence-based treatment. Similarly, for zoonotic 
disease management, the successful approach of multi-stakeholder consultations and 
hands-on training should be scaled up, with a strong focus on developing and 
implementing standardized training modules for medical officers and frontline workers. 
Additionally, the expansion of Regional Venom Centers and the establishment of Poison 
Information Centers for snakebite management should be supported through 

181 
 
comprehensive training programs. The Committee recommends for wider dissemination 
of IEC material for snakebite prevention and control, and protocols on initial 
management of snakebite especially in remote/rural areas. The Committee also 
recommends for launching snakebite Helpline Project and training for medical officers 
on snakebite in all States/UTs. The Committee is of the opinion that a continuous 
investment in human resource development will be the key to ensure long-term success 
and sustainability of these critical public health initiatives. 
(Para 3.10.5) 
 
67. The Committee recommends strengthening surveillance systems and data 
integration to enhance proactive disease management. Building on the AMR 
surveillance network and NOHP-PCZ integration into IHIP, programs should 
implement robust surveillance protocols and data management systems. For AMR, data 
collection and reporting to Global Antimicrobial Resistance and Use Surveillance 
System (GLASS) should be complemented by facility-based antibiograms to support 
evidence-based antibiotic treatment. For zoonotic diseases, implementing the 
Augmented Zoonotic Disease Surveillance (AZDS) proposal is critical for strengthening 
pathogen surveillance in hotspot areas. Additionally, the Rabies Helpline expansion and 
snakebite case notifications should be integrated into a unified national surveillance 
platform for timely data analysis and informed decision-making. The Committee is of 
the view that establishing a Programme Management Unit (PMU) at NCDC will ensure 
coordinated surveillance, data quality, and rapid information dissemination. 
(Para 3.10.6) 
 
Functional Status of NCDC branches in the country 
 
68. The Committee notes that for construction of NCDC branches across the 
country, the land has not yet been identified in the State of Chhattisgarh. Further, the 
construction has been started in eight States only. The Committee recommends that the 
NCDC prioritize and expedite the establishment of fully functional branch 
infrastructure across all states, particularly focusing on Chhattisgarh where land 
identification is still pending and accelerating construction in the remaining states where 
it has not yet commenced. This is crucial for maintaining the continuity and 
effectiveness of the NCDC's national disease surveillance and response capabilities, 
ensuring timely implementation of the Integrated Disease Surveillance Programme 
(IDSP) and the Integrated Health Information Platform (IHIP), and strengthening the 
national public health framework. 
(Para 3.10.8) 
 
INTEGRATED DISEASE SURVEILLANCE PROGRAMME (IDSP)  
 
69. To ensure the sustained effectiveness and responsiveness of the Integrated 
Disease Surveillance Programme (IDSP), the Committee recommends a targeted 
initiative to enhance data collection and analysis to arrive at a decision in specific time-
frame in the Integrated Health Information Platform (IHIP). The Committee believes 

182 
 
that it can be achieved through: (i) intensified training and capacity building for 
healthcare workers at all levels on IHIP data entry & analytics and utilization; (ii) 
streamlined data entry processes and user interface improvements to minimize 
reporting burdens; (iii) regular monitoring and feedback mechanisms to identify and 
rectify reporting challenges; and (iv) leveraging the recently launched community 
reporting tool to supplement and validate data from health facilities, thereby fostering a 
more comprehensive and robust surveillance system. 
(Para 3.10.12) 
 
70. The Committee recommends building on existing collaborations with NACP and 
NFHS, it should implement a standardized framework for data collection and scientific 
analysis of data, integrating seroprevalence data across multiple disease areas to ensure 
continuity and clarity between surveillance of data and programmatic action. This 
expansion will optimize laboratory infrastructure, generate a comprehensive dataset 
essential for informed policymaking, targeted interventions, and effective disease 
control. 
(Para 3.10.14) 
 
NATIONAL PHARMACOVIGILANCE PROGRAMME 
 
71. The Committee has been apprised of the indigenously developed Adverse Drug 
Reaction Monitoring Software (ADRMS) of Pharmacovigilance Program of India 
(PvPI) launched on 19th August 2024. The Committee recommends a strategic focus on 
improving the functionality and accessibility of this indigenously developed ADRM 
software to strengthen the efficacy and safety database for new drugs. The key measures 
in this direction should include implementing rigorous data validation and quality 
control protocols; expanding targeted training programs and developing user-friendly 
interfaces and advanced data analytics tools within ADRMS to enable timely signal 
detection and benefit-risk analysis, reinforcing the program’s leadership in international 
drug monitoring and safeguarding public health. The whole purpose is to create 
effective nation-wide system for patient safety reporting in respect to adverse drug 
reactions (ADRs) of medical products. 
(Para 3.11.4) 
 
DEVELOPMENT OF NURSING SERVICES 
 
72. The Committee believes that, to achieve sustained improvements in nursing 
services and rectify geographical disparities, the Government need to implement a 
comprehensive, data-driven strategy for the targeted expansion and enhancement of 
nursing education and training with a particular focus on underserved regions. The 
Committee urges for integrated implementation of the existing Central Sector Scheme 
for the Development of Nursing Services, the establishment of 157 new nursing colleges 
in co-location with existing medical colleges, and the Continuing Nursing Education 
(CNE) framework. The Committee further recommends that the scheme must 
emphasize the training of nurses in the field of specialization, frequent/periodic in-

183 
 
service training, infrastructure development by way of strengthening of Schools and 
upgrading of Schools of Nursing to Colleges of Nursing. 
(Para 3.12.6) 
 
73. The Committee is of the opinion that Government should actively enforce CNE 
regulations with rigorous monitoring of training outcomes to ensure that all nurses 
maintain their competency through mandatory training. The approval and funding 
processes for state government proposals should be streamlined, expediting 
infrastructure development and training initiatives. These measures will directly 
address the shortage of qualified nursing personnel and enhance healthcare delivery 
across India. Additionally, the Committee give emphasis to the efficient utilization of 
allocated funds and the strategic distribution of resources based on state-wise data on 
nursing institutions and admission capacities. 
(Para 3.12.7) 
 
HEALTH SECTOR DISASTER PREPAREDNESS AND RESPONSE AND HUMAN 
RESOURCE DEVELOPMENT FOR EMERGENCY MEDICAL SERVICES 
 
74.    The Committee noted the under-utilisation of funds under Health Sector Disaster 
Preparedness and Response and Human Resource Development for Emergency Medical 
Services during the past two years. In the FY 2023-24, Rs. 128.14 crore was allocated at 
BE stage which was reduced to Rs. 70 crore at RE stage. Moreover, the actual 
expenditure was just Rs. 30.50 crore (43.57% of the already reduced revised estimate). 
Similarly, in the next FY 2024-25, the amount of Rs. 94 crore allocated at BE stage was 
reduced to Rs. 15 crore at RE stage, and again, the actual expenditure has been reported 
to the tune of Rs. 7.57 crore as on 07.02.2025. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
the Government to implement a rigorous, time-bound monitoring and evaluation 
framework for the "Health Sector Disaster Preparedness and Response and Human 
Resource Development for Emergency Medical Services" scheme, specifically focusing 
on the timely execution of infrastructure projects and Capacity Building Initiatives that 
inter-alia include setting up of National Emergency Life Support (NELS) skill centers in 
medical colleges/institutions under Central/State Government and setting up of 
Chemical, Biological, Radiological and Nuclear (CBRN) medical management centers 
besides imparting skills training of doctors, nurses and paramedics on NELS and 
CBRN, and fund utilization by grantee institutions. 
(Para 3.13.6) 
 
75.     For efficient and optimal utilization of funds under the scheme, the Committee 
believes that the Government should consider the following: (i) pre-identified and vetted 
site alternatives to mitigate delays due to site changes; (ii) mandatory quarterly fund 
utilization reports from grantee medical colleges for NELS Skill Centers, with clear 
timelines for disbursement and penalties for non-compliance; and (iii) a proactive, 
needs-based assessment system for disaster and public health emergency response 
logistics, rather than solely relying on state/UT requests, to ensure allocated funds are 

184 
 
strategically utilized. This systematic approach will address the recurrent issues of 
delayed project execution and underutilization of funds. 
(Para 3.13.7) 
 
NATIONAL ORGAN TRANSPLANT PROGRAM (NOTP) 
76. The Committee notes that the funds utilization trend under the National Organ 
Transplant Program (NOTP) show significant discrepancies between budgeted and 
actual expenditures, indicating poor fund utilization. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends the Government to establish a robust mechanism incorporating 
accountability and ensuring enhanced and effective fund utilization by ROTTOs and 
SOTTOs. The Department needs to address the root causes of underutilization, 
including administrative bottlenecks and lack of state-level capacity and overcome the 
reasons for under-utilization and also persuading the non-performing States for optimal 
utilization of allocated fund.  
(Para 3.14.5) 
 
77. The Committee, on examination of the submissions made by the Department of 
Health and Family Welfare, observes that many ROTTOs and SOTTOs are in their 
infancy stage, lacking infrastructure and operational efficiency. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends the strengthening of ROTTOs and SOTTOs by provide 
comprehensive capacity-building programs for ROTTOs and SOTTOs, focusing on 
website development, data management, budget utilization, and training program 
organization. 
(Para 3.14.6) 
NATIONAL TELE MENTAL HEALTH PROGRAMME 
 
78. The Committee notes significant under-utilisation of fund under the National 
Tele Mental Health Programme. In both FYs 2023-24 and 2024-25, the allocation of 
funds at RE stage was significantly reduced as compared to the allocation at BE stage. 
The Committee believes in immediate and targeted interventions to resolve bottlenecks 
in fund utilization to ensure the sustained growth and effectiveness of Tele MANAS. 
Further, the recruitment processes for human resources at the State and Union 
Territory levels must be expedited through streamlined procedures and proactive 
central government support.  
(Para 3.15.3) 
 
79. The Committee urges upon the Department for an intensified focus on targeted 
outreach and awareness campaigns to build on the increasing engagement of female 
callers and extend services to vulnerable populations. These efforts should leverage the 
Tele MANAS mobile application and diverse media platforms to address specific 
barriers preventing access to services. Furthermore, the nationwide expansion of video 
consultation services, currently available in select states, should be prioritized to 
enhance accessibility, particularly for individuals in remote and underserved areas. This 
expansion must be supported by comprehensive training and technical assistance to 

185 
 
ensure effective implementation and utilization of the video consultation platform. 
 
(Para 3.15.4) 
 
NATIONAL DIGITAL HEALTH MISSION (NDHM)/ AYUSHMAN BHARAT 
DIGITAL MISSION (ABDM) 
 
80. The Committee, on examination of the Demands for Grants (2025-26), observes 
with concern persistent and declining trend of utilisation of funds allocated under the 
ABDM. In the FY 2023-24, in BE an amount of Rs. 341.02 crore was allocated to the 
scheme which was reduced to Rs. 200 crore at RE stage and at the end of the FY, the 
actual expenditure came to Rs. 175.36 crore (just 51.42% of BE allocated initially). In 
FY 2024-25, the BE allocation was reduced to Rs. 200 crore (which is a reduction by 
41.35% from the BE 2023-24), however, at RE stage the allocation was increased to Rs. 
225 crore, but the actual expenditure up to 31.01.2025 comes to Rs. 112.97 crore only 
(which is just 50.21% of the RE allocation).  This pattern of under-expenditure, coupled 
with fluctuating budget allocations, directly hampers the scheme's ability to achieve its 
targeted objectives within the stipulated timeframe, potentially leading to time delays 
and cost overruns. To rectify this situation, the Committee recommends immediate and 
decisive action to address the root causes of these inefficiencies and streamline the fund 
utilization for successful implementation of Mission. In the FY 2025-26, the scheme has 
been allocated an enhanced amount of Rs. 340.11 crore at BE stage. The Committee, 
therefore, urges upon the Department to address the issues with strategic planning and 
project execution to ensure proper utilization of the funds so that intended targets are 
achieved within stipulated time-frame.  
(Para 3.16.5) 
 
81. Given the recurring issues of underutilization of funds within the Ayushman 
Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM), specifically due to delays in state-level Project 
Monitoring Unit (PMU) establishment, inefficient management of advertising contracts, 
performance issues with Managed Service Providers (MSPs), and complications in 
professional service contracts, the Committee recommends a robust fund disbursement 
and monitoring mechanism should be instituted. This should include strict timelines and 
performance-linked incentives for States/UTs to expedite the establishment of Project 
Monitoring Units (PMUs), along with real-time tracking of fund allocation and 
utilization. Furthermore, ensuring timely on-boarding of competent service providers 
and enforcing clear contractual obligations with penalties for non-performance will help 
streamline expenditure under Digital Equipment and Professional Services. 
Strengthening coordination between NHA, States/UTs, and implementing agencies will 
be critical for optimizing fund flow and ensuring effective implementation of ABDM 
objectives. 
(Para 3.16.7) 
 
82. The Committee recommends to fully leveraging the potential of AI and ML in 
healthcare by accelerating the integration and scaling of AI-driven tools within the 

186 
 
Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM). Specifically, the successful collaborations, 
such as the MoU with IIT Kanpur and the AI-based Clinical Decision Support System 
(CDSS) in e-Sanjeevani, should be rapidly replicated. This includes prioritizing the 
development and deployment of unified learning platforms, quality-preserving 
databases, and open benchmarking systems, ensuring that these technologies are 
accessible and beneficial across all healthcare facilities, especially in rural and 
underserved areas. This will enhance diagnostic accuracy, streamline patient care, and 
improve overall health outcomes by making data-driven decisions more efficient. 
(Para 3.16.10) 
 
83. The Committee believes that the ABDM should effectively reach rural and tribal 
populations for gains in real terms. The Committee, in this connection, emphasizes the 
need for enhanced digital literacy and connectivity initiatives alongside the mission's 
technical advancements. The NHA should intensify efforts to implement and promote 
the assisted mode for ABHA creation in areas with poor internet connectivity and 
limited digital literacy. Additionally, targeted awareness campaigns and training 
programs should be conducted in local languages to educate healthcare providers and 
citizens about the benefits and usage of ABDM services like Scan and Share and Scan 
and Pay. Collaborations with local community leaders and NGOs can further facilitate 
the adoption of ABDM, ensuring that even the most remote populations have equitable 
access to digital health services while maintaining robust privacy and security measures. 
(Para 3.16.11) 
 
STATUTORY AND REGULATORY BODIES 
 
84. The Committee notes that the reduction in budgetary allocation for the Food 
Safety and Standards Authority of India (FSSAI) in the RE 2024-25 was primarily due 
to delayed submission of Utilization Certificates (UCs) from States/UTs. This delay 
hinders the timely disbursement of funds for strengthening the food safety ecosystem. 
The committee, therefore, recommends the Department for implementing a streamlined 
UC submission and tracking system. To ensure consistent and timely fund utilization, 
the FSSAI should establish a mandatory, digitally enabled system for States/UTs to 
submit and track their UCs. This system should include automated reminders and alerts 
for pending submissions, and provide real-time visibility of UC status to both FSSAI and 
the respective States/UTs. Furthermore, the FSSAI should conduct periodic workshops 
and training sessions for State/UT officials to improve their understanding of UC 
requirements and submission processes, thereby preventing future delays and ensuring 
efficient fund utilization. This proactive approach will facilitate smoother fund flow and 
enable FSSAI to effectively carry out its mandate of ensuring food safety across the 
country. 
(Para 3.17.6) 
 
85. Given the importance of FSSAI's activities, particularly in supporting 
States/UTs, the Committee is of the opinion that a phased funding release tied to 
performance and capacity building should be implemented. Instead of a single annual 

187 
 
allocation, funds could be released in tranches, contingent upon the timely submission of 
UCs and demonstrable progress in enhancing food safety infrastructure and 
surveillance. In addition, a portion of the funds should be dedicated to capacity-building 
initiatives, such as training programs for food safety officers, procurement of essential 
equipment, and the establishment of robust laboratory facilities at the state and local 
levels. This approach will incentivize States/UTs to prioritize food safety initiatives, 
improve their administrative efficiency, and build sustainable food safety systems. 
                (Para 3.17.7) 
 
86. The Committee notes that FSSAI is actively deploying Mobile Food Testing 
Laboratories (MFTLs) to enhance on-the-spot food testing and awareness, and while 
progress is made in this connection, achieving the target of one MFTL/FSW per district 
by 2026 is contingent on state readiness. The Committee recommends FSSAI to 
implement a structured program to actively assist states in achieving 
readiness/willingness to deploy MFTLs/FSWs. The Committee feels that through 
dedicated State liaisons, FSSAI personnel should work directly with state governments 
to identify and address specific barriers to FSW deployment. The Committee desires 
that targeted training programs and expedited funding mechanisms should be 
established showing proactive approach to prevent delays and ensure consistent food 
safety monitoring across all districts. 
(Para 3.17.13) 
 
87. FSSAI faces a significant number of vacant positions, which could impact its 
operational efficiency. The Committee believes that to improve the operational 
efficiency of FSSAI and enhance the consistency of food safety regulation across the 
country, FSSAI should be directed to prioritize and expedite the direct recruitment 
process to fill up the significant number of vacant positions. Additionally, FSSAI should 
implement strategies to improve employee retention and recruitment on regular basis 
must be adhered to instead of hiring staff on contract basis as contractual staff lacks 
sense of organizational belongingness and commitment to work and there remain sense 
of being on ad-hoc at work that too without responsibility and accountability. The 
Committee, therefore, believes that efforts be made to grant regularization of 
contractual staff in FSSAI.  
(Para 3.17.14) 
 
88. Furthermore, the Committee notes that not all States have separate food safety 
departments, which could lead to inconsistencies in implementation, however, most of 
the States have Food Safety Appellate Tribunals.  The Committee, therefore, holds that 
separate Food Safety Departments should be promoted. The Committee strongly 
emphasizes the Department to encourage the remaining states/UTs to establish separate 
Food Safety Departments. Providing guidance and examples of the benefits of separate 
departments, as well as possible assistance during the transition, will help to increase the 
number of states with separate departments. 
(Para 3.17.15) 
 

188 
 
ALL INDIA INSTITUTE OF MEDICAL SCIENCES (AIIMS), NEW DELHI 
   
89. The Committee notes with concern that six construction projects are ongoing in 
the AIIMS, New Delhi and one construction project related to expansion of NDDTC, 
Ghaziabad AIIMS is proposed to be undertaken whose detailed estimate is in progress. 
The Committee wishes to highlight that the implementation status of the ongoing six 
projects is not satisfactory, which can be corroborated by the fact that out of the six 
ongoing projects, one is in tendering process, one is pending due to tree cutting and 
environmental clearance, one has attained just 2.50% of physical progress and the other 
one has attained the 28.6% of physical progress.   
(Para 3.18.3) 
 
90. The Committee's assessment reveals a concerning trend in AIIMS, New Delhi's 
utilization of budgetary allocation. While the utilization of budgetary funds was 
satisfactory in FY 2023-24, a significant shortfall occurred in FY 2024-25, with only 
81.96% of the revised estimate being spent till the end of January 2025. This 
underutilization is further underscored by the slow progress of ongoing construction 
projects. The key issues include delayed Project implementation, weak project planning 
and execution, delay in tendering process and environmental and regulatory hurdles.  
(Para 3.18.4) 
 
91. The Committee recommends that AIIMS, New Delhi, establish a robust project 
monitoring mechanism with clearly defined milestones and regular progress reviews. 
This mechanism should include a dedicated team to proactively address regulatory 
hurdles, such as environmental clearances and tree-cutting approvals, by engaging with 
relevant authorities at the earliest stages of project planning. Furthermore, AIIMS 
should streamline its tendering process to minimize/eliminate delays, potentially through
