pre-qualifying vendors and implementing a more efficient evaluation system to ensure 
that projects like the 150 bedded Critical Care block, and the super speciality OPD 
block are not delayed due to avoidable administrative issues. 
(Para 3.18.5) 
 
92. The Committee strongly recommends a comprehensive review of the residential 
redevelopment project, given the project's extremely slow progress (2.50% physical, 
3.68% financial). This review should assess the project's feasibility, identify the root 
causes of delays, and explore potential restructuring options, including phased 
implementation or alternative construction methodologies. The Department should get a 
detailed report outlining the findings of this review and the proposed corrective actions. 
The Department should also examine the large amount of fund that has been allocated 
to this project, and its efficient utilization. 
(Para 3.18.6) 
  
93. The Committee notes the AIIMS, New Delhi's efforts to address patient 
overcrowding and improve services which include providing temporary shelter 
("Ashray"), enhancing patient convenience through various facilities, streamlining 

189 
 
appointments via online systems, expanding infrastructure with new specialty blocks, 
and offering accommodation through "Vishram Sadans." Additionally, the Committee 
desires the AIIMS, Delhi to expand the "Ashray" initiative by providing more 
permanent, weather-protected waiting areas with adequate seating, clean washrooms, 
and access to affordable food options. This will significantly reduce the hardship faced 
by patients and their attendants awaiting appointments. However, significant challenges 
remain, particularly with long wait times for walk-in OPD appointments and substantial 
staff vacancies across all cadres. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Institute implements a robust, real-time digital queue management system for walk-in 
OPD appointments. This system should provide patients with estimated wait times via 
SMS or a mobile app, allowing them to wait in designated, comfortable areas rather 
than overnight outside. Keeping in view the fact of long waiting time for urgent 
treatment of even critical patients, the Committee desires that Department should 
explore the possibilities of construction of another AIIMS in NCR region close to New 
Delhi.  
(Para 3.18.9) 
 
94. The Committee acknowledges that the ambitious Master Plan for AIIMS, aimed 
at transforming it into a world-class medical university, is progressing but faces delays 
due to land acquisition and budgetary approvals. Simultaneously, the Department must 
expedite the approval and allocation of the necessary budgetary resources for the 
AIIMS Master Plan. To ensure timely execution, a dedicated project management team 
should be established to oversee the phased implementation of the plan, with regular 
progress reviews and reporting to the Committee. This will ensure that the 
infrastructure upgrades are completed without further delay, and that AIIMS can reach 
its goal to become a world class medical university. 
(Para 3.18.10) 
  
95. The Committee observes that there is significant shortage of staff in the AIIMS in 
various cadres. The faculty posts have 430 vacancies (34.81% of the sanctioned 
strength), the non-faculty posts have 2109 vacancies (16.29% of the sanctioned strength), 
the Sr. Resident (Non-Academic) posts have 434 vacancies (37.48% of the sanctioned 
strength) and Jr. Resident (Non-Academic) have 113 vacancies (46.89% of the 
sanctioned strength). In addition to the above, the Department has submitted that there 
is 12213 outsourced non-faculty staff in the Institute. The Committee urges upon the 
Ministry to prioritize the immediate filling up of all vacant faculty and non-faculty 
positions. AIIMS should implement a streamlined recruitment process, including 
targeted outreach and competitive compensation packages, to attract qualified 
candidates. 
(Para 3.18.12)

infrastructure and facilities in the Institute in an efficient manner leading to solutions of 
many challenges faced by the Institute.

192 
 
budgetary fluctuations occurred in FY 2024-25 due to the Model Code of Conduct 
(MCC) imposed during election which highlights potential planning issues. The

during election is only for a certain period in the whole duration of a fiscal year. The 
Institute must have been aware of the imposition of MCC in advance and should plan 
the execution of projects and utilization of funds accordingly. The Committee believes 
that the Department should promote enhanced Budgetary Planning and Mitigation 
Strategies among the Institutions under its purview. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that NIMHANS implement a more robust budgetary planning process that 
accounts for foreseeable disruptions, such as the Model Code of Conduct. Specifically, 
NIMHANS should develop contingency plans for procurement and project execution to 
minimize the impact of such events. This includes creating a detailed timeline for major 
projects, prioritizing essential procurements, and establishing clear communication 
channels with relevant authorities to anticipate and address potential delays. By 
proactive planning, NIMHANS can ensure consistent and efficient utilization of 
allocated funds, maintaining the continuity of vital projects and services. 
(Para 3.21.3) 
 
103. The Committee acknowledges that NIMHANS, Bengaluru is a premier institute 
involved in research and community based mental health initiatives, and achieved 
significant recognition and maintained high rankings, including the Nelson Mandela

patient care, research, and training, addressing various mental health needs. The 
Committee, however, expresses concern that there are large number of vacancies across 
various positions, indicating potential staffing challenges. The Committee urges the 
Department to develop and execute a strategic recruitment and retention plan to 
address the significant number of vacant positions, particularly among professors, 
assistant professors, and other key personnel in the institute. This plan should include 
targeted recruitment campaigns on regular basis and not on contractual basis, 
competitive compensation packages, and professional development opportunities to 
attract and retain qualified staff. Addressing these staffing gaps is essential to ensure the 
institute can maintain its high standards of service delivery, research output, and 
training programs, and continue to serve the mental health needs of the nation 
effectively. 
(Para 3.21.7)

must adopt a strict project management framework to mitigate delays in ongoing 
infrastructure projects. Given that all five projects have experienced time overruns,

should be enforced to yield desirable result. The work agency should be directed to 
mobilize additional resources, ensure close coordination with local authorities to address 

194

expenses. The Committee, therefore, recommends an immediate and thorough review of 
the Institute's budget allocation for FY 2025-26. Given the institute's documented 
essential financial requirements, including salaries, pensions, and critical operational 
expenses, the proposed allocation of Rs. 65 crore is deemed insufficient as stated in 
submission to the Committee. The Committee, therefore, strongly advises the Ministry 
of Finance to enhance the budget allocation to adequately meet the institute's financial 
requirements. All such financial estimation and enhancement should be based on a 
detailed analysis of the institute's expenditure patterns and projected operational costs, 
ensuring uninterrupted service delivery and compliance with financial obligations. This 
action is critical to prevent operational disruptions and maintain the institute's ability to 
provide essential healthcare services. 
(Para 3.25.4)

particularly in Group A category. Moreover, there exist substantial vacancies of the post

proposals for pay scale upgrades and the creation/revival of essential posts. Addressing 
the critical vacancies, particularly in Group A positions and Senior Resident roles, is

initiated to fill up the senior resident positions as quickly as possible. This combined 

196 
 
approach of improved pay and targeted recruitment will help to fill up the critical 
staffing gaps, thereby enhancing the institute's capacity to deliver quality patient care.

National Health Mission 
 
Budgetary Allocation 
 
114. The Committee notes that the National Health Mission (NHM) aims to achieve 
universal access to equitable, affordable, and quality healthcare, aligning with 
Sustainable Development Goal 3. The NHM has two sub-missions, NRHM and NUHM, 
and its objectives include reducing child and maternal mortality, controlling diseases, 
ensuring access to primary healthcare, and promoting healthy lifestyles. The program 
has shown progress in several areas, such as achieving and sustaining Total Fertility 
Rate (TFR) at 2.0 and exceeding targets for Ayushman Arogya Mandirs and full 
immunization coverage. However, challenges remain in reducing Maternal Mortality 
Rate (MMR) and Infant Mortality Rate (IMR) to target levels, and achieving 90% of 
districts meeting TB case notification and treatment success targets. The Committee 
expresses concern on the fact that the MMR and IMR are still above the targeted levels 
and, therefore, recommends the Department for a targeted and data-driven approach 
with focused intervention for maternal and infant mortality reduction. This should 
involve detailed micro-level analysis of the regions with the highest MMR and IMR, 
focusing on the specific factors contributing to these rates and chalking out sustained & 
resilient strategy to combat those factors so as to achieve TB elimination, MMR and 
IMR to target level within specified period. 
(Para 4.2.4) 
 
115. The Committee recommends the Department that NHM should prioritize 
increased funding and resources for these high-risk areas. Interventions should include 
strengthening facility-based newborn care, improving access to skilled birth attendants, 
enhancing prenatal and postnatal care, and addressing nutritional deficiencies in women 
and children. A feedback loop must be put in place to measure the effectiveness of the 
interventions and allow for quick adjustments. 
(Para 4.2.5) 
 
116. The Committee notes that budgetary allocations to NHM have generally seen 
increase in funds, but expenditure in FY 2024-25, as of February 7, 2025, stands out to 
be only 81.28% of the allocated amount. The operational period of NHM is five years i.e. 
upto FY 2026, with a total outlay of Rs. 1,65,356.63 crore from FY 2021 to FY 2026. The 
budget for FY 2025-26 is proposed to be Rs. 37,226.92 crore, a 3.4% increase from FY 
2024-25. Recognizing the under-utilization of allocated funds in FY 2024-25, the 
Committee strongly recommends implementing a more robust monitoring and 
evaluation system to track expenditure and program implementation. This system 
should provide real-time data on fund utilization and identify bottlenecks in the 
disbursement and expenditure processes. The NHM should streamline administrative 

197 
 
procedures, provide capacity building for state and district-level health officials in 
financial management, and ensure timely release of funds. The committee further 
recommends that the NHM provides reports quarterly regarding the budget utilization 
and provides the reasoning for any deficit in spending, along with plans to correct the 
deficit. This will allow for more efficient program implementation and ensure that 
allocated resources are effectively used to achieve the NHM's objectives. 
(Para 4.2.6) 
 
117. The Committee having micro-analysis of the identified challenges (infrastructure 
gaps, human resource shortages, drug and diagnostic deficiencies, service delivery 
issues, urban-rural divide, and fragmented digital portals) and the government's current 
strategies (NQAS, PM-ABHIM, HR incentives, free drug and diagnostic initiatives, tele-
consultation, and ABDM), strongly recommends the urgent and prioritized 
establishment of a unified, real-time, and interoperable national health data platform. 
This platform should integrate all existing health portals, including HMIS, NFHS, 
ABDM, and others, to provide a comprehensive, longitudinal view of health 
infrastructure, human resources, drug and diagnostic availability, service delivery 
metrics, and patient outcomes across all levels (rural and urban). This unified system 
will enable data-driven decision-making, facilitate efficient resource allocation, 
streamline service delivery, enhance monitoring of program implementation, and 
ultimately bridge the urban-rural divide by providing equitable access to healthcare 
information and services. The Committee believes that the implementation of these steps 
would maximize the impact of ongoing programs and enhance the credibility of public 
hospitals. 
(Para 4.2.9) 
 
Reproductive, Maternal, Newborn, Child, Adolescent Health and Nutrition (RMNCAH 
+ N)  
118. The Committee acknowledges the initiatives taken by the Government to 
improve maternal healthcare like PMSMA and extended PMSMA for antenatal care, 
showing a significant increase in beneficiaries, and Optimizing Postnatal Care with 
ASHA incentivisation, showing stable post-partum check-up numbers. The Committee 
recommends strengthening the Optimizing Postnatal Care program by focusing on 
targeted training for ASHAs in the early detection of danger signs in postpartum 
mothers. While postpartum check-up numbers are stable, the Committee suggests 
implementing a digital tracking system to monitor the individual progress of high-risk 
postpartum mothers, similar to the extended PMSMA's approach for high-risk 
pregnancies. This system should integrate with existing digital health platforms to 
ensure seamless data flow and timely intervention. The Committee also recommends the 
Department to conduct specific audits of the post-partum care, to assess the quality of 
the care that is given. This will ensure that the ASHAs are correctly identifying the 
danger signs, and that the mothers are getting the needed care. 
(Para 4.2.11) 
 

198 
 
119. The Committee takes into account the initiatives taken by the Department to 
improve child survival like Facility-Based Newborn Care (FBNC) through 
NICUs/SNCUs/NBSUs and new MNCUs, with increasing newborn treatment numbers; 
Home-Based Newborn Care (HBNC) and Home-Based Care of Young Children (HBYC) 
by ASHAs, with fluctuating but generally high coverage; Diarrhea campaigns (ORS and 
Zinc), showing consistent coverage; and Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakram (RBSK) 
for screening and treatment, demonstrating significant growth in children screened and 
treated. The Committee recommends the Department to make public awareness 
campaign about these programmes in rural, semi-urban and tribal & hilly areas. The 
Committee desires that the Department should assess the success rate of these 
programmes in underserved areas and after taking stock of ground realities launch 
special drive to achieve the cherished objectives in stated areas.    
(Para 4.2.12) 
 
120. The Committee recommends prioritizing the strengthening of District Early 
Intervention Centres (DEICs) to match the significant increase in children screened and 
treated under the Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakram (RBSK). While screening 
numbers are rising, ensuring timely and effective treatment at DEICs is crucial. The 
Committee suggests a comprehensive review of DEIC functionality, including staffing, 
equipment, and referral systems. Based on the review, the Department should provide 
targeted support to DEICs, including increased funding, training for personnel, and 
streamlined referral pathways. This will ensure that children identified through RBSK 
receive the necessary interventions promptly, maximizing the program's impact on child 
health outcomes. Furthermore, the committee suggests that the Department create a 
system of feedback from the families that have used the DEIC services, so that the 
DEIC's can be improved based on the users feedback. 
(Para 4.2.13) 
 
STRENGTHENING OF STATE DRUG REGULATORY SYSTEM 
 
121. The Committee recommends the urgent implementation of a proactive, 
structured engagement strategy with all States and Union Territories well in advance of 
each fiscal year's budget cycle to enhance the demand for the Strengthening of State 
Drug Regulatory System (SSDRS) scheme. This strategy should focus on providing clear 
guidelines, technical assistance, and regular communication to ensure that States 
understand the SSDRS scheme's requirements, and to assist States in developing 
comprehensive, eligible proposals. This process should also involve regular monitoring 
of fund utilization by states, providing early feedback and support to address any 
bottlenecks hindering the 75% utilization requirement. Given the scheme's demand-
driven nature and the observed pattern of reduced allocations due to insufficient or non-
compliant state proposals, actively facilitating state participation will mitigate the risk of 
underutilization and ensure the crucial strengthening of drug regulatory systems, 
particularly as the scheme's extension is being considered. Specifically, the department 
should establish a dedicated team to conduct workshops, provide templates for 

199 
 
proposals, and track state progress, thereby ensuring that the allocated funds are 
effectively utilized to improve drug quality and accessibility across the nation. 
(Para 4.3.3) 
 
122. To ensure a consistently high standard of drug regulation across India, mirroring 
the success achieved in vaccine regulation, the Committee recommends the immediate 
implementation of a comprehensive, nationwide, and phased "Medicines Regulatory 
Maturity Enhancement Program." This program should be modelled after the WHO 
NRA re-benchmarking process used for vaccines, involving a detailed scientific review 
of state and union territory drug regulatory systems against the WHO Global 
Benchmarking Tool. It should include: i) mandatory, challenging-mode state audits 
conducted by a central team of international and national experts, focusing on the 
quality, safety, and efficacy of medicines, APIs, and formulations; ii) targeted regulatory 
capacity building and infrastructure upgrades in states/UTs, informed by the audit 
findings and stakeholder consultations; iii) the establishment of clear, time-bound 
milestones for achieving and maintaining higher maturity levels, with progressive 
implementation based on the turnover of the manufacturers as done with GMP 
revisions; iv) continuous professional development and training for state drug 
regulatory officials, leveraging existing CDSCO training programs and expanding to 
incorporate international best practices; and v) the integration of the Online National 
Drug Licensing System (ONDLS) with the benchmarking process to track and monitor 
progress, thereby ensuring uniformity and efficiency in drug regulation across the 
country. This systematic approach, coupled with stringent enforcement measures like 
risk-based inspections and amended Drugs Rules, will elevate the entire drug regulatory 
framework to international standards, safeguarding public health and reinforcing 
India's position as a global pharmaceutical leader. 
(Para 4.3.5) 
 
123. The Committee is of the view that India being pharmaceutical hub of the world, 
the same level of benchmarking/ capacity building of States/UTs in the field of 
medicines/drugs is the need of the hour. This requires regulatory capacity building up-
gradation of the States in medicines/drugs/API /formulations. The Committee welcomes 
the launching of Online National Drug Licensing System (ONDLS) portal for 
submission of applications and issuance of licenses by the States through this portal. 
(Para 4.3.6) 
 
FUNCTIONING OF CENTRAL DRUGS STANDARD CONTROL ORGANIZATION 
(CDSCO) 
 
124. The Committee is having the concerns that centralization of authority at any 
level may result in creating a situation for arbitrary/ discretionary decision making. 
This may have an adverse impression that India is keen on over regulation since last two 
years to create a business of licensing rather than making it a manufacturing 
powerhouse for medical devices. The Department should ensure faster documentation 
processes including reduction of human interface to facilitate the growth of medical 

200 
 
devices industry in the country. The Committee is of the view that the Department 
should promote start-ups in medical devices by streamlining and overhauling the 
licensing system and preventing the deliberate delays by following a transparent process 
with minimum room for discretion/human inference. The Committee believes that these 
steps would result into India becoming a hub of the medical devices’ manufacturing and 
alone generating an over one trillion dollar industry in medical devices.  
(Para 4.4.8) 
 
125. The Committee, on examination of the submissions made by the Department, 
expresses concerns that the CDSCO's current licensing process is plagued by delays, 
inconsistent timelines, and a lack of transparency, hindering the growth of the medical 
device industry. The Committee expresses serious concern that due to rigid process and 
inordinate delays by CDSCO, a large number of manufacturing units have gone to 
Vietnam and Malaysia.  Rather, several medical devices manufacturers are scared to set 
up a unit in India due to delaying tactics of the CDSCO.  The Committee recommends 
the implementation of a fully digitized, time-bound, and trackable licensing system. This 
system must include: (a) clearly defined timelines for each stage of the licensing process, 
with automatic notifications to applicants; (b) a standardized, publicly accessible 
database detailing all applications, queries, and approvals, allowing for real-time 
tracking; (c) the utilization of AI and automated systems to pre-screen applications for 
completeness, reducing the need for repeated queries and human intervention, especially 
for globally certified, non-invasive products; (d) a system that records all queries and 
the reason for the query along with the date of the query and the date of the reply. The 
Committee is of the belief that this will allow for easy tracking of the process, and will 
allow for analysis of where the process is being slowed. This will help to identify 
bottlenecks. This will promote transparency, reduce discretionary decision-making, and 
foster a more efficient and predictable regulatory environment. 
(Para 4.4.10) 
 
126. The Committee further observes that the frequent and delayed queries raised by 
CDSCO are a major obstacle for applicants, particularly startups and entrepreneurs. 
The Committee recommends a significant overhaul of the query process. This includes: 
(a) establishing a "single query" policy, where all necessary queries are consolidated 
and raised at once, rather than in multiple rounds; (b) implementing a system of 
conditional approvals within 45 days, based on self-declaration and submission of 
necessary documentation, especially for products with existing international 
certifications (e.g., CE, US FDA); (c) providing detailed, standardized guidelines and 
FAQs to minimize ambiguity and reduce the need for queries; (d) creating a dedicated 
support team to assist applicants with the query process and provide timely 
clarifications. The Committee believes that this approach will expedite the approval 
process, encourage innovation, and allow businesses to adapt to the rapidly evolving 
technology landscape. 
(Para 4.4.11) 
 

201 
 
127. The Committee acknowledges that the CDSCO faces challenges related to 
capacity and expertise, contributing to delays and inconsistencies in the licensing 
process. The Committee opines the acceleration of the recruitment of qualified 
personnel, particularly Drugs Inspectors and other key positions in the Medical Device 
Vertical; and exploring the lateral entry of professionals from the medical device 
industry to bring in specialized expertise and a more industry-friendly perspective. The 
Committee urges upon the Department to implement a system of regular, mandatory 
training programs for CDSCO officials to enhance their understanding of the latest 
technologies, regulatory best practices, and their role as facilitators of industry growth; 
and implement a system of rotating officials to prevent the development of vested 
interests and ensure a fresh perspective. The Committee believes that these measures 
will strengthen CDSCO's capabilities, improve efficiency, and foster a more 
collaborative relationship with the industry. 
(Para 4.4.12) 
 
128. The Committee would like to express that the perception of CDSCO as a "license 
raj" highlights a critical need for improved communication and collaboration with the 
medical device industry. The Committee, therefore, recommends the establishment of an 
independent Industry Advisory Board, comprising representatives from manufacturers 
(including startups), importers, healthcare providers, and experts. This board would 
provide regular feedback to CDSCO on regulatory challenges and potential 
improvements; participate in the development of new guidelines and policies; and serve 
as a platform for resolving disputes and addressing industry concerns. Furthermore, 
CDSCO should implement a system of regular stakeholder consultations, including open 
forums, workshops, and online platforms, to gather feedback and address concerns 
proactively. This will foster a more transparent and collaborative regulatory 
environment, build trust between CDSCO and the industry, and ensure that regulations 
are aligned with the needs of a rapidly evolving sector, moving CDSCO from a 
perceived roadblock to a true facilitator. This will also create a clear channel for 
industry concerns to be heard and addressed, and allow for a more dynamic and 
responsive regulatory approach. 
(Para 4.4.13) 
 
TERTIARY CARE PROGRAMS (TCPs)  
 
129. In the FY 2023-24, the BE amounting to Rs. 289.62 crore was slightly reduced to 
Rs. 276.39 crore at RE stage, however, the actual expenditure remained Rs. 258.83 
crore. The Committee observes with concern over low trend of underutilization of funds, 
even during the FY 2024-25, out of the allocation of Rs. 322.90 crore at RE stage, only 
Rs. 170.08 crore till 31.1.2025 have been utilised which translates into just 52.67% 
utilisation of fund. The Committee wishes to highlight the fund utilization within the 
National Mental Health Programme (12.46% of RE 2024-25 till 31.01.2025), National 
Programme for Prevention and Management of Trauma & Burn Injury (35.05% of RE 
2024-25 till 31.01.2025), National Programme for prevention & Control of Non-
Communicable Diseases (45.60% of RE 2024-25 till 31.01.2025) and Health Care for the 

202 
 
Elderly (1.61% of RE 2024-25 till 31.01.2025) where the percentage utilization is below 
50%. The Committee also acknowledges that the National Tobacco Control Programme 
& National Drugs De-addiction Programme have achieved 100% of the RE which shows 
that this program is working well. 
(Para 4.5.4) 
 
130. The Committee is of the opinion that the BE for 2025-26, amounting to Rs. 483.54 
crore, shows a substantial increase, indicating a commitment to these programs, 
however, this increased allocation will be wasted if the fund utilization issues are not 
addressed. The Committee recommends a detailed, program-specific review of the 
factors contributing to the significant underutilization of funds. This review should 
include analyzing the specific bottlenecks in fund disbursement and program 
implementation at the state level; conducting on-site assessments of program activities 
and infrastructure; implementing a real-time monitoring system with clear performance 
indicators to track fund utilization and program progress; providing targeted technical 
assistance and capacity-building support to states with low utilization rates; and 
specifically for the NMHP, the Department should investigate why the money allocated 
for the Centers of Excellence, and PG programs are not being used. This proactive 
approach will ensure that the increased budget allocation for 2025-26 translates into 
tangible improvements in service delivery. 
(Para 4.5.5) 
 
131. Given the importance of Tertiary care programs the Committee is of view that 
there has been considerable rise in mental health issues in the country. The Committee, 
accordingly, recommends the Department to take effective measures to increase the 
outreach of mental healthcare services and integrate psychological support services into 
primary health care. Further, considering the prevalence of non communicable diseases, 
the Committee feels that concerted efforts must be taken to tackle lifestyle diseases. The 
budgetary allocation under NPCDCS must be increased considerably, the Department, 
however, must improve the absorption capacity under various Schemes. The Committee 
in its 139th Report had assessed the status of NPCDCS Scheme and noted that the 
screening of common Cancer under NPCDCS program must be done nationwide as 
seriously as it should be to prevent the killer disease and unstable the fiscal foundation 
of Household management. The Committee had recommended that cancer must be dealt 
separately, with specific programmes and intended budgetary allocation and enhanced 
and forensic inputs of early detection and prevention of cancer. and must not be 
grouped under other lifestyle diseases. The Committee reiterates its recommendation 
that the Department must devise a targeted plan for cancer control. 
(Para 4.5.7) 
 
HUMAN RESOURCE FOR HEALTH AND MEDICAL EDUCATION 
 
132. The Committee observes with concern that in the FY 2023-24, the BE allocation 
amounting to Rs. 6500 crore was significantly reduced to Rs. 1519.87 crore at RE stage 
reflecting into huge decline by 76.62%. The Committee wishes to express further 

203 
 
concern that even this reduced amount at RE stage could not be utilized fully (Actual 
expenditure 2023-24 is Rs. 1321.78 crore). Adding to the woes, in the FY 2024-25 also, 
the budgetary allocation of Rs. 1274.79 crore at BE stage was decreased to Rs. 579.12 
crore at RE stage.  The reduction in budgetary allocation under the Scheme become 
more alarming and attention seeking of budget planner particularly when the Scheme 
contains components of establishment of new medical colleges attached with 
district/referral hospitals, up-gradation of State Government Medical Colleges for 
increasing the MBBS seats and PG seats.  On the one hand, the Government aims at 
increasing patient-doctor ratio as per WHO norm for ensuring better health care and on 
the other hand, Government is significantly reducing the budgetary allocation.  The 
Government must adopt the prudent budgetary process. 
(Para 4.6.2) 
 
133. The Committee recommends that the Department of Health and Family Welfare 
implement a proactive, multi-pronged strategy to ensure consistent and efficient 
utilization of allocated funds for the Human Resource for Health & Medical Education 
schemes by enhancing the absorption capacity. Specifically, given the recurring issues of 
underutilization due to lack of state demand, delayed state share release, and Real-time 
System of Integrated Quick Transfers (SNA SPARSH) compliance delays, the 
Department should establish a dedicated task force to provide intensive, institutionalised 
support to each state and union territory. This task force will conduct pre-emptive 
assessments of state needs, offer hands-on assistance with SNA SPARSH compliance, 
and facilitate timely release of state matching funds through regular communication and 
problem-solving workshops. Furthermore, the Department should implement a 
quarterly review system that incorporates direct feedback from states, allowing for real-
time adjustments to funding allocations and program implementation. This approach 
will not only address the immediate issue of underutilization but also build stronger 
collaborative relationships with states, ensuring the long-term success of expanding 
medical education infrastructure and increasing the availability of medical 
professionals. 
(Para 4.6.6) 
 
PRADHAN MANTRI AYUSHMAN BHARAT HEALTH INFRASTRUCTURE 
MISSION (PM-ABHIM) 
 
134. The Committee expresses concern over the declining trend of fund utilisation 
under PM-ABHIM during the last two financial years 2023-24 and 2024-25 i.e. 42.99% 
and 62.74% respectively, of the BE allocation that casts aspersion on absorption 
capacity of Scheme and the professional proficiency of implementing agency of the 
Scheme. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends a more streamlined and front-
loaded disbursement process for the PM-ABHIM scheme as the scheme contain 
visionary aim and objective of Government to fill critical gaps in health infrastructure, 
surveillance and health research – spanning both the urban and rural areas so that the 
communities are Atmanirbhar in managing such pandemic/ health crises. The data 
clearly shows consistent underutilization of allocated funds, particularly in the initial 

204 
 
phases of each fiscal year, despite the capital intensive infrastructure project under the 
scheme. While the Department attributes this to the lead time of capital expenditure 
projects, the significant reductions from Budget Estimates to Revised Estimates indicate 
a predictable pattern of slow pace of expenditure. To address this, the Committee 
suggests that the Department implement a system where a larger portion of the annual 
budget is released during the first quarter, contingent on states submitting detailed 
project implementation plans and timelines. This would allow for early initiation of 
infrastructure projects, reducing the lag time and ensuring more consistent expenditure 
throughout the fiscal year. Moreover, the Department should focus on providing 
technical assistance and capacity building to states to expedite project approvals and 
implementation, thereby mitigating delays and ensuring the timely achievement of the 
scheme's objectives. 
(Para 4.7.4) 
 
135. The Committee notes the progress made under the PM-ABHIM scheme. 
Although there is advancement in work initiation and completion across various 
components [Secondary Health Care-Ayushman Arogya Mandir (SHC-AAM), U- 
Ayushman Arogya Mandir, Block Public Health Unit (BPHU), Integrated Public Health 
Laboratories (IPHL)], however, the Critical Care Blocks (CCBs) are significantly 
lagging the pace of execution, with only 3% work completion. The Department has 
taken steps to accelerate progress, including pre-poning activities and conducting 
regular reviews. However, land acquisition for CCBs remains a major bottleneck. The 
scheme's focus on strengthening health infrastructure across all levels is well taken, but 
the timelines for completion of project need closer monitoring. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department prioritize and implement a focused strategy 
to address the land acquisition and construction challenges facing the Critical Care 
Blocks (CCBs). This strategy should include (a) establishing a dedicated task force at the 
state and district levels to expedite land identification and allocation for CCBs; (b) 
providing technical assistance and streamlined approval processes to States/UTs to 
overcome bureaucratic hurdles in construction; (c) exploring alternative construction 
models, such as modular construction, to reduce lead times; and (d) implementing a 
strict monitoring system with milestone based review of each CCB project to ensure 
timely completion of the project. Given the critical role of CCBs in pandemic 
preparedness and critical care, immediate and decisive action is crucial to ensure the 
timely completion of the project. 
(Para 4.7.10) 
 
136. The Committee recommends the establishment of a robust, real-time monitoring 
system to track not only the physical construction progress but also the timely 
operationalization of completed health facilities, particularly U-AAMs. This system 
should include regular audits to verify the functionality and service delivery of 
operationalized units, mechanisms to ensure adequate staffing and resource allocation 
for newly completed facilities, review of impact on community health outcomes, and 
creation of a public dashboard that displays the current status of each project, to 
increase transparency and accountability. The department should create a system to 

205 
 
track the time taken between civil work completion and full operational status to ensure 
that completed projects are made operational with delay. By focusing on operational 
effectiveness, the Department can ensure that the investments made in infrastructure 
translate into tangible and timely improvements in healthcare access and quality. 
(Para 4.7.11) 
 
AYUSHMAN BHARAT-PRADHAN MANTRI JAN AROGYA YOJANA (AB-PMJAY) 
 
137. The Committee is of the opinion that the Ayushman Bharat-Pradhan Mantri Jan 
Arogya Yojana (AB-PMJAY) is a pivotal and visionary health initiative aiming to 
provide financial protection and healthcare access to a large number of India's 
vulnerable population. While the scheme has seen substantial growth in terms of state 
participation, card creation, and hospital admissions, several key areas require attention 
like Budget Utilization and State Participation. The Committee observes a persistent 
trend of underutilization of allocated funds, particularly in the previous year as well as 
in the current financial year. The Committee observes that, in the FY 2023-24, an 
amount of Rs. 7200.00 crore was allocated as BE 2023-24 which was reduced to Rs. 
6800.00 crore at RE stage, however, the actual expenditure was Rs. 6670.47 crore.  In 
the FY 2024-25, Rs. 7300.00 crores was made in BE 2024-25 which was revised to Rs. 
7605.54 crores at RE 2024-25, however, the actual expenditure was to the tune of Rs. 
5034.03 Crores till 09.01.2025. In FY 2025-26, the enhanced amount of Rs. 9406 crore 
has been allocated as BE 2025-26.  This indicates potential inefficiencies in fund 
disbursement or implementation bottlenecks. The Committee, therefore, recommends a 
thorough review of the fund release mechanisms to states/UTs. This review should focus 
on identifying and eliminating bottlenecks that contribute to underutilization. 
Specifically, the Department should implement a system of proactive monitoring of fund 
utilization by states, coupled with targeted support to address any implementation 
challenges. This support could include technical assistance, capacity building, and 
simplified reporting procedures. Furthermore, the committee suggests that the release of 
funds be linked to the performance of states in terms of card creation, hospital 
admissions, and beneficiary feedback, ensuring that funds are directed to areas of 
greatest need and impact. The Department should also create and publish a clear and 
concise guideline for states, explaining the process of fund requests, and the expected 
timelines. To ensure that the allocation of fund to the tune of Rs. 9406.00 crore in BE 
2025-26 is utilized, a quarterly review system may be set up to avoid any delays in 
release of fund to States/UTs. 
(Para 4.8.7) 
 
138. The Committee is of the view that besides the large number of empanelled 
hospitals and issuance of cards, the crucial factor for successful implementation of the 
Yojana in public awareness about the Yojana is public awareness about the Yojana. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends a focused campaign, high-level engagement with the 
respective state governments, addressing their specific concerns and highlighting the 
benefits of the scheme for their populations. The Department should intensify its efforts 
to enhance beneficiary awareness, particularly in rural and underserved areas. This can 

206 
 
be achieved through targeted Information, Education, and Communication (IEC) 
campaigns, utilizing local languages and community-based approaches. Leveraging 
ASHA and Anganwadi workers, as well as digital platforms, to disseminate information 
and facilitate card creation is crucial. The Committee also recommends that the 
Department conduct regular beneficiary feedback surveys to assess the effectiveness of 
awareness campaigns and identify areas for improvement. A dedicated helpline and 
easily accessible grievance redressal mechanism should also be established to address 
beneficiary concerns promptly. Further, public displays of information regarding 
hospital empanelment and available procedures should be made mandatory at all public 
health centers. 
(Para 4.8.8) 
 
139. The Committee appreciates that the Government has recently expanded the AB-
PMJAY to cover 6 crore senior citizens of age 70 years and above belonging to 4.5 crore 
families under AB-PMJAY Vay Vandana scheme, irrespective of their socio economic 
status. The Committee observes that an amount to the tune of Rs. 1443.00 crore has 
been earmarked for treatment of senior citizens of the age of 70 years and above holding 
Ayushman Vay Vandna Cards.  The Committee is of the view that age criteria of 70 
years and above for Ayushman Vay Vandna Cards should be rationalized to 60 years 
and above irrespective of their socio-economic status for widening the coverage of the 
scheme in the better interest of commn masses. 
(Para 4.8.9) 
 
140. The Committee also recommends the Department to launch public awareness 
campaign about AB-PMJAY so that intended beneficiaries of the Yojana are not left out 
from coverage. 
(Para 4.8.10) 
 
141. The Committee, keeping in view huge expenditure on the critical healthcare, 
should revise healthcare coverage from existing Rs. 5 lakh per family per year to Rs. 10 
lakh per family per year.  
(Para 4.8.11) 
 
142. The Committee observes that several high-end interventions/procedures, and 
even high-end diagnostics are not included in AB-PMJAY. The Committee recommends 
that the number of packages/procedures covered under the Yojana should be reviewed 
and the fresh packages/procedures related to treatment of critical illness with high cost 
of treatment and expensive high-end investigations/diagnostics like radiological 
diagnostics (CT, MRI, imaging including Nuclear Imaging) be incorporated in AB-
PMJAY instead of leaving the same to be booked as Add-on packages.  
(Para 4.8.12) 
 
143. The Committee has come across the instances of delay in settlement of claims of 
empanelled hospitals under AB-PMJAY and that the package rates have not been 
reasonably revised in many States resulting into inconvenience caused to the patients 

207 
 
due to denial of treatment by many empanelled hospitals. The Committee, therefore, 
considers that the States/UTs which have not revised the package rates may be 
persuaded by the Department to revise the package rates on the similar lines of rates 
revised by some of the States in 2022. In addition to the above, the Committee 
recommends that the Department review and streamline the process related to 
settlement of claims submitted by the empanelled hospitals within the specified time.  
(Para 4.8.13) 
 
144. The Committee has been given to understand that the operational period of 
National Health Mission (NHM) is from FY 2021 to FY 2026, therefore, coming to an 
end on 31.03.2026. The Committee, considering the pivotal significance of the Mission, is 
of the considered view that the operational period of NHM must be extended beyond 31st 
March 2026 and cover at least the duration of 16th Finance Commission to enable the 
Department to achieve the remaining goals and objectives of health indicators under the 
Mission.  
(Para 4.8.14) 
 
SUBMISSION BY THE DEPARTMENT TO THE 16TH FINANCE COMMISSION 
 
145. The Committee has been apprised that the Department has presented before the 
16th Finance Commission the status/progress made in the health sector, focusing on 
infrastructure improvements. In the discussion with the Commission, the focus was on 
enhancement of diagnostic capabilities, up-gradation of infrastructure to meet Indian 
Public Health Standards (IPHS) 2022, and addressing the key infrastructure gaps in 
healthcare facilities to align with National Quality Assurance Standards (NQAS). The 
Committee recommends the Department to conduct a comprehensive, year-wise 
projection of health sector demand for the 16th Finance Commission. This projection 
should especially quantify the financial resources required to achieve the goals of 
enhancing diagnostic infrastructure, meeting IPHS 2022, and aligning with NQAS which 
would facilitate more effective resource allocation and ensure sustained progress in the 
health sector. 
(Para 5.1.2) 
 
146. The Committee observes that the primary issue is the significant shortfall in 
funding for the National Centre for Ageing (NCA) at Banaras Hindu University (BHU). 
While a demand of Rs 95.38 crore was made for the 2025-26, only Rs 50 crore has been 
allocated. Given that the NCA is under construction with a total cost of Rs 150 crore, 
and Rs 47.20 crore has already been sanctioned, the remaining funds are crucial for 
timely completion. To ensure continuity and prevent project delays, the Committee 
recommends that the Ministry prioritize the release of the remaining Rs 35 crore as 
initially planned. Furthermore, the Committee explicitly advocates for a proactive 
monitoring mechanism to assess the project's progress during the first half of 2025-26. 
This will allow for the timely identification of any additional funding needs. This 
approach ensures the NCA's timely completion, benefiting elderly citizens. 
(Para 5.2.2) 

208 
 
 
EMISSION GAP REPORT 2020 UN ENVIRONMENT PROGRAMME 
 
147. Keeping in view of the observation of Emission Gap Report 2020 UN 
Environment Programme that India has seen over 6% more Green House Gas Emission 
than previous year, the Committee feels that it is high time for India to establish an 
Environmental Health Regulatory Agency for Comprehensive and Cohesive 
Environmental Governance to control detrimental health effects of exposure to air, 
water and soil pollutants to prevent wide range of non-communicable diseases. The 
Committee appreciates that the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare (MoHFW) has 
been working closely with other ministries, such as the Ministry of Environment, Forest 
and Climate Change (MoEFCC) and NITI Aayog, to develop multi-sectoral strategies 
for mitigating environmental health risks and National Programme on Climate Change 
and Human Health (NPCCHH) has developed an organizational structure at all levels-
National, State and District level. The Committee recommends that the Department 
significantly enhance the regulatory authority and scope of the State Level Environment 
Health Cells within the NPCCHH framework, especially, these cells should be 
empowered with clear mandates for enforcement and monitoring of environmental 
health standards, including air, water, and soil pollution, and be given the resources to 
conduct independent assessments and issue binding directives. This expansion would 
leverage the established structure of the NPCCHH, particularly the state-level Multi-
Sectoral Task Forces, to ensure a more robust and proactive approach to mitigating the 
detrimental health effects of environmental pollution, thereby strengthening the existing 
framework ensuring continuity and efficient utilization of existing resources while 
addressing the urgent need for stronger environmental health governance. 
(Para 5.3.3.) 
 
REGULAR NURSING CADRE 
 
148. The Committee recommends a significant increase in the earmarked funds for 
nursing training programs, coupled with the establishment of a dedicated, centrally 
managed nursing cadre. There is need for advanced medical technology and emergency 
care training to the nurses. Therefore, the Committee advocates for a substantial rise in 
funding for expansion of training programs to include specialized modules on cutting-
edge medical technologies and emergency response protocols. Furthermore, to ensure 
continuity and attract and retain skilled nurses, a regular nursing cadre should be 
created, offering standardized pay scales, benefits, and career progression opportunities 
across both public and private healthcare sectors, building on the existing Supreme 
Court expert committee recommendations. This would not only improve working 
conditions and incentives but also establish a stable and skilled nursing workforce 
capable of handling complex medical challenges. 
(Para 5.4.2) 
 
OPPORTUNITY FOR GLOBAL HEALTH LEADERSHIP 
 

209 
 
149. Given the fact of US withdrawal from the WHO and India's existing strong 
engagement and digital health leadership, the Committee recommends a focused 
strategy to solidify India's global health leadership by leveraging its digital health 
expertise and educational capacity. The Committee recommends that India should 
proactively establish a dedicated "Global Digital Health Partnership Initiative" under 
the WHO framework that would certainly streamline the offering of India's Digital 
Public Infrastructure (DPI), like Ayushman Bharat Digital Health Mission and Co-Win, 
to developing nations through Global Initiative on Digital Health (GIDH), coupled with 
robust capacity-building programs and educational exchanges. By actively promoting its 
successful digital health models and expanding its medical education offerings, India can 
not only fill the leadership vacuum but also foster a more robust domestic biomedical 
ecosystem and become a premier healthcare training hub for Asian-African countries, 
thus maintaining continuity with its existing contributions and maximizing the 
opportunity presented by the changing global health landscape. 
(Par 5.5.2) 
REVIEW OF NATIONAL HEALTH POLICY 2017 
 
150. The Committee notes that almost 8 years have been passed since the formulation 
of the National Health Policy 2017. The Committee, therefore, recommends establishing 
a formal, periodic review mechanism for the policy's implementation and impact 
assessment. This mechanism should mandate consistent, transparent engagement and 
feedback collection from various stakeholders and the public. The review of the policy 
and publishing the findings can ensure that the policy remains responsive to evolving 
healthcare needs and maintains the initial broad consensus achieved through its 
formulation. This will guarantee continuity between the policy's foundational principles 
and its ongoing adaptation of global practices in the field of healthcare. 
 
(Para 5.6.2) 
HEALTH AND EDUCATION CESS 
 
151. The Committee notes that 25% part of the Health and Education Cess collected 
is allocated to the Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Nidhi (PMSSN). This PMSSN 
funding is then designated for utilisation in specific centrally sponsored health schemes 
viz. a) Flexible Pool for RCH & Health System Strengthening, National Health 
Programme and National Urban Health Mission (component of NHM) b) Infrastructure 
Maintenance (Component of NHM) c) Human Resource for Health and Medical 
Education (HRH&ME). The Committee urges upon the Government to increase the 
share of PMSSN in the corpus accumulated through collection of Health and Education 
Cess. 
(Para 5.7.2) 
ISSUANCE OF ABHA CARDS 
 
152. The Committee notes the expansion of coverage from different segments of 
population under AB-PMJAY scheme from time to time, however, the Committee 
recommends the Department for the immediate and consistent integration of updated 

210 
 
demographic data into the ABHA card issuance and PMJAY beneficiary identification 
processes. Specifically, while the PMJAY scheme has made efforts to expand its 
coverage beyond the 2011 census, the fundamental reliance on outdated data for initial 
identification and ABHA card linkage creates potential exclusion and inaccuracy. This 
continuous updating will ensure that the schemes remain relevant and equitable, 
reflecting the true health needs of the population. 
(Para 5.8.2) 
AYUSHMAN BHARAT- TRIBAL AREAS AND MIGRANT POPULATION 
 
153. The Committee is of the view that initiatives like "Aapke Dwar Ayushman" 
campaign should not be confined to creation of Ayushman Cards as highlighting vast 
number of creation of card is not the only objective of the scheme. Rather the 
Department should make efforts to ensure establishing robust healthcare infrastructure 
and service delivery in tribal and rural areas. While the portability feature and card 
creation drives are valuable, the core issue remains the lack of essential medical facilities 
like blood storage in PHCs, and availability of specialists like gynaecologists, 
particularly in regions with migrating populations. Therefore, the Committee urges 
upon the co-ordination between different components of the Ayushman Bharat scheme. 
The National Health Authority (NHA) should integrate these critical healthcare 
components with the "Aapke Dwar Ayushman" framework. This would entail 
deploying mobile medical units equipped with basic ICU facilities, blood storage 
capabilities, and specialist services like gynaecology to remote areas during the 
campaign. Additionally, the campaign should facilitate the establishment of sustainable 
blood collection and storage systems at PHCs and prioritize the recruitment and 
deployment of specialists like gynaecologists to these underserved regions, to bridge the 
gap between awareness and accessible, comprehensive healthcare delivery. 
(Para 5.9.2) 
GIG WORKERS UNDER PMJAY SCHEME 
 
154. The Committee, to ensure the effective inclusion of 10 million gig workers under 
the PMJAY scheme, recommends the Department for immediate establishment of a 
dedicated, automated, and real-time data synchronization system between the E-Shram 
portal and the AB-PMJAY database. This system should be designed to facilitate 
seamless, continuous, and error-free data transfer, enabling instant verification and de-
duplication exercise to identify and eliminate any overlap. This focused approach to 
data integration, alongside the planned awareness campaigns and platform 
collaborations, will minimize enrollment delays and ensure that all eligible gig workers 
receive timely access to essential healthcare benefits, while preventing double counting 
of beneficiaries. 
(Para 5.10.2) 
 
COVERAGE OF OPD SERVICES AND COST OF MEDICINE UNDER THE 
AYUSHMAN BHARAT 
 

211 
 
155. The Committee recommends the phased inclusion of essential OPD services and a 
broader range of post-hospitalization medication coverage within the Ayushman Bharat 
PMJAY scheme. While acknowledging the scheme's focus on secondary and tertiary 
inpatient care, the current exclusion of routine OPD services and limited post-
hospitalization medication coverage creates a significant gap in accessible healthcare. To 
ensure continuity and build upon the existing framework, the recommendation should 
propose a phased approach, starting with the inclusion of essential OPD services for 
chronic diseases and commonly reported ailments. Furthermore, extending post-
hospitalization medication coverage beyond the current 15-day limit, particularly for 
conditions requiring prolonged treatment, would significantly reduce the financial 
burden on beneficiaries while addressing a need for comprehensive healthcare access. 
(Para 5.11.2) 
MISUSE OF FUND UNDER AB-PMJAY SCHEME 
 
156. The Committee recommends the National Health Authority (NHA) implement a 
proactive, data-driven, and transparent monitoring system focusing on early detection 
of potential misuse patterns in AB-PMJAY. This system should go beyond reacting to 
reported fraud by continuously analyzing claim data, hospital activity, and patient 
feedback to identify statistical anomalies and emerging trends indicating potential 
abuse. Specifically, the NHA should develop and publicly report on key performance 
indicators related to fraud detection, including the frequency of pre-authorization 
requests for specific procedures, the ratio of medical camp referrals to hospital 
admissions, and the rates of hospital de-empanelment and penalties by state. In the 
collection and analysis of statistical data and feedback, the extensive use of AI and 
digital tools should be considered upon by the Department. This transparent, data-
driven approach will not only allow for quicker intervention but also serve as a 
deterrent, ensuring the scheme's resources are used for their intended purpose and 
maintaining public trust. 
(Para 5.12.4) 
PRADHAN MANTRI NATIONAL DIALYSIS PROGRAMME (PMNDP) 
 
157. The Committee is of the view that the Dialysis facility under PMNDP should be 
given to the needy persons. The facility is being availed by persons who can afford 
treatment from their own pocket in private hospitals. The scheme provisions need to be 
reviewed. The Committee finds that the dialysis centres are at the District level at 
present, however, the Committee desires the Department should make strategy for 
setting up of  Dialysis facility at the Taluka level also as kidney patients are increasing. 
(Para 5.13.3) 
CANCER DAY CARE CENTERS 
 
158. The Committee has come across that a provision of 4-6 beds are proposed in Day 
Care Centres for cancer Chemotherapy and Chemotherapy and medicines are proposed 
to be provided to the patients in Day Care Centres. The Committee recommends that 
the said proposals gets actualised on the ground level, sooner the better. The Committee 
also desires that separate human Resources (including health professional and nurse) 

212 
 
may be provided instead of seeking from the pool of District Hospital for smooth 
functioning of the centre since there are already scarcity of health professionals in the 
District Hospitals where such professionals must made available. 
(Para 5.14.3) 
HUB & SPOKE MODEL FOR CARDIAC AND STROKE 
 
159. The Committee endorses the views of Department for strengthening 
infrastructure, human resource development, health promotion & awareness generation 
for prevention, early diagnosis, management and referral to an appropriate level of 
healthcare facility for treatment of the Non-Communicable Diseases. The Committee 
desires that for effective management of patients with ST Segment Elevated Myocardial 
Infarction (STEMI) and Stroke, Hub and Spoke model may be implemented. The 
Department may persuade the States have to identify the Tertiary care hospitals as 
‘Hub’ based on infrastructure, experts and laboratory and intervention services and 
Spokes for early interventions. 
(Para 5.15.3) 
 
160. The Committee understands that NCD screening are integral component of 
NPNCD programme, therefore, the population aged 30 years and above must be 
undertaken for screening of NCDs like Diabetes, Hypertension, Oral cancer, Breast 
cancer and cervical cancers under National campaign launched from 20th Feb 2025. 
(Para 5.15.4) 
TRAUMA AND EMERGENCY CARE SERVICES 
 
161. The Committee understands that NPPMT&BI scheme aims at reducing the 
mortality and morbidity occurring due to injuries (including trauma and burns), by 
developing a system of services at pre- hospital & hospital level and continuum of care 
through rehabilitation. The Committee desires that adequate funds may be allocated for 
Trauma and Emergency Care Services to achieve the significant goals of the scheme that 
includes creating center of excellence from trauma and Burn Care in existing 
Government Medical Colleges. 
(Para 5.16.3) 
HIGH MALARIA CASES IN KOKRAJHAR, ASSAM 
 
162. The Committee expresses concern that all 4 blocks of Kokrajhar districts have 
reportedly witnessed an increase in cases in 2024, with the majority of cases from the 
northern part of the district. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that the 
Department should persuade the State Government to undertake concrete steps for 
combating the menace of malaria in Kokrajhar district, at the earliest. The Committee 
further recommends that the Department must ensure strengthening of treatment 
follow-up and capacity building mechanism of healthcare professionals to eliminate 
malaria from the country. 
(Para 5.17.3) 
 

213 
 
PUBLIC PRIVATE PARTNERSHIP (PPP) MODEL FOR PROMOTING MEDICAL 
TOURISM 
 
163. The Committee understands that the promotion of medical tourism requires 
multi-disciplinary and multi-pronged approach, thereby seeks the involvement of 
Ministry of Tourism and the Ministry of External Affairs but no one can exclude the 
pivotal role of the Department of Health and Family Welfare because the subject health 
where upon the focus is to be made for promoting medical tourism. The Committee 
perceives that the rate of medical tourism growth is much higher than the normal 
tourism growth, therefore the role of the Department of Health and Family Welfare is of 
pivotal importance. The Committee observes that growth of medical tourism in private 
sector is better than the public sector, therefore, there is enormous scope for promoting 
medical tourism in public sector. The Committee further recommends that the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare, in tandem with the Ministry of Tourism and 
the Ministry of External Affairs, leave no stone unturned in emphasising Public Private 
Partnership (PPP) model for promoting medical tourism. 
(Para 5.18.2) 
 
ACCESSSIBLE AND AFFORDABLE TREATMENT OF RARE DISEASES 
 
164. The Committee notes that 63 rare diseases are covered under National Policy for 
Rare Diseases (NPRD) which of course needs to be reviewed and expanded from time to 
time. In order to make the treatment of rare diseases accessible and affordable, the 
Department may make provision for cheaper diagnosis, prevention and treatment. The 
Committee also desires that the Department should launch national 
campaign/awareness programme for seeking treatment of the rare diseases. 
(Para 5.19.3) 
 
OTHER RECOMMENDATIONS OF THE COMMITTEE 
 
165. The sanctioning of Medical colleges should be on the basis of population and not 
district-wise as this creates imbalance in Hospital to population ratio. Moreover, the 
quality of Medicine and Nursing education should be as per National Quality Assurance 
Standards (NQAS) and the global best practices;  
(Para 5.20 (i)) 
 
166.  Private medical education should be made cheaper and the Capitation fees needs 
to be regulated to make the medical education more affordable and accessible to merit 
candidates with poor financial background; 
(Para 5.20 (ii)) 
 
167.  Expediting the process of consideration of HPV vaccine roll-out plan and 
bringing the same under the Universal Immunisation Programme (UIP); 
                                                                                                                 (Para 5.20 (iii)) 
 

214 
 
168. Making availability of Doctors at PHC/CHC level and lucrative pay and perks 
for specialist doctors ensuring their retention in the Government Hospitals. Department 
may persuade the States/UTs to overcome the shortage of doctors at PHCs/CHCs 
through various measures including the creation of separate Medical Services 
Recruitment Boards, specialist cadres, and providing flexibilities under NHM for 
recruitment and retention; 
                                                                                                                 (Para 5.20 (iv)) 
 
169. Provision of sufficient staff, essential diagnostics and quality generic medicines 
must be made available in Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (AAMs); 
                                                                                                                       (Para 5.20 (v))                     
 
170. Strengthening healthcare facilities upto District Hospital level so that patient 
need not be referred to tertiary hospitals;  
                                                                                                               (Para 5.20 (vi)) 
 
171. Strengthening infrastructure including Advanced Life Support (ALS) 
ambulances to handle cardiac cases and making available essential medicines and para-
medical staff at rural health centres as per the norms of the Indian Public Health 
Standards (IPHS) 2022 for strengthening/improving rural health infrastructure. Efforts 
should be made that all Health Schemes should be implemented at ground level 
especially in rural, semi-urban, tribal and hilly areas; and 
                                                                                                                (Para 5.20 (vii)) 
 
172.   Keeping in view the pivotal role of ASHA workers in the effective 
implementation of the various schemes of the Government on the ground level but being 
paid ranging from Rs. 5000- 9000 per month which is, of course, not sufficient for 
earning bread of two times for a single person, leave apart maintaining good health and 
basic needs, there is urgent need to enhance the honorarium of ASHA workers. 
                                                                                                               (Para 5.20 (viii)) 
 
173.   Appreciating the decision of establishment of the Day Care Cancer Centres in 
all districts, particularly to provide the services to rural areas, in next three years, the 
Committee apprehends about the practical implementation of the provision on the 
ground level provided adequate budgetary allocation is made for the purpose. Although 
the Committee is uncertain about the Corporate Hospitals coming forward to join the 
programme, however, in case some Trust Hospitals come forward to participate in the 
programme, the Department should welcome the move and extend assistance & 
facilitate all kind of collaboration, co-operation and co-ordination network; 
                                                                                                                 (Para 5.20 (ix)) 
 
174.   Keeping in view that the Department has formulated National Sickle Cell 
Anaemia Elimination Mission setting goal for screening of 7 crore population in the 
country. The Committee desires to know the number of population covered so far 
towards this goal and the number of persons given sickle cell genetic status card, along 
with the strategy chalked out to achieve the goal during 2026. The Committee wishes to 

215 
 
highlight the incidence of sickle cell anaemia in tribal areas of the country, including 
Goa, where the Committee had recently visited and came across the prevalence of sickle 
cell anaemia in 1.5 lakh tribals, but without any certain provision for elimination of the 
disease. The Committee has further come across that even if ‘PSA’ Test Kit is available 
at the health centre, it is not opened till sufficient number of patients visit for full 
utilisation of the kit. The Committee is of the view that the health centre may be directed 
to open the ‘PSA’ Kit even if single patient visits the centre;  
                                                                                                                  (Para 5.20 (x)) 
175.  Simplification of procedures/norms, rules and regulations for establishing 
nursing, occupational therapy & physiotherapy and dental colleges for ensuring their 
availability at the CHC and District Hospital level.  Arrangements be made for study of 
occupational therapy & physiotherapy in Government Hospital Colleges for producing 
sufficient number of occupational therapists and physiotherapists ensuring their 
services at the Block Level; and  
                                                                                                                 (Para 5.20 (xi)) 
 
176. Continuous Glucose Monitors (CGMs) and diabetic medicines are essential for 
effective diabetes management which is a chronic disease condition. However, the high 
cost of CGMs, their limited lifespan and the lack of long-term insurance coverage for 
these devices and medicines create significant challenges for patients. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends increasing the lifespan of CGM sensors, implementing price 
controls and price caps on CGM sensors, cardiac stents, etc., mandating insurance 
coverage for CGMs and diabetic medicines on a long term basis to ensure affordability 
and accessibility. The Committee further desires that ‘Make in India’ initiative should 
be promoted to reduce prices and achieve self-sufficiency in CGM production. 
(Para 5.20(xii)
